# Władysław Rusiński, *Osady tzw. „olędrów" w dawnym województwie poznańskim* — English translation

> **Translator's note.** This is a translation of the monograph
> (Poznań 1939, Kraków 1947). Conventions, agreed with the editor:
> British spelling; the ethnic term **Olęder(s)** (Pol. *olędrzy*,
> German *Hauländer*) is retained and glossed at first use; archival
> sigla (AW., Rel. Posn., Tp. 89 …), archive call marks and
> bibliographic titles are kept in their original form; numbers
> follow the print exactly, with Polish thousand/decimal separators
> converted to the English convention (13.737 → 13,737; 6,05 → 6.05)
> and no value altered — including the print's own internal
> discrepancies; measures are glossed at first use; quotations from
> archival Polish are translated, the original wording being given
> only where the author discusses it; quotations in German keep the
> original with a translation; the tables of ANEKS I are not
> translated in this edition. The bracketed `[p. N]` markers are the
> print's own pagination.
>
> **Terms used.** *Olęder(s)* — one of the Dutch-origin farming
> colonists and their settlements (Pol. *olędrzy/olędry*, German
> *Hauländer*); "Ol. X" = an Olęder settlement named X. *huba*
> (pl. *hubas*) — a full hide, the basic holding unit (cf. German
> *Hufe*). *włóka* (pl. *włókas*) — the field measure (in this book
> the 30-morg włóka). *morg* (pl. *morgs*) — a land measure. *dym* —
> hearth, the fiscal unit (the hearth tax = *podymne*). *tymf* — the
> debased Polish coin of the late 17th and early 18th century.
> *folwark* — manor farm. *sołtys* — village headman. *ławnik* —
> lay assessor (juror). *wolnizna* — a rent-free period granted at
> settlement. *wilkierz* — a village by-law. *oblaty* — enrolments
> of documents in the court books. *wieś zaciężna* — a contract
> village settled under Polish law. *chałupnik* — a cottage dweller
> without land. *komornik* — a lodger. *zagrodnik* — a smallholder
> with a garden plot. *starostwo* — a royal domain held by a
> starosta. *królewszczyzny* — royal estates. *majętność* — a
> private estate complex. *serwituty* — servitudes (rights of use).
> *szarwark* — corvée labour. *robocizna* — labour due. *przymus
> mlewa* — the milling monopoly. *propinacja* — the propination
> (liquor) monopoly. *pogłówne* — poll tax. *hyberna* — the winter
> levy. *korzec/garniec* — dry measures (pl. *korcy/garnce*).

[p. I]

POLISH ACADEMY OF LEARNING

WORKS OF THE COMMISSION OF THE HISTORICAL ATLAS OF POLAND, VOL. V

THE SETTLEMENTS OF THE SO-CALLED "OLĘDRY"
IN THE FORMER VOIVODESHIP OF POZNAŃ

[*Osady tzw. „olędrów" w dawnym woj. poznańskim*]

BY
WŁADYSŁAW RUSIŃSKI

WITH A MAP

(PUBLISHED WITH A GRANT FROM THE SCIENCE DEPARTMENT OF THE MINISTRY OF EDUCATION)

POZNAŃ 1939 — KRAKÓW 1947

PUBLISHED BY THE POLISH ACADEMY OF LEARNING
MAIN DISTRIBUTION: THE GEBETHNER AND WOLFF BOOKSHOP
WARSAW — KRAKÓW — ŁÓDŹ — POZNAŃ — ZAKOPANE

[the printer's imprint, on the verso of the title page:]

The Jagiellonian University Press in Kraków, under the management
of Karol Kieć. M – 14707

LIST OF ABBREVIATIONS

The abbreviations used in the study are set in italics in the print.

A. Sources

(Sources not further specified were located, or are still located,
before the outbreak of the war in the State Archives in Poznań).

AC. 1739–40 — Acta Causarum of 1739–40, the Archdiocesan Archives
in Poznań.

AGH — land and mortgage records (Akta gruntowe i hipoteczne).

AM — town records (Akta miejskie).

AW — village records (Akta wiejskie).

Dom. — Prussian records concerning the state domains, classed under
the call mark: Domeny.

G — Johann Friedrich Goldbeck, Vollständige Topographie des
Königreichs Preussen. Zweiter Theil, welcher die Topographie von
Westpreussen enthält. Marienwerder 1789.

GE — Allgemeine Geographische Ephemeriden. Verfasset von einer
Gesellschaft Gelehrten und herausgegeben von A. C. Gaspari u.
F. J. Bertuch. Achter Band. Weimar 1801.

Decr. Posn. — the court books of the Poznań grod (castle court).

Inser. Valc. — the grod books of Wałcz (Inscriptiones Valcenses).

Kat. I, II — Kataster der der Westpreussischen Kontribution
unterliegenden Ortschaften im Regierungs-Bezirk Bromberg, vols
I–II, call mark: WPZ. Kam. Dep. Bydgoszcz. XII. A. 1.

Kl. — the Prussian classification records (Klassifikationsanschläge)
of the years 1773–4, call marks: WPZ. Kam. Dep. Bydgoszcz,
XII. 6 I–V, XII. 7, XII. 13.

Kl. M. — the Prussian classification records of 1795 concerning the
estates of the Order of the Knights of Malta, call mark:
SPZ. C. I. 14.

Kośc. — records concerning religious affairs in the Prussian
partition after the First Partition, call mark: WPZ. Kam. Dep.
Bydgoszcz II. B.

Landr. — records of the former Prussian landrats' offices
(Landratury), classed under the call mark: Landratury.

Lustr. pruska 1664 — the lustration of the royal domains of Royal
Prussia, published as vol. 32 of the Fontes of the Toruń Scientific
Society under the title: Opis królewszczyzn w województwach
chełmińskim, pomorskim i malborskim w r. 1664 (A description of the
royal domains in the chełmno, pomorskie and malbork voivodeships).
Toruń 1938.

MG — Gilly, Special Karte von Südpreussen (a reduction at the scale
of 1:115,200). Berlin 1802 and 1803.

MGd — the Map of South Prussia of the years 1796–1805 at the scale
of 1:57,600 (the large Gilly).

MP — Mappa szczegulna Woiewództwa Poznańskiego zrządzona... przez
Karola de Perthees... 1791 (A particular map of the Poznań
voivodeship, made... by Karol de Perthees).

[p. VI]

M. Schr. — Karte von Ost-Preussen nebst Preussisch Litthauen und
West-Preussen nebst dem Netzdistrict, aufgenommen unter Leitung
des... Frey Herrn von Schroetter in den Jahren von 1796 bis 1802.

M 1821 — a general map and 17 detailed maps of the Poznań
department. Each map bears the inscription: "Special-Karte von
dem... Kreise, entworfen u. gezeichnet von E. Peschel... auf Stein
grav. v. Anton Freundreich in Posen 1821".

O 83 — the parish descriptions (opisy parafii) of the years 1783–4,
vols XVIII–XXII. The property of the National Library in Kraków.

OWK 89 — "Ofiara na Woysko Powiatu koscianskiego" 1789 (The
contribution for the army of the Kościan county). Call mark:
Ms. C. 17.

Q — Quellen und Urkunden zur Geschichte des Netzekreises, edited by
Professor Carl Schulz. Schönlanke u. Kreuz 1934, part I, a special
edition for the members of the Grenzmarkische Gesellschaft zur
Erforschung und Pflege der Heimat.

Rel. Cost. — the grod books of Kościan (Relationes Costenses).

Rel. Posn. — the grod books of Poznań (Relationes Posnanienses).

Rel. Valc. — the grod books of Wałcz (Relationes Valcenses).

Rel. Vsch. — the grod books of Wschowa (Relationes Vschovienses).

St. T. — Special Kreis Tableau aller in Kön. Südpr. Posenschen
Kammer Dep. vorhandenen Ortschaften u. Feuerstellen... 1793. The
State Central Archives in Warsaw, SP section.

Szk. — records concerning schooling in the Prussian partition after
the First Partition. Call mark: WPZ. Kam. Dep. Bydgoszcz III. B.

T 1769 — Tarifa lanealis Posnaniensis Calissiensis Palatinatum in
Majori Polonia Reperibilium, ex Tarilis Generosorum Hectoris
Koszutski Posnanienis Anne 1619, Petri Gniewosz Calissiensis Anno
1620... reducta... consensu ac mandato Antonii, Jacobi...
Szołdrskich... conscripta, sumptuque illorum... impressa anno...
1766. Posnaniae, Typis Academicis.

Tb. — Topographische Beschreibung von... Vorwerken und Dörfern
etc., 1797/8. The State Central Archives in Warsaw, SP section.

TKS. 72 — Wypis wsiow y miast przez wojewodztwa w całey Koronie
znaydujących się tudzież y zakordonowanych (An extract of the
villages and towns situated in the voivodeships of the whole Crown,
and likewise brought under the border cordon). (On the title page
another hand has added: "written out from the Tariffa of the Crown
Treasury Commission of the year 1772".) The property of the
Ossoliński National Institute in Lwów. Call mark: Manuscr. II. 494.

Tod. 89 — Tabella Ogólna Dymów Powiatu Kościańskiego przez Komissyą
Powiatową Sporządzona Roku 1789 (The general table of the hearths of
the Kościan county, drawn up by the county commission). Call mark:
M.S. C. 10.

Tołł. — Tabella Ogólna Ludności Powiatu Kościańskiego z tabel
szczególnych przez Kommissyę Powiatową sporządzona 1789 (The general
table of the population of the Kościan county, drawn up by the
county commission from the particular tables). The State Central
Archives in Warsaw, call mark: 163, no. 5. A.

Tp. 75 — Taryfla dymów i podymnego powiatów poznańskiego,
kościańskiego, oraz gnieźnieńskiego i kcyńskiego, rok 1775 (The
tariff of the hearths and of the poll tax of the counties of
Poznań, Kościan, Gniezno and Kcynia). The Treasury
Archives in Warsaw, call mark: LI no. 111, supplemented by an
extract kept in the State Archives in Poznań, call mark: Ms. C. 13.

Tp. 89, 90 — Regestr podymnego powiatów Poznańskiego, Kościańskiego
i Ziemi Wschowskiej ułożony podług Taryffy Roku 1789 jako też
Taryfly łustracji z r. 1790 na rok 1791 ułożony (The register of the
poll tax of the counties of Poznań and Kościan and of the Wschowa
land, drawn up according to the tariff, and likewise according to
the tariff of the lustration). The Treasury Archives in Warsaw:
call marks LI. 508, 113.

[p. VII]

Tp. — Topographisches Verzeichniss aller in dem Kön.
Südpreussischen Posenschen Cammer-Departement gehöriger Städte u.
Dörfer aufgenommen 1793 nach geschehener Arrondirung der Kreise u.
ausgefertigt den Kreis Tableau im März 1794. The State Central
Archives in Warsaw, SP section.

TW — a copy of the tables: 1. the contributions for the army,
2. the hearths, 3. the population of the Wschowa land of the year
1789. Call mark: Mscr. G. 23.

Verz/1800 — Verzeichniss sämmtlicher Ortschaften im
Südpreussischen Kammer-Departement Posen 1820. Call mark: Generalia A. VII. 12.

Verz/1845 — Verzeichniss sämmtlicher Ortschaften des
Regierungs-Bezirks Posen. Poznań 1845.

Wol. Leg. — Volumina Legum.

VO 78, 83, 84, 87 — Visitatio generalis in archi-diaconatu
Srzemensis decanatuum Srzemensis, Borcensis, Neomestensis et
Costensis... 1778. Call mark: A. V. no. 2. — Visitatio Generalia
Decanatuum Crobensis, Smiglensis, Wschovensis et Cosminensis...
Anno 1777-mo inchoata... 178 expedita. Call mark: A. V. no. 25. —
Visitatio generalis in Archi-Diaconatu Posnaniensi Decanatuum
Kostrzynensis, Srodensis, Pyzdrensis, Rogoznensis, Obornicensis et
Bucoviensis... in... Anno 1777-mo inchoata... 1784-to expedita.
Call mark: A. V. no. 26. — Visitatio Generalis Trium Decanatuum
Zbąszynensis, Grodecensis et Lwowecensis... Anno 1780... ad 1787...
expedita. Call mark: A. V. no. 28. The protocols of these
visitations, known under the name of the Okęcki visitations
(carried out under the episcopate of Andrzej Młodziejowski, later
of Onufry Okęcki), have been partly published by the Theological
Department of the Poznań Society of the Friends of Learning
(Series I, fascicles 1–3, 1917–8). They are kept in the
Archdiocesan Archives in Poznań.

[p. 1]

Introductory remarks

A. The subject and scope of the study. Literature and sources

The task of the present study is to present one of the forms of settlement in old Poland, falling in the XVI–XVIII centuries, known under the name of Dutch settlement or, in its Polonised rendering, of Olęder settlement. The territorial scope of the study covers the former voivodeship of Poznań within its borders before the first partition, that is, the then counties of Kościan, Poznań and Wałcz, and the Wschowa land.

The question of “Olęder” colonisation has so far received no broader treatment in Polish scholarship. The study of J. T. Baranowski,[^1-1] published in 1914, rests chiefly on material concerning the areas along the Vistula, while the more recent work of Inglot[^1-2] does indeed give an exhaustive survey of the literature, but takes no account of the sources. The more numerous German studies are fragmentary in character; apart from Schmidt's work,[^1-3] already outdated as regards some of its results, mention must be made here of the more recent studies of W. Maas[^1-4] and W. Schulz.[^1-5] These works show considerable discrepancies, and moreover are not always based on sufficiently secure documentary foundations. There thus arose the need to base the study, as far as possible, on source material alone, and that both material already known and material hitherto unexploited. The literature was utilised above all for comparative purposes.

Thanks to the valuable card index of localities, compiled by the Poznań Commission of the Historical Atlas of Poland,[^1-6] it was possible to study, from the point of view that

[^1-2]: Problem kolonizacji flamandzko-holenderskiej.
[^1-3]: Geschichte des Deutschtums im Lande Posen.
[^1-4]: See the list of abbreviations.
[^1-5]: Ibid.
[^1-6]: cf. Wojciechowska M., Sprawozdanie z prac nad mapą woj. poznańskiego w dobie Sejmu czteroletniego. Poznań 1934. Offprint from Sprawozdania Poznańskiego Tow. Przyjaciół Nauk no. 3 for the year 1933. Some of the source materials utilised here are characterised more closely ibid.

[p. 2]

interests us here each individual locality of the territory under investigation — which in any other case would probably have been unthinkable. The greater part of the source materials utilised is held in the State Archive in Poznań. Of these, the most valuable are the so-called village records, and next the grod books and land books for the four grods of Kościan, Poznań, Wałcz and Wschowa.[^2-1] The village records form a collection arranged alphabetically according to the individual villages, in three divisions: in division A the original diplomas (charters, grants, etc.) are kept; division B comprises copies of the diplomas; finally, in division C the remaining documents are grouped together. Among the other records use has been made of the land and mortgage records, those of the former Landratsämter, the urban records, and finally the Prussian fiscal records (of the domains, the classification and the cadastral survey), as well as old maps. The Prussian classification records were discussed by M. Bar in his work Westreussen unier Friedrich dem Grossen, Leipzig 1909, p. 210, and by M. Kniat on p. 13 ff. of his work on the economic significance of the burdens of the peasant population in Poland in the XVIII century. For the demographic part of the study particular importance attached to the following items kept in the same Archive: the table of the hearths of Kościan county, and the table of the hearths and population of the Wschowa land of 1789.[^2-2] From the holdings of the Archdiocesan Archive in Poznań the protocols of the parish visitations of 1777–87 proved particularly useful. Valuable statistical material was supplied by the manuscripts of the Fiscal Archive in Warsaw, containing tables of hearths from 1775, 1789? and 1790 (sygn. LI. 111 and LI. 508. 113), and further the table of the population of Kościan county of 1789, kept in the Main Archive in Warsaw (sygn. 163 no. 5 A). This table, like those in the other counties, was compiled as a result of a resolution of the Sejm of 22 June 1789 ordering the carrying out of the first census in the history of Poland — an incomplete one, it is true.[^2-3] Unfortunately, the scrupulous searches for an analogous table for Poznań county, carried out by the Historical Atlas Commission, remained without result. Among other sources, mention should be made in the first place of the extensive Opisy parafij of 1. 1783–4, drawn up by the parish priests of the time at the request of Primate Poniatowski, who wished in this way to obtain material

They were handed over by the individual courts to the archival depository only in part, which is why the numbering of the individual volumes could not for the time being be carried out. They are therefore cited according to the courts' own numbering, and where a series of identical sigla occurs, or where there are none at all, the number of pages or folios is given with each volume by way of distinction.
[^2-2]: A table compiled independently of the table of the same year mentioned previously.
[^2-3]: Korzon T., Wewnętrzne dzieje Polski, vol. I, p. 68.

[p. 3]

for the projected map of Poland. These manuscripts are the property of the National Library. Among the old maps, particularly useful proved to be the Map of South Prussia (of the Prussian partition), made in 1796–1805 under the direction of Brodowski, Gilly and Langner on a scale of 1 : 57,6001 (abbreviated MGd). The original map is in Berlin; in this study its photographic reprint was utilised. The abbreviations of the sources 1 of the literature are given before the text.

I acknowledge with gratitude that in many instances the notes of the Poznań Commission of the Historical Atlas of Poland, of which I had the opportunity to make full use, greatly facilitated and shortened for me the laborious process of searching out the needed source materials. To the Commission's collaborators — in particular to its head, Dr M. Wojciechowska, to A. Sikorska, and to A. Kaleta, keeper of the State Archive in Poznań — I extend on this account my warmest thanks. I am no less sincerely obliged to Professor Dr J. Rutkowski for the kind, valuable advice and guidance given to me during the writing of this work, and to Dr K. Kaczmarczyk, director of the State Archive in Poznań, for far-reaching facilities in the use of the workroom 1 of the archival collections.

*

B. The origins of “Olęder” settlement[^3-1]

The beginnings of “Olęder” colonisation in the territory of Wielkopolska are rooted in the earlier Flemish-Dutch settlement, which already in the Middle Ages was developing on the territory of Germany proper, and in the XVI century came to life again with new force in the area of Ducal and Royal Prussia. Traces of colonising activity issuing from the Netherlands appear in Germany already at the turn of the XI and XII centuries. Settlement then embraced the whole of North Germany, in the first place the surroundings of Bremen, southern Holstein (especially Wagria, after its occupation in 1140 by[^3-2] Count Adolf of Holstein), and further the duchy of Mecklenburg and Brandenburg. The colonisers Henry the Lion and Albert the Bear settled Netherlands emigrants there in large measure at the expense of the Slav population.[^3-3] Soon the colonisation spread also to Central Germany, taking in part of Saxony and of Lusatia. The colonists were always settled either on the

[^3-1]: cf. Praesent H., Der kartographische Standpunkt Siid- und Neuostpreussens. Deutsche Warschauer Zeitung no. 183–4, 1918, and: Olszewicz B., Polska kartografia wojskowa. Warsaw 1921, p. 60.
[^3-2]: Wersebe, Uber die niederlindischen Colonien; Schróder, Die niederlindischen Kolonien.
[^3-3]: Albert the Bear in particular carried on an especially vigorous colonising activity (Kronika Helmolda, I, 88, cited after Schróder, p. 18).

[p. 4]

banks of waters or on low-lying and marshy ground; thanks to the centuries-old experience of struggling against the flood of water, brought with them from their homeland, they were able, by laying out a whole system of ditches, weirs 1 embankments, to bring even quite useless stretches of land to a state of flourishing cultivation. In their husbandry the dominant role was played by the keeping of cattle and of sheep. The tradition of the Dutchmen as stock-breeders has been preserved in Mecklenburg down to our own times.[^4-1]

After the exhaustion of the first wave of settlement, a new, intensified period of emigration from the Netherlands begins in the era of the religious Reformation. The reforming movements found in the Netherlands an especially receptive soil. Luther as well as the other reformers gained numerous adherents here. Sects arose, such as, for example, that of the Anabaptists 1 of the Mennonites. On the other hand, both Charles V and Philip II of Spain, fervent Catholics, were determined to root out heresy with fire and sword in the Netherlands subject to them. Conditions thus arose in which not only the Mennonites but also a considerable part of the remaining population was compelled to seek refuge beyond the borders of the country. This time Prussia — Ducal as well as Royal — stood open to the emigrants. After the secularisation of Prussia, Albert Hohenzollern gladly welcomed to his lands fellow-confessionals persecuted in their own country.[^4-2] In favour of receiving them there spoke, no doubt, also the experience which the Germans had had with Netherlands colonists in the XII and XIII centuries. The first Dutch settlement in Ducal Prussia arose in 1527 in the vicinity of the present town of Pasłęk (Preussisch Holland),[^4-3] and soon after that a series of further ones were founded. Somewhat later Dutch colonies began to arise in Royal Prussia. Danzig initiated the colonising activity. That city had received as early as 1454, from King Kazimierz Jagiellończyk as a gift, the Żuławy Gdańskie, and after unsuccessful attempts to settle them with the local population, from the middle of the XVI century, on the initiative of the burgomaster Ferber,[^4-4] Dutchmen began to be settled there.[^4-5] Dutch colonisation in Royal Prussia now began to draw ever wider circles. The settlers were either brought directly from Holland by sea through Danzig,[^4-6] or they came from Ducal Prussia, which they were induced to leave by the economic difficulties

[^4-1]: Behcim-Schwarzbach, Hohenzollernsche Colonisationen, p. 124.
[^4-2]: Schmidt, Geschichte, p. 315.
[^4-3]: Schumacher, Niederliindische Ansiedlungen, p. 25.
[^4-4]: Ludkiewicz, Osady holenderskie, p. 31.
[^4-5]: Schmidt, Geschichte, p. 316. Baranowski, Wsie holenderskie, p. 66.
[^4-6]: Ludkiewicz, Osady holenderskie, p. 31.

[p. 5]

created by Albert of Prussia.[^5-1] In 1562 the Dutch settlement of Tygienhof arises on the Wielka Żuława?. Similar settlements[^5-2][^5-3] appear also in the Puck starostwo (the settlement of Kniewskie Błoto) 3, and at the same time the march of Dutch settlement up the Vistula begins. Montawy are colonised, as are Zajączkowo Wielkie and Małe,[^5-4] then Szkaradowo in the Sztum starostwo 5,[^5-5] and there also grows up a whole series of settlements in the sartawicko-nowskie valley 6. In the Dybów starostwo the settlement of Silno arises as early as 1562 ?. On the left bank of the Vistula[^5-6] further Dutch settlements are founded in the neighbourhood of Fordon and of Bydgoszcz,[^5-8] of Toruń (Nieszawka Wielka 1 Mała)[^5-7], on the right bank in the neighbourhood of Solec[^5-9] and in the Dobrzyń land "1. Moving further up the Vistula, on the right[^5-10] side of its course we meet the settlements of the Dutchmen in the Bobrowniki starostwo, on the left in eastern Kujawy, in the Gostyń land and in the Sochaczew land. In 1624 the colonising movement reaches Warsaw; the Dutchmen settle on the Saska Kępa !?. It must nevertheless be noted that with the pas-[^5-11][^5-12]sage of time the ethnic composition of the “Dutch” settlements becomes more complicated; besides the Dutchmen proper, an undoubted participation of the German and of the Polish nationality can be discerned in the settlement 1%. Settlements of this kind we meet in various parts of Poland, e.g. in Volhynia, and their formation can be observed right up to the moment of the partitions, while the process

Just as in Germany in the Middle Ages, the Dutch colonists were settled on low-lying, waterlogged ground, flooded by inundations. Everywhere the best possible results were achieved; the Dutchmen knew how to maintain themselves even on the most waterlogged and flood-exposed sites. They governed themselves by their own “Dutch law”, which constituted a kind of variant of the old

[^5-2]: Baranowski, Wsie holenderskie, p. 66.
[^5-3]: This settlement is mentioned by Lustr. pruska 1664, p. 123.
[^5-4]: Ibid., p. 92.
[^5-5]: Ibid., p. 143.
[^5-6]: Ludkiewicz, Osady holenderskie, p. 32. The settlers here were Dutch Mennonites.
[^5-7]: Schmidt, Geschichte, p. 318.
[^5-8]: Ibid., p. 319.
[^5-9]: Szper, Nederlandsche Nederzetuingen, p. 238 (cited after Baranowski).
[^5-10]: Schmidt, Geschichte, p. 320.
[^5-11]: Baranowsk:, Wsie holenderskie, p. 60. Iż ‘Trże, S. 57– 0.
[^5-12]: For example, the “Dutchmen” settled in Wielkie Książki in the Brodnica starostwo bore, according to Lustr. pruska 1664, p. 63, the surnames: Bahr, Prusak, Maniecki, Meller, Karkiu, Spychacz, Bieniek, Bednarz. Hcuer (Die Hollinderdorier, p. 122–155) cites the village of Brzoza near Toruń, where in 1788, of 4 “Olęders”, 3 were Poles.

[p. 6]

Chełmno law !, and it very much recalled the legal foundations on which[^6-1] colonists from the Netherlands were settled in the Middle Ages.[^6-2] Comparing the legal position of the Dutchman settler with that of the medieval peasant located under German law (by which was understood also the Chełmno law), we find the following most important differences:

I. The Dutchmen settled on the basis of contracts which the lord concluded not with a locator appearing in his own name as the future superior factor in the communal hierarchy, but with the whole commune, or with a deputy acting in its name;

2. the whole village answered solidarily for the obligations towards the lord. This solidarity was either expressly stated in the location document, or presumed 3,[^6-3] 3. the indispensable consequence of the equality of all the members of the commune vis-à-vis the lord was their equal standing in the internal relations of the commune, and a strong sense of community within the commune.[^6-4] Hence the locator, if there was one, received no lasting advantages in the form of exemption from rents, and the sołtys was elective;[^6-5]

4. all the manorial obligations of the Dutchmen amounted to money rents, whereas in the villages under German law — quite apart from the labour dues, minimal in any case — rents in kind played an important role. It must here, however, be noted that if we have in view[^6-6]

Ducal Prussia, certain small labour dues were imposed there on the Dutchmen too, it is true,[^6-7] which became a precedent at the founding of the later settlements on the Polish lands;[^6-8]

[^6-1]: Tnyglot, Problem kolonizacji, p. 41; Schumacher, Niederlindische Ansiedlungen p. 84; Baranowski, Wsie holenderskie, p. 73. cf. also Lustr. pruska 1664, p. 54: “Under which law (i.e. the location charter) iidem (the Olęders) are conservantur [are maintained], however in such wise that the said włókas be sown as soon as possible and the rent due to the castle, at 18 grosz per morg, be paid
[^6-2]: and be subject to the castle under the Olęder law.”
[^6-3]: Beheim-$chwarzbach, Netzcdistrikt, p. 125 ff.
[^6-4]: Most often it took the form of paying the lord jointly a certain global rent. Such, as appears from Lustr. pruska 1664, was the state of affairs in the Dutch villages of the Prussian royal estates.
[^6-5]: An expression of this community was the elaborately developed system of administrative norms of communal self-government (the wilkierz).
[^6-6]: Schumacher, Niederlindische Ansiedlungen, p. 88.
[^6-7]: Kaczmarczyk K., Ciężary ludności wiejskiei i miejskiej na prawie niemieckim w Polsce XIII 1 XIV w., Przegl. Hist., vol. XI, p. 19 ff., and Rutkowski, Zarys dziejów, p. 47. A somewhat different light on this matter is cast by the work of J. Masłowskicego, Kolonizacja wiejska na prawie niemieckim w województwach sieradzkim, łęczyckim, na Kujawach i w ziemi dobrzyńskiej, Roczniki Hist. XIII, 2, p. 278. In the territory discussed there money rents played the decisive role.
[^6-8]: Schumacher, Niedcerlindische Ansiedlungen, p. 96.

[p. 7]

5. the Dutchmen's right to the land rested as a rule on a lease for years, amounting to some fifteen to several tens of years.[^7-1] Later, however, we meet also with perpetual lease.[^7-2]

From the serf population of the time the Dutchmen differed in law also in that, like the settlers under German law, they were personally free.[^7-3] All these differences, and further the already-mentioned type of agricultural culture, consisting in the improvement of the land by drainage and in the tilling of the soil in one piece, and finally the linguistic distinctness, constituted a complex of characteristics distinguishing the Dutchmen in the new settlement environment. C. The characteristics of the “Olęder” settlements in the area of the former voivodeship of Poznań

“Olęder” settlement in the former voivodeship of Poznań constitutes the counterpart, and at the same time a further stage, of Dutch colonisation in the area of Royal and Ducal Prussia.[^7-4] To denote the settlers we find in the Polish sources most often the term “olędrzy” (occasionally in the spelling “oleęndrzy”), more rarely “olendrzy” and “holendrzy”, while in the German texts: Holldnder and Hauldnder. The duality of the German terminology provoked among German authors disputes as to whether the terms Holldnder and Hauldnder had distinct meanings[^7-5] or were two equivalent designations.[^7-6] The disputes arose from the fact that the “Olęders” also cleared forests, founding their settlements far from bodies of water, on dry, wooded ground.[^7-7] Opinions therefore appeared that the term Hauldnder had no connection with Dutch colonisation proper, but denoted a settler farming land cleared by himself (Hauland, from the word hauen — to cut, to clear 7,

[^7-1]: Ludkiewicz, Osady holenderskie, p. 27, 32. Lusir. pruska 1664, p. 63 and 325 (30 years), p. 80, 92 and 246 (50 years). In Ducal Prussia leasehold for years found application only under Albert's successor (Schumacher, p. 84). Villages under German law, on the other hand, were located, in all probability, on the terms of perpetual lease. cf. Masłowski, Kolonizacja wiejska, p. 75.
[^7-2]: Lustr. pruska 1664, p. 328 (Walknowy in the Osiek starostwo).
[^7-3]: cf. Rutkowski, Zarys dziejów, p. 47 and Ludkiewicz, Osady holenderskie, p. 27.
[^7-4]: See further, p. 12.
[^7-5]: Guradze, among others, took up this position in his work: Der Baucr in Posen, p. 244.
[^7-6]: See further, p. 55.
[^7-7]: This view had already been expressed at the close of the XVIII century by Stenger (Die Hauliinder in Sidpreussen, p. 247). He quoted the following explanation from a contemporary statistical-geographical dictionary (Topographisch-Statistisch-Greographisches Wórterbuch der simmtl. preuss. Staaten, u. s. w., ] Th., Halle 1790): “Hauland in der gemeinen Mundart Holand oder Hollande polnisch Olędry, bedeutet ein urbares Feld, dass nur, nachdem erst alles darau! gewachsene Holz abgehauen, und dann aui einem FHlaufen verbraunt war, bebaut werden konnte” [‘Hauland in the common speech Holland or Hollande, in Polish Olędry, means an arable field which could be tilled only after all the timber growing on it had first been felled and then burnt on a heap’].

[p. 8]

This view, however, has been recognised — and quite rightly so — as unjustified, for the term Hauldinder is met with for the first time only in the official Prussian records after the first partition. Before that, to denote[^8-1] all the settlers, the term Holldnder alone was used. That both terms were used interchangeably, irrespective of the manner in which the settlements arose, is demonstrated irrefutably by the following example: for the settlement of Ujskie Olędry, founded on a drained riverside lowland, not on forest ground, the German name Usch Hzuland became established, whereas the settlement of Jędrzejowo, which arose by the clearing of forest, bore the German name Hollanderdorf. At present the view prevails that the word Hauldinder is a later, synonymous variant of the word Holldnder[^8-2] (just as the Polish “holender” passed into “olęder”), and that this variant arose through the confusion of the name of the settlers with the name of forest clearings.[^8-3]

“Olęder” settlement was not the only form of settlement in Wielkopolska in the early modern period. Hładyłowicz[^8-4] divides all the settlements that arose in those times into: holendry, budy, huty, hamry 1 kuźnice, sołtys villages and colonies, forest settlements and others. Hence, as the first and most important task of the study, there presents itself the need to define what is to be understood by “Olęder” settlements in the area of the former voivodeship of Poznań, and in what these settlements differed from the remaining ones. Such a distinction is all the more necessary in that, besides the considerable number of settlements about which all the sources agree in speaking as “Olęders”, there existed a certain number of settlements whose character appears unclearly in the source tradition; and finally such as are classified wrongly as “Olęder” by certain sources, lists of localities or maps, although with the settlements of the proper “Olęder” type they

[^8-1]: cf. the dates of the previous note. Not entirely consistent with this is the phrase used in the charter for the Radoszkowskie Olęders of 1781 (Rel. Cost. no. 215, p. 351): “Haulendry Radoszkowscy”, undoubtedly an echo of the German “Hauliinder”, which would seem to indicate that the latter term was by then in use not only in official circles. Quite exceptionally, under the undoubted influence of the German terminology, the word “nolendry” appears in Polish texts in the sense of cleared fields (Haulinder). Thus, for instance, in the charter of 1793 for Tomasz Galas of the Nowe Łoskońskie Ol. (AW. B. 367) we read: “A certain farmstead, or rather Hollendry, lying in the Długołęka forest, formerly adapted for the burning of potash 1 the digging of iron ore... lying in the neighbourhood of other Holendry on the left side, and of the same origin... we ordered to be measured out...”.
[^8-2]: Beheim-Schwarzbach, Hohenzollernsche Colonisationen, p. 123–4. Schmidt, Geschichte, p. 392; Maas, Haulindereien, p. 200; Baranowski, Wsie holenderskie, p. 70: Schmitz, Geschichte, p. 151.
[^8-3]: Kremmer u. Dalchow, Die Provinz Posen, p. 50; Schitze, Das Posener Land, p. 279.
[^8-4]: Zmiany krajobrazu, p. 86.

[p. 9]

have nothing in common. As the starting point for further considerations we shall take the enumeration, given in the preceding chapter, of the differences between the Dutch settlers and the local population in the territory of Prussia.

I. The criterion of nationality, essential in relation to the settler-Dutchmen of Prussia in the first period of colonisation, loses its significance in the area of the former voivodeship of Poznań. Of the Dutch nationality of the settlers, especially in the later period, it is difficult here to speak at all; instead of it we have the German and the Polish nationality 1.[^9-1] In order to emphasise that the ethnic affiliation of the settlers is not the factor deciding the essence of “Olęder” settlement, and also in agreement with the terminology of the sources, in the further course of the study the term olędrzy will be used to denote the settlers, and olędry to denote the settlements.

2. Another characteristic — settlement on low-lying ground and the preparation[^9-2] of the soil for tillage by way of drainage — likewise does not adequately characterise the Olęder settlements in the territory of north-western Wielkopolska. The majority of these settlements arose on dry, wooded sites, and the improvement of the land took place not through drainage but through clearing?.

3. What remain are distinctions of a legal character. Of these, personal freedom is the privilege of almost all the settlers located in the XVI–XVIII centuries, and consequently provides no basis for separating the Olęder settlements from the other, younger settlements. The essence of the Olęders' distinctness must be sought in certain principles of the so-called Olęder law, which have been preserved in their social organisation. This concerns in the first place the settlers' relation to the lord. As in the territory of Prussia, it is regulated by a charter which the lord issues on the basis of an agreement with the whole commune, or with a deputy acting in its name. The manorial obligations are fixed per unit of land; every settler pays them in strict proportion to the area of his farm. Often the whole commune answers solidarily for their fulfilment by the appointed term. A privileged position vis-à-vis the lord is held only by the locator 1 the innkeeper. The first, for services already rendered, and the second

[^9-1]: Kremmer u. Dalchow, Provinz Posen, p. 49: “Wenn man im 16 und 17 Jahrhundert cinen Baucrn als Hollinder bezeichnete, so dachte man nicht an seine Nauonalitir, sondern an seine Titigkelt und seine Rechtstellung.” [‘If in the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries one called a peasant a Holländer, one was not thinking of his nationality but of his activity and his legal position.’] Belieim-Schwarzbuch, Netzedisujkt, p. 121: “A ndersents ist. cs ganz augeuscheinlich, dass die Heimath vieler dieser Leute unmóglich HoJland gewesen sein kam. Die Drewnoer „Hollander” sind sogar durchwcg Polen und tragen vollstindig slavische Namenu, eben so die von Chalupsk, Szerzawy, und anderc, deren Namer. weiter unten angegeben sind.” [‘On the other hand it is quite obvious that the homeland of many of these people cannot have been Holland. The “Hollander” of Drewno are even, without exception, Poles and bear entirely Slav names, as do those of Chalupsk, Szerzawy and others, whose names are given further below.’] cf. also Baranowski, Wsie holenderskie, p. 75.
[^9-2]: Maas, Haulindereien, p. 201. Warschauer, Geschichte der Prov. Posen, p. 104.

[p. 10]

on account of services still to be rendered they receive, at the measuring-out of the village, a certain area of land free from rent. The Olęder settlements are also distinguished by the character of their communal self-government. The administration and jurisdiction in the commune[^10-1] are exercised by a sołtys and by ławniks elected annually. There is thus no hereditary sołtys[^10-2] nominated by the lord, permanently endowed with certain privileges, as was the case in the colonisation under German law. Among the members of the commune there exists a far-reaching coexistence and co-operation. Equality and solidarity, both in relation to the lord and in their mutual communal relations, thus constitute the essence of Olęder settlement in the territory of the former voivodeship of Poznań 1. The rent-charge, as the legal-economic basis of the Olęders' relation to the manor, is in many cases a valuable indication when establishing the character of settlements, but it does not possess the significance of a decisive criterion. For especially in the XVIII century non-Olęder rent villages are in the area of the former voivodeship of Poznań a fairly frequent phenomenon. The same applies to leasehold for years; this form of holding land, at first characteristic of the Olęders, is completely absent from the later Olęder settlements; in its place we have the perpetual lease proper also to the other types of younger settlements.

The soundness of the distinctions made above is confirmed by the terminology of the sources. The settlements classed as Olęder on the basis of the considerations presented here are also designated in the sources, as a rule, as Olęder; conversely, the settlements whose source terminology is uncertain usually show the absence of one or other of the elements we have adduced as characterising this settlement. These settlements will be discussed at greater length in the following chapter.

There remains to be considered the question whether the essence of Olęder settlement in the area of the former voivodeship of Poznań remained unchanged throughout the whole time of the settlements' existence, or whether in the course of the years it underwent a certain evolution. The sources show that such an evolution did exist

[^10-1]: in the direction of an assimilation with settlements located under German law. This came about either through the individualisation of the originally solidary
[^10-2]: The view that the term “olędry” is, in relation to Wielkopolska, a concept of a legal character is shared by a number of authors, though not always in a sufficiently clear and exhaustive form. We quote here the most apt sentence of Baranowski (Wsie holenderskie, *. 0): “The name ‘Holendry’ thus acquires in Poland a different meaning; it ceases to characterise the nationality of the colonists 1 the economic tasks of the colony, adapting itself to a certain social organisation of the colonists, having forms modelled on those which the proper Dutch colonies once assumed. In the XVIII century the name ‘Holendry’ is in Poland rather a legal than an ethnic term.” Already during the war (1943) there appeared the work of Reinhard, based on materials relating to the eastern part of the present-day voivodeship of Poznań. Its results (p. Gl–04) are in complete agreement with the characterisation of the Olęder settlements given here.

[p. 11]

1 based on the equality of all the members of the commune in their relation to the lord, or through the disappearance of the equal standing of the Olęders within the commune in favour of the sołtys. The first case occurred especially when individual Olęders settled their accounts with the lord separately for the earlier bringing of new lands, not covered by the charter, under the plough, or obtained individually the consent for the future use of parts of the manorial lands.[^11-1] The enlargement of a settlement, however, through the creation and annexation of new farms caused no differentiation within the commune. For usually in such cases the charter contained a formula permitting new settlers to join the commune, of course with the lord's consent,[^11-2] or else the lord issued a new common charter for all the members of the commune.[^11-3] The question of Olęder settlement formally ceases to exist with the introduction by Prussia of a uniform communal self-government and of the regulation of rural conditions carried out by virtue of the statute of 1823. The years of the regulation thus form the lower chronological limit of the present study.

[^11-1]: Such cases were especially frequent in the settlements belonging to the Wieleń estate.
[^11-2]: Rel. Cost. no. 155, 1719–20, fol. 144, Tarnowa: “For future times, should any Hollender besides the present ones wish to settle on these lands, then this is to take place with the authority and by the will of the lord.” Rel. Posn. no. 643, 1792, fol. 94, Sękowo Ol.: “Should a new inhabitant wish to have lands made out to himself, then the names written on the margin of this charter are the ones he must bear.”
[^11-3]: cf. the charters for the Nowe (Wielkokrobielewskie) and Gębickie Ol. in Appendix I.

[p. 12]

CHAPTER I The rise of the settlements, their geographical distribution, and their numbers in the period of the Four-Year[^12-1] Sejm

The oldest surviving location charter from the territory of the former voivodeship of Poznań concerns the Ujskie Olędry. They were founded in 1597. The second surviving location document, in order, is the charter of 1601 for the Olęders of Nowe Dwory in the Wieleń estate. In this second case, as Schmidt 1 rightly observes, one can point to an undoubted connection between Dutch settlement in Royal Prussia and in the voivodeship of Poznań. Namely, Jan Czarnkowski, heir of the Wieleń estate, had as his wife the widow of Jan Kostka, starosta of Świecie, née Herburt. She, while still the starościna of Świecie, settled Dutchmen by the village of Stwolno (in 1593). That she took part also after her marriage to Czarnkowski in colonising the Wieleń estate with Olęders is proved by the names of the settlements: Herbardowo (Herburtowo) and Folsztyn (from the surname Herburt z Fulsztyna). These settlements, and also Maryanowo O.[^12-2], arose likewise shortly after 1600 3. The next settlement in chronological order is that of the Murzynowskie Olędry, located in 1613 in the Międzyrzecz starostwo, and then the Olędry Róża, founded[^12-3][^12-4] in 1624 4 in the Krobielew estate. All these settlements arose on low-lying, waterlogged ground; the Ujskie Olędry and the Olęders of the Wieleń estate on the Noteć, the Murzynowskie Olędry and the Olędry Róża on the Warta.

[^12-1]: Geschichte, p. 323.
[^12-2]: According to the mention in the lawsuit records of the Wieleń castle, cited by W. $chulz (Die zweite deutsche Ostsiedlung, p. 140), this settlement was located by a charter of 3. 3. 1614.
[^12-3]: The exact date cannot be established, for the relevant location charters have not survived.
[^12-4]: cf. Appendix I b. According to Werner-Steffani (Geschichte der evangelischen Parochicen, p. 372), the Ol. Świniary were founded in 1619, and thus before this date. The author did not, however, state from what source he took this information.

[p. 13]

After the founding of the settlements mentioned there follows a period of temporary pause. Colonising activity, this time already on a broad scale, revives at the close of the XVII century and continues with unabated force until the partitions.[^13-1] Great clusters of settlements form: on the Noteć along the Czarnków–Wieleń line, on the Warta in the neighbourhood of Międzychód, and to the east of the Obra line. On the border of the former voivodeships of Poznań, Kalisz 1 Gniezno we have two further great clusters of settlements: the first on the Warta along the Śrem–Pyzdry stretch, and the second, stretching in a broad belt along the line Czerniejewo–Murowana Goślina–Rogoźno–Chodzież. All the settlements lie in the former counties of Kościan and Poznań; in Wałcz county and in the Wschowa land Olęder settlements are entirely absent. The distribution of the settlements is shown on the map appended at the end. In a number of cases the drawing of the boundaries of the individual counties on it presented great difficulty. The boundaries drawn on the map are of a provisional character; the Poznań Atlas Commission is working on their exact reconstruction.

In the brochure Uber Ursprung und Verbreitung des Deuischtums im Grossherzogihum Posen, published in 1849 (p. 34), the number of Olęder settlements in the territory of Wielkopolska was put at 400. This figure was repeated 1 by the later authors as well. In recent times, in the sphere of the statistics of settlements, independent attempts have been made by Hładyłowicz and Maas. The former, in his work on the changes of the landscape and the development of settlement in Wielkopolska from the XIV to the XIX century, reckoned for Kościan county 64, and for Poznań county 133 localities. The author writes that these are settlements of the XVI and XVII centuries, which, however, must be explained as an error, perhaps a printer's error, since for example in Kościan county the settlements date, in all the instances known to us, from the XVIII century, and it was that century the author undoubtedly had in mind. The above figures do not reach the actual number of settlements. These differences nevertheless become smaller if we take into account that a part of the settlements was classed by Hładyłowicz among settlements of other types. To entirely different results as to the number of settlements Maas came in his work Die Posener Hauländereien (DWZP, 34), supplemented in 1941 by the article: Weitere Posener Hauländereien (DWZW, 2). This author, as will be said later, arrived at the conviction that at least 75% of the inhabitants of the Olęder settlements belonged to the German nationality 1. In the interest, therefore, of that nationality he made every effort to increase the number of the settlements as far as possible. To this end, independently of his long and detailed disquisitions on which settlements should, and which should not, be regarded as Olęder, he noted down from various lists of localities and maps all the settlements with the addition “olędry”, or of equivalent designations

[^13-1]: See further, p. 31.

[p. 14]

and classed them all as Olęder settlements. In this way he arrived at the figure of 832 localities, that is, as he writes, he succeeded in doubling the number of settlements given hitherto.[^14-1] The author indeed qualifies this, observing that some of the localities he mentions may belong to settlements of other types, and that he may have counted some others twice, “but all that again does not amount to very much”. In reality the number of settlements in the territory of Wielkopolska is about 550, so that the author's errors amount to one third of the figure derived by him. The errors are in fact even more considerable, for the author, without having carried out any source research, has, despite the best of intentions, overlooked a whole series of Olęder settlements. The author's mistakes consist most often in counting certain villages twice,[^14-2] in adding on settlements which never

[^14-1]: The figure given is difficult to check, for the author compiled his enumeration in a very unclear manner. In one place he states that a given settlement corresponds to another one, elsewhere he writes again: “see” the other settlement, or even: “is it identical” with the other settlement? It is likewise unknown whether, in the case when a village, for example, consisted of three parts also bearing their own separate names, the author counted it as 3 or as 4 villages (e.g. the Szamocin Ol.; here, by the way, the author made an error, since the colonies of Antoniny and Atanazyn were never part of the Szamocin Ol., having been founded only about 1820 as a result of the Germanising action in the annexed territories). The classing among the Olęder settlements of colonies that arose in the Prussian period is of course a mistake, since these colonies lacked the characteristics of that settlement (a government or some other entrepreneur recruited the individual colonists, and the whole settlement was the planned creation of that entrepreneur).
[^14-2]: Thus, for example: Dąbrowo Ol. (on the border of Pyzdry county; with the author as Dąbrowo and as Grodzewo), Grabowiec Ol. (as Birkwerder and as Bzower Hld; the author, moreover, admits the mistake himself, though outside the text), Lubaskie Ol. (as Lubascher Hld. and as Miłkówko Hild.), Łoskońskie Stare Ol. (as Alt Laskun and as Gozdowiec), Obra (either Nowe or Stare Oborskie Ol.), Olszewniak (as Olszewniak and as Chmielowo), Pietronki (as Miren Hld. and as Pietronke), Wymyślanka (as Wymyslanke 1 as Maschlanke). Some settlements the author counted even three times, e.g. Mechnacz (with the author separately Rozbitek, Mechnacz, Urbanówko; the Ol. Mechnacz were sometimes called Urbanówek because they were located (in 1784) also on part of the abandoned holdings of Urbanówek), or the Szarki Ol. The author cites Głuchowo wrongly, following Schmidt. The document A. 1, kept in the village records, was assigned by mistake to the folder of Głuchowo in Szamotuły county, whereas it relates to Głuchowo in the parish of Goraj. Nor is it at all a location document, but a copy of a judgment of the patrimonial court of 1741. At that time the parish contained the Ol. Róża (founded 1024), and in all probability the document mentioned concerns precisely that settlement. The Fitzen Hid. and Frey Hld. given by the author are parts of the Pącheńskie Ol.; they did not form independent communes; neither name figures in the sources of the Polish period. The Ol. Glinki included two houses marked on MGd as Sątop Ol. (with the author Hollendry); there are no data showing that these farms already existed in the Polish period. The name Jabłonka Nowa (Neu Jablonke) appears for the first time only in the list of 1845 (1843). This is probably a part of the Nowosileńskie Ol., or else a completely new settlement. The old village of Wierzchabno has nothing in common with the Ol. Strychowskie. It cannot have been part of them, if only on account of its position in relation to the Olęders. The name Goraj Ol. refers to one of the settlements belonging to the Goraj estate, or else all the settlements of the Goraj estate are understood by it.

[p. 15]

never existed 1, and finally in classing as Olęder settlements of another type?.

For an enumeration of the Olęder settlements to have any value at all, it is necessary, having first established suitable criteria, to carry out, in relation to each individual locality of the territory under investigation, examinations aimed at determining whether a given settlement belongs to the Olęder type. We gave such criteria in the preceding chapter. On their basis there was compiled the list, given in the appendix, of the Olęder settlements existing in the territory of the former voivodeship of Poznań 1 For example, the Rajewskie or Wyszyńskie Ol., wrongly given already, by the way, by W. Schulz (Die zw. d. Ostsiedlung). By that last name were designated collectively the settlements belonging to the Wyszyny estate, namely the Raczyńskie, Filipowo and Józefowo Ol., at times also the Huta Ol. Besides other sources this is shown exactly by MGd. In just the same way there never existed the Kicińskie Ol. (= Kliny, Czerwonak and Janikowo Ol.), Ćmak Ol., Dronniki Ol., Koninko Ol., Smolarnia, Trocken Hld. (by the Kamińskie Ol.) % An example here may be Chojno near Wronki. In reality this is an old Polish contract village; it never had anything in common with the Olęder settlements, and its marking on MG as olędry occurred through an obvious mistake (cf. AGH court of Wronki, Chojno estate, Generalia fol. 38). The author classes as Olęders a whole series of such villages, among others: Chudopsice, Grzebienisko, Jaroszewo, Jezierze, Krobielewko (Klein Krebbel), Samołęż, Stajkowo, Sławienko, and further several rent villages located under German law (the so-called sołtys villages); we shall mention here, for instance, Gościejewo, Podlesie, Turowskie Ol. This last settlement is marked wrongly as olędry in some sources; 3 settlers sat here under individual, separate charters (of the years 1771, 1778 and 1781), and entirely separate obligations bound them as well. (cf. AGH court of Pniewy, Turowo estate, vol. I, fol. 188–9). As olędry there figure also with Maas folwarks (e.g. Mokre or Mokrz), mill settlements

(e.g. Mniszek), forest lodges (e.g. Tarnówko), and finally the colonies of the Prussian period (e.g. Birkenhorst (a settlement that developed from the older suburb of Trzciel), Julianka, Władysławowo (a settlement that came into being in the first half of the XIX century from the forest lodge of that same name), Lubs Gross and Klein, Schwaben Hld., Siewert Hld. (on MG 1 settler), Trzydomy near Chodzież (this hamlet is absent from the classification records of 1773), probably also Wełna Ol. (for the first time on MG and MGd I — a settler), and the settlement of Schwarzlug (sometimes described as a forest lodge (Fórsterei)); this settlement could not be located; near the Trzciel Hamr there lay separate Ol. Czarne (Scharne). Such questions of the author as: “are they Olęders?” (e.g. Stróżewo), or: “since when Olęders?” (e.g. Piotrowo), “where?” (e.g. Fiechoren, or else: Bożny, Bronisz, Kościelec, Buckow Hld.) must be regarded as pointless, since the settlements called in doubt were never Olęder, or (in the third case) did not exist. Besides this, the author gathered a mass of erroneous data concerning the settlements from other sources, e.g. from the Słownik Geograficzny K. P. (Sowinki, Stodólsko, Włościejewki, Różyńskie Ol. etc.), from the studies of Goldmann (Glinno or Glinki, and probably Boruja as well), Hampel (Bukowiec), and even of W. Schulz (Jędrzejewskie Ol.). Equally wrongly does the author infer the founding of the Nowe Jastrzębskie and Chojnickie Ol. from an article on the Zbąszyń Olęders in the newspaper Posener Tageblatt of 18 March 1934. We quote only examples relating to the former voivodeship of Poznań, for it is not possible within the frame of the present study to enumerate all the errors committed by the author. The author made no effort to present the true state of affairs; he did not even examine the fundamental source for knowing the numbers of the settlements, namely the original (large) map of Gilly, kept in the state collections in Berlin.

[p. 16]

in 1789. This year was adopted as the basis of the calculations for the reason that it is precisely from that time that there date a series of important sources, already mentioned in the introduction, which make it possible to carry out fairly exact calculations of the population of the settlements. According to the above list the number of Olęder settlements is: in Kościan county 111, in Poznań county 156 settlements whose Olęder character appears clearly in the sources, 11 settlements of transitional type, and 4 settlements whose belonging to the Olęder settlements could not, on account of insufficient, uncertain or contradictory source tradition, be established with greater probability. In sum we obtain 282 localities. The known dates of location of the settlements have been given in the appropriate column of the list. Question marks in brackets at some of the dates mean that in those cases the date of the charter was adopted as the date of location. In qualifying the settlements a rather broad interpretation was adopted. Thus all those settlements were classed as Olęder about whose organisation (of decisive significance, as is known) there were no data, provided only that the sources unanimously described them as olędry. We shall illustrate the manner of deciding about the character of the settlements by two examples:[^16-1][^16-2]

d. 1; TB Wymysłowo, an inn belonging to Czarkowo, własn. szlach.”?,
