hearth; Verz/1845, no. 1492 — Wymysłowo, a forestry belonging to Czarkowo, d. 1; MP, MG and MGd — absent. The review of the sources leads one to assume here that probably about 1775 there existed in this place a new settlement (an inn, the forester's cottage, and 1 or 2 further dwelling buildings). It was called the Czarkowskie Olędry. In 1783 we already have only mentions of the inn 1 the forestry, or of the inn alone. Of an Olęder settlement there can, consequently, be no question. A further example:
[^16-2]: Burzykowo in the parish of Oborniki. Sources: MP — absent. MG and MGd — Burzykower Holl (one house); Tp. 89, 90 — the Burzykowo cottage, 1789 — absent, 1790 — I hearth. Of all the other sources of the Polish period

[p. 17]

— absent. TV — the Burzykowo cottage, folw., inn, d. 3. (własn. szlach.). From this it is evident that the marking on MG and MGd is erroneous. This is probably a newly founded folwark, or else an individual farm. The absence of sources from the Polish period and the name “cottage” give rise even to the doubt whether at that time this was not some abandoned building, inhabited only occasionally.[^17-1]

Two special situations required a separate decision: when an old settlement received Olęder rights, and when single-homestead settlements were called olędry. In the first case, despite the absence of a new location in the material sense, there arose, in the legal sense, a new Olęder settlement, and from then on it must be assigned to such unconditionally. Single-homestead settlements, however — whose number is, moreover, small 1 and which are usually treated unclearly in the sources — do not belong to the Olęder settlements. For here the coming into being of the conditions deciding their classing among the Olęder settlement is not possible. These are usually villages located on the principles of German law, and they acquired the name of olędry in some sources because they arose in the early modern period together with the Olęders 1, either in the surroundings of Olęder settlements, or else they were settled by settlers coming from those settlements. On the basis of the sources the following single-homestead settlements can be eliminated:

In Kościan county: 1. Katrynki,[^17-2] a folwark, today Katarzynki in the parish of Dłużyna; 2. Szklana Huta,[^17-3] a folwark in the parish of Bukowiec; 3. The settlement of Wymysłowo already mentioned.

In Poznań county: I. Budy, a folwark (2 1/5 hubas); 2. The already-mentioned Burzykowo; 3. Czerwieniec, a pitch kiln, or else a folwark, in the parish of Międzychód; 4. Kamień,[^17-5] in the parish of Międzychód; 5. Krebs,[^17-4] parish of Skwierzyna; 6. Kurzastopa,[^17-6] in the parish of Sieraków ] gosp.; 7. Nadziepozna

[^17-1]: Maas classes this hamlet too among the Olęder ones.
[^17-2]: Comparison of the sources: MP — Katrynka; MG — Vw. Katrynka; VO 83 (10 TX 1777) — a manorial folwark called “a new colony”; O 83 XXII — “Holędrzy, a little folwark”; Tod. 89 — Katrynki d. I., i.e. manorial buildings, 1 d.; Tol. — Katrynki Holędry; OWK 89 — “Katrynka, a hereditary folwark”.
[^17-3]: Comparison of the sources: MG — Vw. Glashiitte; TV — Huta (Glashiitte); Tol. d. 3.
[^17-4]: Tod. 89 — d. 3., of which manorial buildings 1 d., gardener's cottages 2 d.; Verz/1800 — Huta folwark.
[^17-5]: This settlement was founded only about 1800 (according to Verz/1800 it had 1 d.; earlier sources are lacking). The proper village arose in 1802–1809, when the Prussian minister Stein was one of its co-owners (hence the German name Neustein).
[^17-6]: This settlement appears for the first time on MP as a folwark. All the other sources of the Polish period are lacking. In Verz/1800: Hol. d. 2.; in Verz/1821, no. 239: Krebs CGolonie.

[p. 18]

jewskie Ol.1 in the parish of Goraj; 8. Podolany, or Golęcińskie Ol., in the parish[^18-1] of Poznań (Św. Wojciech) 1 gosp.; 9. Stojenka in the parish of Parkowo 1 — — gosp.[^18-2]; 10. Szóstaki, parish of Sieraków;[^18-3] 11. Trzy Góry in the parish of Ryczywół. The Olęder living there belonged to the commune of the Gościejewskie Olędry, and there too he was entered. 12. Turowskie Ol. in the parish of Brody; 13. Zwierzyniec,[^18-4] in the parish of Międzychód, 1 gosp.

There were also cases in which Olęders were settled in villages still partly inhabited by a serf population. Mixed settlements arose in this way. The Olęders were then usually given a separate self-government 1 treated as an independent commune. Their separation from the total number of the inhabitants presented as a rule no difficulty; it was harder, however, to divide between them and the serf peasants the other groups of the rural population (craftsmen, lodgers, etc.), for it is not known how many of these inhabitants lived from work for the Olęders and how many from work for the peasants. In every such case, therefore, one had to have recourse to a more or less probable estimate 5.

As a special case there must still be discussed the settlement of Jędrzejewo. It was located by Piotr Czarnkowski in 1625 6, and in the charter he calls the settlers “holendrami”. The charter, however, lacks data specifying the legal position of the sołtys, and likewise lacks provisions on communal self-government. From the later information it appears that the settlement possessed an organisation proper not to the Olęder settlements but to the settlements founded at that time under German law (the so-called sołtys villages). Namely, the sołtys was hereditary, privileged as regards the manorial obligations, and sat under a separate law. In the period of the regulation (1832) we have here already 3 hereditary sołtyses, 2 privileged innkeepers,

[^18-1]: Sources: MP — Holendry Nadziejewskie; MG — absent; MGd — Vorw. Nadzielewskie; M 1821 — Col. Nadziejewski; O 83 XXII, fol. 300 — “The folwark that is called Nadzieiewskie belongs to an Olęder, lying in the forest”; Verz/1800 — absent; GI* — Nadziewska, folw.; Verz/1821, no. 278, Nadziejewski, hol. d. 10; Verz/1845, no. 462 — Nadziejewskie, Colonie, d. 11. As appears from the sources, in the Polish period there existed only the folwark; the proper settlement arose later.
[^18-2]: AW. Steinke, B. 942–3.
[^18-3]: Tp. 89, 90 — Szóstaki in the forest; 1790 — 2 hearths. This settlement, belonging to the parish of Biezdrowo, numbered 2 contract peasants. One of them bore the surname Szóstak, whence the name of the settlement. The marking “Holl.” on MG refers to the neighbouring locality also called Szóstaki, which arose in 1800 but already in the parish of Kwilcz, consisting of a forest lodge and a plot of forest. Contrary to the terminology of the sources, this was not an Olęder settlement. For it consisted of 3 farmers, sitting under individual charters and paying individually assigned obligations (AGH court of Pniewy, Turowo estate, vol. I, fol. 189). 5 The following localities with mixed population figure in the list of settlements: Daleszyno, Jabłonna Nowa, Maryanowo, Rakoniewice (Kościan county) and Popowo (Poznań county).
[^18-4]: An unverified copy of a charter, originating probably already in the XIX century (AW. Jędrzejewo, B. 1).

[p. 19]

further[^19-1] privileged peasants, 10 privileged half-peasants and one smallholder. A social composition of this kind is not met with in the Olęder settlements. Either, therefore, the name “holendrzy” was used in the location charter in a different sense — which is the most probable, especially as the charter speaks of “holendrs of German origin” [“holendrów niemieckiego pochodzenia”] — or else the settlement transformed itself gradually from its initial Olęder one into a settlement of a different character. In any case, at the time of the partitions Jędrzejewo cannot be classed among the Olęder settlements.

Finally the question arises whether there existed Olęder settlements that did not enter the enumeration of localities of 1789 — either because by that time they had ceased to exist, or else because they had been founded after 1789. To the first category belongs the settlement of Esterpole in the parish of Brodnica. It was located by Kasper Cerekwicki by a charter[^19-2] of April 1781 1. Three years later his estate was bought by Józef Wybicki, who did not confirm the charter issued by the previous owner, did not permit the Olęders to settle permanently, and when they left the village he turned the lands abandoned by them into a folwark, which he named Esterpole after the Christian name of his wife Estera née Kowalska (the village had previously borne the name Przylepki). Even before the first partition the Murzynowskie Ol., located by a charter of 1 November 1613 in the Międzyrzecz starostwo,[^19-3] had been detached from the voivodeship of Poznań. The king of Prussia unlawfully seized the village of Murzynowo with the said Olęders in 1770 and annexed it to the Marchia; and when several years of efforts to recover the seized area proved fruitless, and on account of internal weakness armed repossession could not be thought of, the Sejm of 1782 confirmed the cession of the seized villages to Prussia (Rel. Posn. no. 599, 1782, fol. 450). To the second category belong the settlements: 1. Dzięczyna in the parish of Poniec[^19-4]; 2. Miłostowo in the parish of Kwilcz.

[^19-1]: Rel. Cost. no. 214, 1781, fol. 300.
[^19-2]: AW. A. 1–4.
[^19-3]: The source information presents itself here as follows: MP — Holendry Dzięczyńskie; MG — Dzienczyn Holl.; MGd. — Dzienczyn KI (ein); TV — Dzięczyna, Colonisten, d. 4; AGH court of Bojanowo — Dzięczyna, Colonie; Verz/1845 — Dzięczyn, Kolonie, d. 6. On account of the absence of all the sources of the Polish period except MP, one must assume that this settlement arose after 1789 and before 1791 (the year in which MP was compiled). Whether it had at all the character of an Olęder settlement seems doubtful, the more so as in the German period it is called a “colony”.
[^19-4]: Before 1791 Miłostowo consisted of a contract village and a folwark. In that year Krzysztof Unrug sold to the Miłostowo commune both the lands it had hitherto occupied and the folwark fields, on the terms of perpetual lease, reckoning 100 Dutch ducats per huba, and granted to it, by a charter of 10 June 1791 (Rel. Posn. no. 641, 1792, fol. 450), the rights and the organisation of the Olęder settlements. We have here thus one of the cases in which a hitherto corvée village was converted into an Olęder one without any change in its personal composition.

[p. 20]

The names of the settlements. All the names of the Olęder settlements can, according to their immediate source, be divided into two groups: the first group consists of names inherited from settlements existing previously.[^20-1] This was the case when an Olęder settlement was located on the site of a depopulated village?. In cases where Olęder rights were granted to[^20-2] a village that had existed earlier, the previous name of the settlement was kept.[^20-3][^20-4]

In the second group, comprising the names of new settlements, one can in turn distinguish: a) names derived from the topographical names of the terrain on which the settlements were located, e.g. Dziewczastruga (previously a stream), Orłowiec, Wymyślanka (previously a wood of that same name) 5; b) leasehold names with the addition “Olędry”, formed from the localities on whose ground the settlements were founded$; c) characteristic names, e.g.[^20-5] Wielkie Olędry, Nowe Olędry, Pólsko-Czeskie, Niemiecko-Czeskie Olędry.

All the new names were either bestowed on the settlements by the owner — which, moreover, happened exceedingly rarely 7 — or they arose[^20-6]

[^20-1]: The question of the etymology of these names is a question of the etymology of names in general, and as such does not fall within the frame of the present study. A fair amount of material in this field,
relating to the former voivodeship of Poznań, can be found in the works
of Fr. Kozierowski,
[^20-2]: In all these cases, in the table of settlements (Appendix I), in the column “the character of the terrain before the founding of the settlement on it”, there is given: “village” or “folwark”.
[^20-3]: Such cases are marked in the appropriate column of Appendix I with the annotation: “the same village and inhabitants”.
[^20-4]: Sometimes it is difficult to establish whether a given settlement is really the first settlement at all on this site. This distinction is of course of no significance for the present considerations if the area on which a village had once existed showed no traces of it at the moment when the Olęder settlement was founded.
[^20-5]: AW. Dziewczastruga, B. 1028; AW. Orłowiec, A. 1; Rel. Posn. no. 641, 1792, fol. 260, Wymyślanka.
[^20-6]: A considerable majority of the Olęder settlements bore such names. The following qualification must, however, be made here: the term “Olędry” is used in the sources beside the proper name of a settlement in a twofold sense — to denote the character of the locality (like the terms: folwark, colony, etc.) and as a component part of the settlement's name. On account of the old orthography, however (the word “Olędry” always written with a capital letter), no distinction can be made here. In giving the term “Olędry” with the names of settlements we were guided by considerations of a purely external kind; we gave it in those cases in which it figured beside the name of the village in the majority of the sources. 7 Even in those few cases in which the owner assigned a name to the settlement in the location charter, a different name often later prevailed. The cases known to us of the bestowal of a name by the owner relate to the following settlements: Cegielskie Ol. (in the charter the name Trzcielskie Ol. was given), Gębickie Ol. (in the charter Mielęcin), Marienwald (Marigenwalde), Nowe Ol. (part of Krobielewo, part Baba), Róża Ol. (Rosenthal), Ujskie Ol. (Olędrowo). Further, names such as Marianowo (in the parish of Zbąszyń and in the parish of Wieleń), Filipowo, Folsztyn, Herbardowo, Józefowo, Krystynka, Wyssogotowo, Zakrzewo undoubtedly took their origin from the owner.

[p. 21]

of themselves. That the settlers had an influence on the formation of the name of a settlement can be surmised only in sporadic cases. Names that come into question here are: Niemiecko-Czeskie and Polsko-Czeskie, and probably also the Albertowskie and Amtskasnerowskie Ol. It is possible that the last two names became established from the surnames of the locators or of the sołtyses of the villages, but this supposition cannot be established with complete certainty. Frequently the same localities appear under two or more names; in such[^21-1] cases we adopted the name that recurs most frequently in the sources. If the settlements bore two names at the same time, both were given in the enumeration (e.g. Błonie Ol., or Źdżar, etc.).

[^21-1]: prepared for the Historical [Atlas] with the collaboration of Dr A. Tomaszewski, docent of Slavonic philology of the University of Poznań.

[p. 22]

CHAPTER II Demographic conditions in the Olęder settlements

A. The numbers of the Olęders

The considerable number of Olęder settlements suggests the supposition that the Olęders constituted a substantial proportion of the population of the former voivodeship of Poznań. For that reason the detailed examination of the demographic structure of these settlements must be regarded as a matter of importance. Owing to the lack of sources it is not possible to present the changes in the size of the Olęder population over the whole period of the colonisation's existence; the source data do, however, make it possible to carry out a fairly exact calculation of this population in the period of the partitions 1. In view of the greatest abundance of the source tradition we shall adopt the year 1789 as the moment of the calculation. For Kościan county we possess from this period the following statistical materials: I. the “General table of the population” (Tol.), compiled by the Tax Commission of Kościan county on the basis of an ordinance of the Crown Fiscal Commission of 1789; 2. the “General table of hearths” (Tod. 89), compiled[^22-1][^22-2] by the same commission; 3. the Tariff of hearths and of the hearth tax for the years 1789 and 90 (Tp. 89, 90). The first table does not include the szlachta and the clergy, and moreover contains a considerable number of numerical errors and omissions. It is likewise, just like the second table, incomplete territorially. The third table, however, is compiled more exactly, and for that reason, despite certain gaps ?, it inspires considerably more confidence. The hearths are shown in it in two columns: in the first for 1789 on the basis of the declarations sworn by the owners, in the second for 1790 on the basis of the official lustration. The numerical differences between the two columns are small; the more important point is that in the tariff of 1789

[^22-1]: The population of the whole of Poland in this period was calculated in rough approximation by Korzon (Ostatnie lata panowania Stan. Augusta, vol. I, p. 50–323). The summarised results obtained there cannot, however, be utilised for calculating the population of the individual voivodeships, still less for calculating the individual strata of that population.
[^22-2]: cf. Rybarski, Skarbowość Polski, p. 241.

[p. 23]

hearth are given in it most often collectively (for neighbouring localities, for whole estates), whereas the tariff of 1790 gives them usually for each settlement separately. As regards the number of hearths in the individual settlements, Tod. 89 and Tp. 89 are in complete agreement with each other. It must further be noted that the tables of hearths cover all strata of the population. In the appropriate columns of the list of settlements (Appendix I) there are set out in turn: I. the number of hearths of the individual Olęder settlements according to Tp. 89 (an asterisk marks those hearths which are given collectively in Tp. 89; they were then usually estimated according to Tp. 90, and in the case of mixed settlements on the basis of the number of Olęder farmers sitting in the settlement); 2. the number of hearths for settlements not covered by Tp. 89;[^23-1] 3. the population of the settlements according to Tol. Omitted were the figures given in Tol. for the Jaromierskie Nowe and Stare Ol. and for Ujście Stare, as evidently incomplete. On summation we obtain for the settlements covered by Tol.: 2144 hearths and 12967 souls. One hearth thus corresponds to 6.05 persons. By this average we calculate the population of the settlements for which Tol. is lacking: 334 hearths*? × 6.05 = 2020 souls.[^23-2] The Olęder population of Kościan county in 1789 amounted in all, therefore, to 14,987, in round figures 15,000 persons. It is harder to answer the question what proportion of the population of Kościan county the Olęder population constituted. For that purpose one would have to calculate the entire population of the county. Such calculations, however, go beyond the frame of the present study. In order to answer the question posed above at least in part, we shall attempt — leaving aside the question of the urban population — to calculate the ratio of the Olęders to the remaining rural population. According to Tol. the population of the rural settlements of Kościan county was 97,557 persons.[^23-3] Tod. 89 gives for these same settlements 15,831 hearths. In the total population according to Tol. the szlachta and the clergy are missing.[^23-4] Not having any more exact data for the calculation of these two strata of the population, we shall adopt as the starting point the calculations of Holsche 5[^23-5] for the Poznań department of 1800. According to

[^23-1]: For the Świętne Ol. on the basis of Tp. 90; the remaining settlements figure in Tp. 89 for Poznań county.
[^23-2]: After adding the previous figure of 2144 hearths we obtain the total sum of hearths for Kościan county shown in the columns of Appendix I, namely 2478 Olęder hearths.
[^23-3]: This is the summa summarum figure [grand total] of 97,788 persons shown in Tol., reduced by the incomplete figures of the population of the Jaromierskie Nowe and Stare Ol. and of Ujście Stare, together 231 persons.
[^23-4]: i.e. the summa summarum of 24,667 hearths shown in Tod. 89, less the 8,520 urban hearths (according to Tp. 89; in Tod. 89 the rural and the urban hearths are given together), less the 225 hearths given in Tod. 89 for which no population is shown in Tol., and finally less the 82 hearths of the settlements mentioned in the previous note.
[^23-5]: Geographie und Statistik, vol. II, p. 448 ff. Korzon (Ostatnie lata panowania Stan. Augusta, vol. I, p. 72) places great confidence in Holsche's statistics.

[p. 24]

these calculations, the szlachta and the clergy in the Poznań department constituted about 2% of the population. This figure is very small, which is to be explained at least in part by the fact that in 1789–1800 the peasant population was increasing at a faster rate than the remaining population. Presumably, therefore, the most fitting course will be to assume that the szlachta and the clergy in 1789 amounted to 3% of the total rural population, that is, in round figures, 3,000 persons. If we add this sum to the figure of 97.5557 souls of the remaining rural population, we obtain 100,557 souls. If we then deduct from this sum the population and hearths of the Olęder settlements calculated previously, there will fall to the non-Olęder rural population 85,570 persons and 13,353 hearths. Hence, as the average per one rural hearth (without taking the Olęder hearths into account), we obtain the figure of 6.41 souls. As is known, however, Tod. 89, on the basis of which we carried out the above calculations, is territorially incomplete. The actual number of rural hearths for Kościan county, after deducting the Olęder hearths, is 13,915 [sic] [sic] hearths.[^24-1] Tod. 89 therefore lacks 552 hearths. Multiplying them by the coefficient of 6.41 we obtain 3,538 souls. The whole rural population of Kościan county will thus amount to 104,095 persons. This is, however, a minimal figure, for the omissions in Tol. which could be established with regard to the Olęder settlements occurred undoubtedly 1 also with regard to the remaining settlements. This number must therefore be somewhat increased. In this way the total of the rural population of Kościan county will be expressed by the round figure of 105,000 persons.

For the former Poznań county we have far more modest source materials. They concern above all the part of the county that remained with Poland after the first partition. Here only the already-mentioned tariffs of hearths and of the hearth tax for the years 1789–1790 (Tp. 89, 90) come into consideration. Of statisticians giving directly the population of this area in 1789 there are none. By means of the hearth tariff of 1789 the population of the part of Poznań county remaining

[^24-1]: We arrive at this figure by way of the following calculation: Tp. 89 shows for Kościan county
    13,737 noble hearths
    2,227 clerical hearths
    339 royal hearths
    16,303 hearths
    less 17 hearths of the village of Orchowo, lying in Pyzdry county,
    16,286 hearths.
    There are added 92 Olęder hearths not given in Tp. 89,
    and 15 hearths of the village of Sowinice,
    together 16,393 hearths
    less 2,478 Olęder hearths.
    We obtain as a result 13,915 hearths.

[p. 25]

with Poland can be calculated approximately by way of estimation. As in the case of Kościan county, in the appropriate columns of the enumeration of settlements (Appendix I b) there have been entered: 1. the hearths of the individual settlements of the county on the basis of Tp. 89 or else 90 (marked with an asterisk); 2. the hearths of the settlements for which Tp. 89 or else 90 is lacking; 3. the hearths of the settlements seized by Prussia in the first partition, according to Goldbeck or the Prussian classifications. The number of hearths shown in the first two columns is 2,254. After multiplying this number by the number of persons falling in Kościan county to 1 Olęder hearth (i.e. 6.05), the sum of the Olęder population in the area of Poznań county remaining with Poland will be expressed by the figure of 13,637 souls.

For the sake of comparison we shall estimate by the same method the remaining rural population of this part of the county. The total number of non-Olęder rural hearths here amounts to 12,462.[^25-1] Applying the average number of persons per 1 hearth (i.e. 6.41) calculated for Kościan county, the rural population without the Olęders will be expressed by the figure of 79,881 persons, and with the Olęders it makes 93,518 persons. In the Wschowa land there were no Olęder settlements. The rural population of the Wschowa land in the population table of 1789 (TW) was reckoned at 10,556 persons, and after adding the estimated 5% for the szlachta and the clergy we obtain roundly 10,900 persons. Wałcz county, where there were likewise no Olęder settlements, was, moreover, seized in its entirety by Prussia in the first partition.

The results of the calculations made so far can be summed up in the following table:

[^25-1]: i.e. those given in Tp. 89:
    10,703 noble hearths
    2,834 clerical hearths
    952 royal hearths
    14,489 hearths
    less 16 hearths counted twice in Tp. 89 (the Psarskie Ol.),
    less 84 hearths of the Sowinice estate belonging to Kościan,
    together 14,389 hearths.
    There are added 42 hearths of the parish of Iwno omitted in Tp. 89,
    together 14,431 hearths. As we see from the column of hearths not covered by Tp. 89, placed in Appendix I, this last source is incomplete for Poznań county. It must be supposed that the omissions in Tp. 89, with which we came up in calculating the Olęder hearths, occurred also in regard to the remaining rural hearths. Since, however, within the frame of the present study it is not possible to check the hearths of each individual locality of the county, we must rest content with increasing the non-Olęder rural hearths in the same proportion as for the Olęders, i.e. in the proportion 38 : 2216. To the previously given figure of 14431 hearths there will thus be added a further 247 hearths, which makes altogether 14678 hearths. After deducting the 2216 hearths falling to the Olęder settlements, we obtain finally 12,462 hearths.

[p. 26]

TABLE I

Rural population; including the Olęder population:

| County | Rural population in total | of which the Olęder population, in absolute figures | as a percentage |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Kościan | 105,000 | 14,987 | 14.3 |
| Poznań | 93,500 | 13,637 | 14.6 |
| Wschowa | 10,900 | — | — |
| **Voivodeship of Poznań** (within the borders after the first partition) | **209,400** | **28,624** | **13.7** |

The figures given include the population of all the settlements of Poznań county listed in Appendix I b, that is, also the settlements of transitional and of uncertain character. If we leave these settlements aside, there remain 2,109 Olęder hearths, and the population of the settlements taken into account will be 12,759 persons. The total sum of the rural population of the county (within the borders after the first partition) will rise to the figure of 93,569.[^26-1] The population of the Olęder settlements will then amount to 13.6% of that figure.

For the part of Poznań county taken by Prussia in the first partition we possess population lists of 1773, contained in the Prussian classification records, and a census of hearths published in 1789 by Goldbeck on the basis of materials of 1783 and later. On the whole the number of hearths in the Olęder settlements showed no tendency to abrupt changes; we shall therefore not depart far from reality,[^26-2] if we accept Goldbeck's data as depicting the state of 1789. The last column of Appendix I b shows 514 Olęder hearths beyond the cordon. After multiplying them by the coefficient of 6.05 established previously[^26-3] we obtain 3,122 persons. For lack of sufficient data, and because of the difficulty of determining the county affiliation of certain localities, it is not possible here to estimate the remaining rural population of this part of the county.

In the territory of Wielkopolska, apart from the counties of Kościan and Poznań, only the counties of Gniezno and Pyzdry had a more substantial number of Olęder settlements. Probably, therefore, for Wielkopolska as a whole the proportion of the Olęders to the total rural population will fall below 10%.

The sources have not preserved data for calculating the natural increase of the Olęder population. According to Tol., however, the non-Olęder families

[^26-1]: Because a larger number of persons falls per hearth of the non-Olęder population.
[^26-2]: This follows, for instance, from a comparison of the number of hearths in Tp. 72 [sic; TKS. 72] and in Tp. 89, 90, or in Kl. and G.
[^26-3]: This coefficient, calculated on the basis of the data of 1789, may possibly not correspond exactly to the state of 1773. Any difference due to fluctuations of the natural increase we have, however, no way of establishing or calculating.

[p. 27]

are considerably more numerous than the families of the Olęders; one may therefore conclude with a high degree of probability that the natural increase of the Olęder population was smaller than that of the remaining population.

B. The religious composition of the Olęder population

We possess no source statistics from which the religious composition of the population of the Olęder settlements could be reconstructed; certain general conclusions can be drawn only on the basis of the loose, chance mentions and data found in the sources. The question of the religious affiliation of the Olęders had already been discussed by German authors; a few, contrary to the obvious, tried to maintain that the whole Olęder population was Evangelical,1 but the majority admitted,[^27-1] that a certain percentage of the Olęders belonged to the Roman Catholic confession. The question therefore reduces to establishing what part of the population of the Olęder settlements was made up of Catholic settlers. The sources show that the population of the settlements founded in clerical estates on the territory of the Poznań voivodeship was always Catholic. In the Kościan county there existed 3 such settlements[^27-2] with a population of 401 souls. In the Poznań county 17 settlements belonged to church property.[^27-3] Their population, on estimation from the number of hearths, will come to 1,252 persons. From chance mentions we learn that in the Kościan county alone, besides the foregoing, wholly Catholic were Ol. Charłub,[^27-4] Ol. Chromiec,[^27-5] Ol. Kiączyńskie 6, Ol. Radoszkowskie 7, Ol. Terespotockie,[^27-6] Ol. Troszczyńskie,[^27-7][^27-8] Ol. Włościejewskie 19 and in part Ol. Łęskie l. Similar[^27-9][^27-10][^27-11]

[^27-1]: Rhode, Das Nationalitiitenverhiltnis in Westpreussen und Posen, p. 59: "Ihrem Bekenntnisstande nach waren die Hollanderdóorfer ganz evangelisch" [As regards their confession the Holländer villages were entirely Evangelical].
[^27-2]: Oberskie Stare and Nowe, and Wilcz Olęders.
[^27-3]: Barcinek, Białokoskie Ol., Czapury, Czerwonak Ol., Główno Ol., Janikowo, Kliny Ol., Krzyżewniki, Łoskońskie Stare i Nowe Ol., Marlewo, Miękowskie Ol., Nowosileńskie Ol., Pącheńskie Ol., Suchylas, Trojanowskie Ol., Wiorek.
[^27-4]: Rel. Cost. no. 223, fol. 187, enrolment of the charter of 1877: "Since on the inherited forest in my Mchowo estates we have been pleased to settle Olęders who are Catholics alone... such a right they take...".
[^27-5]: VO 78: "Chromiec cum Nova Colonia, alias Holendry, Catholica" [Chromiec with the New Colony, alias Holendry, Catholic].
[^27-6]: AW. A. 1, charter of 1778: "Every colonist shall have the power to sell, to cede, to give, to bestow on whom he wishes his lands, except to a Dissident".
[^27-7]: As a Catholic parish community the Radoszkowskie Olęders were still paying meszne in 1823 to the Catholic parish priest in Książ (AGH court of Śrem, the Książ estate, vol. of 1823, fol. 144).
[^27-8]: According to VO 87 (14 March 1778) the inhabitants were Catholics.
[^27-9]: As above.
[^27-10]: Rel. Cost. no. 217, fol. 78, enrolment of the charter of 1783: "Since on the forest and woods belonging to my hereditary estates of Włościejewki I settle Olęders who are Catholics alone...".
[^27-11]: According to O 83 XIX the greater part of the inhabitants was Evangelical. There must therefore have been Catholics as well.

[p. 28]

Similar mentions we meet also in relation to some settlements of the Poznań county.[^28-1] There were undoubtedly more settlements of this kind than follows from the chance, scanty records of the sources; there must also have been numerous intermediate forms, i.e. settlements with a population mixed in respect of religion. The Evangelical confession dominated above all in the northern part of the Poznań county. The Prussian classification files give, among the Olęder settlements, [them] as purely Evangelical (Lutheran). On the other hand, the further the settlement reached to the south and to the east, the stronger in it was the share of the Catholic population. Important conclusions concerning the religious relations of the Olęder settlements are suggested by the social stratification of the population of the settlements. As follows from Table IV, about 36% of the population consisted of landless people, who for settling in the settlement needed neither an individually expressed consent of the lord nor material means for redeeming themselves and establishing a household. On the other hand, on[^28-2] the basis of settler registers sometimes inserted in charters, and of inspections of one and the same settlement carried out at different times, it can be established that the settlers did not bring with them the needed contingent of hired labour forces, but hired them as the farm grew and the demand for labour increased. It is most probable that the Olęders sought such labour forces in the nearest neighbourhood. This met with no obstacles of a legal character, owing to the fact that in the villages of Wielkopolska there existed a number of labourers who were not subject (the free, contract workers), whom the Tol. even groups[^28-3] into the categories: "the free, occupied with work" and "beggars". Undoubtedly their ranks were also powerfully swelled by runaway subjects. Hence it must be accepted that, especially in settlements located among Polish villages, the poorer, landless population was Polish and Catholic. This conclusion is confirmed by contemporary sources1 and later Prussian returns of population. The fact that, for example, in the census of 1843 a considerable percentage of the Catholic population is shown in almost all the Olęder settlements.8 cannot at all be explained by assertions about an alleged polonisation of the German population, and in no case can there be any question here of a change of religion. It is equally difficult here

[^28-1]: The location charter for Ostrówek provides that beside the sołtys the community was to appoint 2 ławniks, of whom 1 was always to be a Catholic, even if there were no Catholic at all in the village besides him. In 1773 there were 4 of them.
[^28-2]: Thus, for example, the parish priest of Vrzcianki writes literally in a petition to the Prussian minister of finance of 1772: "mein Unterhalt in Schónlanke grósstencheils auf die Juribus Stolac beruhet, wcil das Messkorn geringe, und das sandigte Land nichts zu bedeuten, und ich so well fur die Dissidenten, als meine Religions-Verwandite arbeiten muss, indem diec Lutheraner gróss-tentheils Catholisch Gesinde haben" [my subsistence in Schönlanke rests for the most part on the Juribus Stolac, since the mass-corn is small and the sandy land means nothing, and I must work both for the Dissidents and for my co-religionists, seeing that the Lutherans have mostly Catholic servants] (Szk. fol. 40).
[^28-3]: Verzeichniss sammtlicher Ortschaften des Regierungs-Bezirks Posen, Poznań 1845,

[p. 29]

speak of an intermingling of the population, for it must have been quite exceptional that an Evangelical hired man worked for a Catholic employer, considering that such a man was as a rule a Pole.[^29-1] Taking as a point of departure the number of the population of the Catholic settlements enumerated above, and both groups of "free labourers" shown for the Olęder settlements in the Tol., and taking further into account the other reservations given above, we arrive at the statement that the Catholic Olęder population on the territory discussed by us in the period of the partitions amounted to about 40% of the total inhabitants of the settlements.

C. The nationality of the Olęder population

Determining the ethnic affiliation of the Olęders presents very great difficulties. Direct source information in this respect is almost entirely lacking.[^29-2] For contemporaries this question had no significance whatever in view of the complete equality of rights enjoyed by both nationalities. Indirect indications are supplied to us by the surnames of settlers given from time to time.

The national character of the Olęder settlement on the territory of the former Poznań voivodeship was not uniform throughout the whole duration of the colonization. It cannot admit of any doubt that the first settlers were authentic Hollenders.[^29-3] They were settled in the oldest settlement on the territory that interests us, namely in the Olęders of Ujście (Olędrowie), and in the four oldest settlements of the Wieleń estate: Folsztyn, Herbardowo, Marianowo and Nowe Dwory. We possess in this respect a series of certain, irrefutable source data:

I. All the settlements mentioned are located on the waterlogged Noteć lowland. As in Prussia, the Holendrzy were brought in for the purpose of draining the lands assigned to them.

[^29-1]: On the other hand there did occur an infiltration of Catholic farmers into Evangelical settlements and conversely. Hence in villages which in the pre-partition period had been purely Catholic, the census of 1843 revealed a considerable number of Evangelicals.
[^29-2]: Quite exceptionally such a source note is found (Rel. Cost. no. 208, 1775, fol. 800, Brodnickie Ol.): "On these Holendry belonging to Brodnica the first building is that of Jan Gruntowski... Item Kazimierz Niemicc,... Jan Bajer... innkeeper Niemicc... Kazimierz Polak... Jędrzey Niemicc... Marcin Niemicc... Marcin Niemicc...".
[^29-3]: This position was taken by Schmidt and Grossert (Evangelium und Dcutschtum, p. 18), and lately also by Schmitz and Reinhard. A position entirely opposite was taken by P. v. Gebhardt (Parochia Kottnensis, p. XI). He maintains that the first Olęders were Germans coming from the March, but he does not justify his conclusions in any detail. Gebhardt's opinion is supported by Schulz W. (Die zwette deutsche Ostsiedlung).

[p. 30]

2. As follows from the surviving charters for Ol. Ujskie[^30-1] and Ol. Nowe Dwory,[^30-2] the Holendrzy, as in Prussia, receive their lands in perpetual lease. / 3. Above, on p. 12, there has already been demonstrated the connection through the person of the owner that exists between the Wieleń settlements and the Holendry settlements in the Świecie starostwo.

4. In the two charters mentioned above are given the Christian names and surnames of the settlers.[^30-3] The majority of the names and surnames have a Flemish-Dutch sound. 5. Both charters mentioned call the settlers "Olęders" in the sense of their nationality, and the later charters of 1677 for the Wieleń Olęders use the designation: "honourable men of the Olęder nation". The earliest surviving inventories of the Wieleń estate also call the settlers Olęders,[^30-4] setting them in opposition to the German settlers.[^30-5]

6. On the first page of the community book of Folsztyn we find the following note: "Im Jahr 1637 ist die Kirch im Folsteyndorff gebawet. Es haben die deutschen burger auh helfen, Holtz zufuhren, auh den Zimer leuth gelohnet, Das sie mit den holendern gleich wart dor zu haben. Den Form auff der Kirch haben die holander allein bawet. Die Klock haben die holander allein gebaufft vor hundert und zwanzieg Reichstaller. Das Neu biet werck vumb die Kirch haben die holander allein bauven lassen. Dem Zimer man haben wier gegeben 18 Rechstaler drey tohn bier" [In the year sixteen hundred and thirty-seven the church in Folsteyndorff was built. The German burghers also helped, bringing timber and paying the carpenters, so that they should be on an equal footing with the Holendrzy in that matter. The frame of the church the Holendrzy built alone. The bell the Holendrzy alone paid for, for one hundred and twenty Reichstaler. The new pew work around the church they had the Holendrzy build alone. To the carpenter we gave eighteen Reichstaler and three tons of beer]. True, further on the year 1681 is given as the date of the founding of the book; nevertheless the above note must have been entered shortly after 1637, or copied from an older entry, for otherwise the details given there would no longer have been known. This mention testifies in an incontestable way to the nationality of the settlers.

The kinship of language and the community of religion must have led to the complete germanisation of the Holendrzy. There also contributed to this the settlers' lack of any stronger attachment to the land; they melted away

[^30-1]: Of 16 April 1597 (Inscr. Valc. no. 85, 1663–4, fol. 402).
[^30-2]: Of 21 February 1601 (Rel. Posn. no. 140, 1601–2, fol. 659).
[^30-3]: Ujskie Ol.: Jan Borth, Marcin Schueneke, Michał Boleman, Krystian Paul, Joachim Bartz, Joachim Schmiket, Jan Werner, Andrzej Dumken, Jan Elwig. Nowe Dwory Ol.: Barthlomicy Unswaldt, Hanus Renik, Andreas Brozy, Krystow Olemborg, Peter Barkaw, Peter Wanczlaw, Henrik Carstan, Jakub Hanik, Michael Hoffman, Philyp Bem (or Bein). A Polish transcription.
[^30-4]: The inventory of 1630 (Rel. Posn. no. 157, 1632, fol. 169–178) and that of 1653 (Rel. Posn. no. 179, 1654, fol. 718–779).
[^30-5]: The inventory of 1653 distinguishes Polish, German (or mixed) villages, and "Olęder" ones.

[p. 31]

quickly among the incoming German population.1 Whether some of the later settlements were also settled by Holendrzy is difficult to establish. The charter for Ol. Róża of 1624 names the following settlers: Jachim Fresa, Jan Pantka, Jachim Nazbant. They too were probably Holendrzy, as is suggested by the forename Joachim, popular among the Holendrzy and hardly met with at all in the sources used here at surnames that are undoubtedly German. By the Holendrzy, at least in part, there were probably also settled the later lowland settlements on the Warta. On the other hand it seems a certain thing that the settlements

located in forests, on clearances, were colonised in the seventeenth century with the help of German settlers. In relation to these settlements, just as to the later ones originating from the beginning of the eighteenth century, the sources give almost no surnames of Polish sound. Already by the middle of the eighteenth century, however, we meet not only new settlements in which the presence of the Polish element can be established beyond doubt, but Poles also appear[^31-1] in those settlements in which they had not been before. In the period of the partitions it is already difficult to find a settlement in which not one of the inhabitants bore a Polish surname. The national relations of the Olęder settlements in this period have repeatedly been discussed in the hitherto existing literature. German scholarship mostly identified the Olęders with the Germans,[^31-2] and only a few authors noted that there also existed Polish Olęders.[^31-3] W. Maas, who has lately occupied himself with this question, expresses the supposition that they made up up to 25% of the total Olęder population.[^31-4]

[^31-1]: In the list of the population of Ol. Nowe Dwory of 1653 there figures only a single surname that was named in the charter of 1601.
[^31-2]: Thus presented the matter Holsche (Der Neizdistrikt, p. 223), and Schmidt (Geschichte, p. 348 ff.). Particularly interesting in this respect is the work of Schmidt. The author, although in the introduction he assures us of his objectivity, subsequently passed over in silence in the further course of the work the mentions of Polish Olęders found in the previously published works of Beheim-Schwarzbach and Fr. Guradze, identifying all the Olęders with the Germans. Moreover he even adduced source information that did not exist. According to him, for example, the colonies of Gross and Klein Lubs, lying in the neighbourhood of Wieleń, already figure in the inventory of the Wieleń estate of 1653 as German settlements, whereas in reality these settlements were founded a good 130 years later, already after the country had been taken over by Prussia. Nor did Kótschke make use of the results of earlier research; in the Geschichte der Ostdeutschen Kolonisation published jointly with Lbert, p. 151, he lays down briefly: "holender: deutscher Kolonist" [the Holender: a German colonist]. On the same position stood also Warschauer (Geschichte, p. 103), Liick (Deutsche Aufbaukrifte), and Schulz (Die zweite deutsche Ostsiedlung, p. 74).
[^31-3]: For the participation of the Polish nationality in the work of Olęder colonization, besides the already mentioned Beh.-Schwarzbach (who in his work Der Netzedistrikt, p. 122, even cited 4 settlements in the Noteć region inhabited exclusively by Poles) and Fr. Guradze, Martiny also declared himself (Die Formen der Siedlungen, p. 31), and among Polish authors Baranowski (Wsie holenderskie).
[^31-4]: Die Posener Haulindereien.

[p. 32]

How did the matter present itself in reality? For the most part Polish was the population of the settlements founded in church estates, and of the noble settlements mentioned in the preceding section, settled by Catholics.[^32-1] In many other settlements alongside Evangelical Germans sat Catholic Poles.[^32-2]. Nor were there lacking settlements in which part of the population was recruited from Poles, Evangelicals.[^32-3] The German

[^32-1]: Catholic Germans lived in the clerical villages of Czapury and Wiorek. In relation to some other settlements we have the following data: In Janikowo, according to the court records of 1834 (AW. Kliny, C. 3, fol. 39 ff.), there lived the farmers: Cholewa, Nowak, Hauska Walenty, Mańka Bartłomiej, Włodarczak Antoni, Świątek Walenty, Węclewski Franciszek, Krzypiński, Karpiński; Barcinek (AW. CG. I, 1795): "Emphyteutische frey Bauern: Christoph Hintz, Lorenz Mantey, Andr. Sobiejewski, Hirth Woitek Pies, Schifer Knecht Joseph" [emphyteutic free farmers: Christoph Hintz, Lorenz Mantey, Andr. Sobiejewski, farmer Woitek Pies, shepherd servant Joseph]; Krzyżewniki (Kl. M.): 11 half-farmers, I innkeeper, | fisherman, 1 lodger, 5 smallholders, | herdsman, all the surnames Polish except one; Naramowskie Ol. (AGH court of Poznań, the Naramowice estate, vol. I, 1809, fol. 56): Joanna Dorota Knopka sells an Olęder farm to Jan Kamiński. The document is signed by Marcin Guziński, sołtys of the Naramowskie Olęders (fol. 59), Michał Dobroć and Marcin Fryć, ławniks; Czerwonak Ol.: in 1803 (AGH court of Poznań, the Czerwonak estate, vol. no. 12, fol. 11) Paweł Jastrzębkowski of Ol. Czerwonak sells a farm to Simon Laufeld. In 1810 (AGH vol. no. 11, fol. 2) Tomasz Przybylski sells a farm to Andrzej Ryske. The village of Chorzępowa, which in 1753 was transferred to Olęder law, was inhabited in 1759 by Poles alone (AM. Sieraków, C. 53, fol. 102). Polish inhabitants, if not in their entirety, at least in part, must also1 have been possessed by the other villages transferred to Olęder law without a change of their previous inhabitants. Thus, for example, the charter for Ostrów of 1789 is submitted for enrolment: "Honestus Christanus Rozna cultetus et Albertus Casimirus Biały scabinus de villa Ostrowo" [the honourable Christian Rozna, cultivator, and Albert Casimir Biały, ławnik, of the village of Ostrowo] (Rel. Cost. no. 220, 1787, fol. 371). Poles sat undoubtedly in the Polsko-Czeskie Olęders. Lück casts doubt on this (Deutsche Aufbaukrifte, p. 331, map), appealing to figures from the pre-war1 and the present period. It is not known to him, however, that in 1843 the population of the settlement consisted of 167 Catholics and 32 Evangelicals. Do the data he adduces, then, not rather testify to the germanisation of the settlement in the Prussian period?
[^32-2]: Such a mixed population was possessed, for example, by the Sroczewskie Olęders. In 1834 (AGH court of Śrem, the Książ estate, vol. I, fol. XVI) there lived here: Zimowski Mateusz, Kowalski Wincenty, Szatkowski Wojciech, Degner Eleonora, Hełmikowski Franciszek, Pihl Samuel, Pihl Gottlieb, Appelt Gottfried, Pihl Daniel, Adamski Szczepan, Hoeter Samuel, Binnert Ignacy, Barsch Ludwika, Starzebecker Christian, Floeter Renata, Rozdzielski Józef, Rozdzielska Marianna, Zimowski Krzysztof, Brzeziński Jakub, Kulmaj Daniel, Rosin Jan, Jankowska Katarzyna, Tomaszewski Franciszek, Floeter Beata. The classification records of Ostrówek of 1773 mention 4 Catholic farmers. Correspondingly we find there 4 Polish surnames: Jan Mateusz, Walenty Kępa (or Gęba; under the call number AW. Ostrówek A. I there is a charter of 1750 in the name of Jan Gąba), Michał Rodzoński, Jan Wojan.
[^32-3]: Of the Chrząstowskie Olęders VO 78 (19 August 1777) relates that they are inhabited by Dissidents, who had come to an agreement with the previous parish priest on the payment of 36 zł, and since the statute of 1768 they have paid nothing. (Cf. Vol. Leg. vol. VII, 570 1 ff.) Meanwhile in 1787 there was filed to the Kościan grod books in the name of the community the manifest "Antoni Taterka, sołtys of the Chrząstowskie Olęders", undoubtedly a Pole (Rel. Cost. no. 220, 1787, fol. 524 and 530). There was in the village, then, at least a majority of inhabitants who were Poles, if they were able to choose a sołtys from among themselves. Evangelical Poles are found also in Daleszyno. According to VO 78 (27 August 1777) the Olęders who settled there were Lutherans, and yet the inspection of Daleszyno of 1778 (AGH court of Gostyń, the Daleszyno estate, vol. no. 55, fol. 8) gives, among others, the surnames: Fryderyk Wróbel, Samuel Wróbel, Fryderyk Adam Kaźmierczak. These settlers even bear forenames then popular among the Evangelical Olęders. It was similar in Zaborowo Ol. According to a note in Rel. Cost. no. 216, 1783, fol. 32: "On these Holendry there are six farmers, that is Kazimierz Drelich the sołtys, Jan Sztybania, Michał Cierpiński, Szymon Cierpiński, Woyciech Kwaśniewski Ręczkoś, Jakub Holendrzy". The census of population of 1843 (Verz./1845, no. 1417) showed in the village of Zaborowo Evangelicals alone. Several dozen Evangelical Poles are still in Zaborowo today (cf. the census of 1921, p. 80).

[p. 33]

element was represented comparatively most strongly in the Olęder settlements of the northern part of the Poznań county. Yet even in the Prussian classification files for the settlements taken in the first partition Polish surnames occur, though usually given in a form so germanised that deciphering them presents very great difficulties.[^33-1] All these fragmentary source data require completion in two directions. They take insufficient account, or take no account at all, of the lower social strata of the settlements (the journeymen, the lodgers). The reasoning about the origin of these strata from the local population, carried out in discussing the religious relations of the settlements, applies in full to the present considerations. The percentage of Poles among the landless Olęder population was undoubtedly larger than the analogous percentage of Polish farmers.[^33-2] Further, the surname of an Olęder cannot always be regarded as an exponent of his nationality. It must be remembered that a certain percentage of the population of Wielkopolska could bear German surnames as a result of the assimilation processes accomplished in the Middle Ages, and also as a result of the later entering of mixed marriages. Secondly, some Olęders, especially if we have in mind Catholic Germans, still during the existence of the old

[^33-1]: For example in Ol. Wizany (Kat. II, fol. 454) there are enumerated the surnames: Michael Polentzke, Andreas Rosantzke (on the charter attached there Stanisław Różanek), Johann Sapick, the widow Rosankin. The same surnames in 1792 (ibid., fol. 487) are given in the following spelling: Michael Poliński, Andreas Rosanski, Johann Sopick or Suppick (fol. 488). In Ol. Kwiejce Nowe the classifications specify the surname Martin Gudera (Kl. XII 6 I, fol. 46), in Ol. Maryanowo — Andreas Ratcy (Kat. II, fol. 590), in the Stróżewskie Olęders — Andreas Pollak, Christian Radotzki (Kl. XII, 6 II, fol. 114), in Ol. Szklana Huta — five families of the surname Jachalski (Johann Jachalke, Jakob Jacholske, Matias Jachalke, Jachalke, Peter Jachalske). Schulz W. does not regard these surnames as Slavonic; he occupied himself in detail with the classification records (Die zweite deutsche Ostsiedlung, Quellenband). According to him all the Olęders were Germans, and all the surnames — German (cf. the review of this work in Roczniki Historyczne, XIV, 2, pp. 387–400).
[^33-2]: For example in the Stare Dymaczewskie Olęders in 1782 among the surnames of the farmers we meet no surname of Polish sound, while there are named: Józef Kluczyński tailor, Stanisław Piczański potter, further, given without surnames: a herdsman, a fisherman and a forester, possibly also Poles. Cf. also the note on p. 32. To the journeymen or lodgers there belonged presumably also "Józef Świątek of Łokacz", named in the Wieleń tailors' guild book under the year 1737 (Schulz, Die zweite deutsche Ostsiedlung, II, p. 61). prace Komisji Atlasu hist. Polski, z. 5 5:

[p. 34]

of the old Commonwealth they had become completely polonised, adopting the Polish language and culture. To count them in the period of the partitions among the German population is the same error as to count among the Holendrzy the inhabitants of those settlements that had originally been settled by Holendrzy. It must further be stressed that not only the non-Olęder population,1 but the Polish Olęders too[^34-1] possessed more numerous families than the German Olęders. A characteristic example in this respect is afforded by the Olęders of the Sieraków estate. In the purely Polish Ol. Chorzępowa there fell in 1759 to an Olęder family (we take account only of those in possession of land) 6.5 persons, while in the remaining Olęder settlements at the same time 5.1 souls fell to a family.[^34-2] A Polish surname in a settlement thus represented a greater number of persons than a German surname.

Pursuing the line of the considerations conducted on the number of the Catholic Olęders, and taking into account that the number of Catholic Germans among the Olęders could be somewhat greater than the number of Evangelical Poles, the number of Polish Olęders on the territory of the former Poznań voivodeship must be estimated at 35–38% of the total Olęder population. For the whole of Wielkopolska this ratio will turn out still more favourable to the Poles, for as can be inferred already from a cursory review of the archival material, the Polish element was most strongly represented in the settlements situated on the territory of the former Kalisz voivodeship.

D. The origin of the settlers and the causes of the colonization

The origin of the settlers stands in connexion with their nationality. 3,[^34-3] It is understandable that the fatherland of the first settlers, the Holendrzy, was the Netherlands. As for the Polish Olęders, the Catholic part was recruited from the local population, whereas the Evangelicals, who appeared most numerously in the settlements of the southern part of the Poznań voivodeship, in all probability came from neighbouring Silesia. Such names as the Polsko-Czeskie and Niemiecko-Czeskie Olęders likewise testify that a certain number of settlers arrived from the south (in this case from Bohemian Silesia or Moravia). The coming of the German Olęders likewise remained connected with religion. The Catholics came in all probability from the interior of the Empire, from Bavaria or Swabia. The settlements of Czapury and Wiorek[^34-4]

[^34-1]: Cf. pp. 23–24.
[^34-2]: AM. Sieraków, C. 53. The Prussian court councillor Holsche failed to notice this, writing in 1793: "Die polnische Nation vermindert sich von Jahr zu Jahr, so dass sie sich in einigen Generationen fast ganz verlieren wird" [The Polish nation diminishes from year to year, so that in a few generations it will almost entirely disappear] (Netzdistrikt, p. 64).
[^34-3]: This, of course, does not apply to settlements that had existed previously and were transferred to Olęder law without a change in the composition of the existing population.
[^34-4]: On emigration from these countries to Prussia writes Zimmermann (Fryderyk Wielki).

[p. 35]

were settled by colonists from the environs of Bemberg.3[^35-1] The Protestant Olęders, on the other hand, came above all from the Prussian border provinces, that is from Silesia,[^35-2] from the Neumark[^35-3] and from Pomerania.[^35-4] In the later settlements part of them came from the Olęders of other, earlier settlements,[^35-5] or were recruited from the townspeople of the surrounding towns.[^35-6]

The Olęder colonization was caused by the same factor that marked out the roads for many other mass movements of population, namely the difference in the conditions of life in the earlier fatherland and in the place marked out for emigration. This difference aroused the striving to improve the hitherto existing conditions of existence by way of emigration. Conditions of life, in the understanding of the time, meant above all the economic and religious situation. The better that situation presented itself in the country regarded as the goal of emigration, the stronger the intensity assumed by the movement of emigration. Naturally the vision of prosperity and wellbeing in an unknown country could also be created by skilful, appropriate advertising emanating from that country. That too could set emigration in motion. If, however, reality proved considerably humbler, emigration in the long run was unthinkable. The Olęder colonization, based in large measure on emigration from Germany, continued with unabated force right up to the partitions; this would not have been the case had the settlers not found in their new fatherland distinct advantages and an improvement in their condition.[^35-7] The very fact alone

[^35-1]: AW. Czapury, C. 4 (VII), 1793: "4 gross und 3 klein Hibner sind Bamberger" [four large and three small Hibners are Bambergers]. Cf. also Max Bór, Die "Bamberger" bei Posen, p. 308. The settlers belonged to the group of Bamberg emigrants who settled in the villages of the town of Poznań. The remaining "bambrzy", however, although they likewise received location charters (Bir, pp. 348–367), were settled on conditions essentially different from those of the Olęders. Here the community did not appear as a solidary whole; at times charters were even issued individually for particular settlers; there was no self-government developed as among the Olęders, and the sołtys was mostly appointed by the municipal organs and had the character of a municipal official. Cf. Rutkowski, Zagadnienie reformy rolnej, p. 24.
[^35-2]: According to Cieplucha Z. (Z przeszłości, p. 185), the Kawczyńskie Olęders came from Silesia.
[^35-3]: The charter for Ręcko (Rel. Cost. no. 167, 1732, fol. 204) speaks of "border people".
[^35-4]: This view also established itself in German literature. Cf. Beheim-Schwarzbach, Der Netzedistrikt, p. 127, Schmidt, Geschichte, p. 313, 329 ff., 391 ff.; Maas, Hauländereien, p. 210; Schulz, Die zweite deutsche Ostsiedlung, I, p. 47. The last-named demonstrates the kinship between the surnames of the settlers of the northern part of the Poznań county and the surnames of places in the Neumark and Western Pomerania.
[^35-5]: This was noted, among others, by Hockenbeck in relation to the Zużoły Olęders in the neighbourhood of Wągrowiec (Deutsche Ansiedler, pp. 178–181).
[^35-6]: The locator, and at the same time one of the first Olęders in the Proszkowskie Olęders, was Gottfried Scharf, a burgher of Rakoniewice (AW. Proszkowo, A. 1).
[^35-7]: See further, p. 110.

[p. 36]

in itself settles the question to what extent the inflow of the settler Olęders was the result of recruiting action on the part of the Polish lords.[^36-1] The source material also supplies the explanation. Of the more than 140 location charters quoted in Appendix I, only in 28 cases do we meet mentions that in the bringing in of the Olęders a locator co-operated, and here the lord could conceivably indirectly contribute to the bringing in of settlers by promising the locator advantages for the colonizing of the settlement. In no charter is there the slightest trace of a mention of the arrival of settlers as a result of a previous agreement with the lord or of any propaganda action on his part; instead there often recurs the phrase, which we shall quote here from the charter for Ol. Chromiec: "Since certain Holendrzy have undertaken to clear and to break ground for themselves in the Chromiec ground and forests, and have petitioned for this, I, inclining to their petition..." etc.[^36-2] The decisive reason, therefore, for the wanderings of the German Olęders to Poland was the hard conditions of life in their own country.[^36-3] There can be no doubt that for a subject peasant of the March the prospect of existence abroad in the character of a free man must have presented a strong incentive to emigration. Confessional differences did not hinder immigration, for a considerable part of the szlachta conducting the colonization action was Protestant.[^36-4] It seems, however, rather doubtful whether the settlers were induced to leave their country, among other things, by motives of a psychological, emotional character, expressing themselves in a weaker attachment to the native soil, or in an innate inclination to wanderings.[^36-5]

[^36-1]: German literature tries to demonstrate that the German settlers were drawn to Poland solely by the Polish lords. Special weight is attached to the advertising prospectuses printed by certain owners interested in settlement (Reklameblätter). These works serve mostly propagandist, not scholarly purposes. Let us mention here, for instance, the work: Wiesec Hans R., Uns rief Polen! Deutsches Schicksal an Weichsel und Warthe, Leipzig 1937.
[^36-2]: At times such a company of settlers was no longer a blessing but a plague for the neighbourhood, if, having destroyed a stretch of forest, it did not reach an agreement with the lord, as a result of which it left the forest in a state of devastation and began clearing in another estate. Cf. Holsche, Geographie u. Statistik, vol. II, p. 493.
[^36-3]: Cf. Rutkowski, Poddaństwo włościan, p. 57 ff., and Schmidt, Geschichte, p. 325 ff. Most instructive in this respect is the work: Horst-Gotthard Ost, Die zweite deutsche Ostsiedlung im Drage- und Küddow-Gebiet, Leipzig 1939. The author demonstrates in an entirely convincing manner that the flight to Poland from the Neumark was caused by economic oppression1 and the expropriation of the peasants (Bauernlegen).
[^36-4]: Cf. Beheim-Schwarzbach, Hohenzollernsche Kolonisationen, p. 447 ff.; Schmitt F. W. F., Geschichte des Deutsch-Krone Kreises, p. 140; Maas, Die Entstehung, p. 86.
[^36-5]: Such, it seems, is the opinion of Lepucki H. in relation to the German colonists in Galicia (Działalność kolonizacyjna Marii Teresy i Józefa II w Galicji, 1772–1790, Lwów 1938, p. 152).

[p. 37]

CHAPTER III

The legal relations of the Olęder settlements

A. The Olęders and state law

There were no legal norms concerning the Olęders specifically, issued by the public authorities, in Poland before the partitions. Only the tax universały of the times of Stefan Batory and Sigismund III, as it were, take note of the existence of Olęder settlement, assessing taxes also on "pasture włókas, which the Olęders and others lease for pasture".1 This concerned in the first place the Holendrzy in Royal Prussia, who, sitting on lowland lands, carried on livestock farming on a broad scale. Likewise in the scope of other public-law duties the same obligations weighed on the Olęders as on the remaining peasant stratum. There remains to be decided in turn the question to what extent[^37-1] the Olęders had capacity for legal acts, that is, whether they had the right of appearing before the public court against the lord and third persons, and the right of appeal from the lords' judgments. This matter takes a twofold shape, depending on whether we are dealing with settlements founded in the royal estates or in noble or clerical estates. In the royal estates the Olęders, like other peasants, always had the right of seeking justice from the king. Their affairs, whenever damage to the royal estate came into consideration, were attended to by the crown instigator, and complaints against the possessors of the royal estates, or appeals from their judgments, were heard by the referendary court. Charters issued to the Olęders by the starostas were confirmed by the king, thereby guaranteeing their force and effect. Baranowski, who studied the records of the referendary courts, maintains that the Olęders availed themselves of the rights belonging to them quite often and to full effect. The same is proved also by the decrees of the Crown Referendary concerning the okupniks (tenants who had redeemed their land) (among others also Olęders) of the starostwo

[^37-1]: Vol. Leg. II, 982, 996, 1026, 1302; III, 026.

[p. 38]

of Rogoźno. The complainants received here, even before the delivery of the proper judgment, the royal glejt, binding them until the resolution of the matter to only those services which they had been paying before the dispute began, and moreover protecting them against possible acts of repression on the part of the starosta. How these relations presented themselves in the noble estates it is difficult to establish. The old Polish judicial process made no provision in its rules for the peasant population that was not subject. Presumably there existed in this respect some judicial practice, but cases of this kind have not yet been sufficiently investigated. Probably in practice there prevailed the tendency to treat free peasants on a par with subjects. As regards the Olęders, it seems a certain thing that they were granted the right of independently filing complaints and manifests before the grod and land offices,[^38-1] even against their own lord. Such a right, as follows from the practice applied at entries into the land books and grod books, did not belong to subject peasants; these could give testimony before the grod or land offices only in the presence of their lords. Very unclear is the question of the appearance of the Olęders before the public courts. In one case, in which we succeeded in finding the relevant court records, an Olęder who had himself filed a manifest against his former lord and the Olęder community for the unlawful removal from the farm he occupied appears at the court hearings "in assistentia Illustrissimi Lucae Bniński Judicis Terrestris Posnaniensis" [in the assistance of the most illustrious Łukasz Bniński, judge of the Poznań land court].[^38-3] There arises here the possibility that either that Olęder[^38-2] . / 628,

[^38-1]: Rel. Posn. no. 608, 1784, fol. 439 and 460; no. 612, 1785, fol. 394; no. 028, 1789, fol. 202.
[^38-2]: On the legal significance of the complaint, manifest or protest, cf. Balzer Oswald, Przewód sądowy polski w zarysie, Lwów 1935 (1887).
[^38-3]: The plaintiff filed to the grod books the following manifest (Rel. Posn. no. 033, 1790, fol. 302): "H. Bohne ctra Im Mielżyński et alios [H. Bohne against Mielżyński and others]. Having come in person before the Office and Grod Books of Poznań, the honourable Jan Bochme, possessor under the law of okupne of certain lands in the Holendry Essenwaldskie belonging to the Trzciela estate, seeking after the whole of his property, brings a complaint and declares himself against the honourable Maximilian Mielżyński, grand clerk of the Crown, hereditary lord of the Trzcielskie estate with its appurtenances, as well as against the honourable Marcin Pudwill and Jerzy Horn, the present possessors of the lands of which he complains, against Gottlieb Kusz, sołtys, and Jerzy Gebauer, ławnik, of the aforementioned Holendry Essenwaldskie; he brings a grievance and complains as follows: that although the complainant duly paid the rent each year to the manor for the lands held by him in the Holendry Essenwaldskie, and performed the other duties in the same way as the other Holendrzy, and offered the creditors pursuing the complainant satisfaction of their rightful dues, and finally, so as to give the creditors the speediest satisfaction from his own means, wished to sell his lands, having a buyer who offered 3,000 Prussian thalers; yet the complainant, proceeding so openly with everyone, did not receive for himself that justice which ought to have followed, for some three years ago he was violently cast out of his possession. They sold the lands for the sum of only 2,300 thalers, carried off the stock, and although 1,000 thalers stands liquidated to the creditors, and the remainder ought to have belonged to the complainant, they do not think of returning even that sum. Since, then, the complainant has been stripped of his whole property, and has moreover suffered great wrongs, he therefore has recourse to these Books and lodges his grievance both against the honourable Mielżyński and against the sołtys and the ławniks of the Holendry Essenwaldskie, and likewise against the possessors unlawfully occupying the lands of the complainant, wishing to pursue his wrongs lawfully before the proper court.
s. Johann Bóhme" In consequence of the Olęder's action the case came before the Poznań land court, where it was heard at three terms (Decr. Posn. no. 137, fol. 303, 485 and 597–600). The defendants maintained that the whole sum obtained from the sale had been applied to the payment of the debts of the plaintiff, which in their opinion were considerably more than the plaintiff had stated. How the case was finally decided is not known, for the further collections of decrees have not survived.

[p. 39]

at the moment of the suit was sitting on the lands of Bniński (the Biezdrowo estate in the Poznań county), and in consequence the presence of the latter at the hearings had the character of a statutory substitution [of a party],1 or else[^39-1] he appeared as a patron, requested by the Olęder, or appointed to him[^39-2] by the court.[^39-3] We have also the interesting case of the lord enrolling in the grod books[^39-4] a complaint against his own Olęders, charging them that they act disobediently[^39-5] and do not render to the complainant their dues and the debts they have contracted. Elsewhere, again, the lord stipulates: "Should any excess or criminal case arise, they must not go to any court without my knowledge, or that of my possessor".5 Presumably this stipulation would have been superfluous had the judicial lordship of the lord over the Olęders been undoubted and complete. On the other hand we have also to record such an action, in which the plaintiff, the Olęder community, appears "in competenti domini sui assistentia" [before the competent assistance of its lord]. In any case the question whether[^39-6] the Olęders had the possibility of using the public courts cannot be resolved with complete certainty. Moreover, even if that possibility existed, it would not have been of greater significance for the Olęders in view of the judicial procedure of the time, too slow and not protecting against the reprisals of the defendant. For Olęders enjoying the freedom to leave the village it was far simpler to sell the farm and settle under another lord. It seems that this possibility was availed of fairly often.
