[^39-2]: Kutrzeba Stanisław, Dawne polskie prawo sądowe w zarysie, Lwów 1921, p. 65; Balzer, Przewód sądowy, p. 36.
[^39-3]: This last possibility seems less probable owing to the fact that Bniński, as a state official (land judge of Poznań), had no statutory claims to a substitute. (Cf. Rafacz, Zastępcy stron, p. 37; the same, Dawny proces polski, Warszawa 1925, p. 97.)
[^39-4]: Rel. Cost. no. 223, 1790, fol. 519.
[^39-5]: Rel. Cost. no. 164, 1729, fol. 177 c.
[^39-6]: Rel. Cost. no. 224, 1791, fol. 676.

[p. 40]

B. Manor law in the Olęder settlements. 1. The legal relation to the lord. a. The charter.

While the public authorities issued no legal norms concerning Olęder settlement at all, the system of norms concerning this settlement issued by the lord was broadly developed.[^40-1] They regulated a range of matters falling either within the domain of public law (the lord's judicature, communal and church self-government) or within the domain of private law (personal status, the law of things, succession). They concerned, therefore, the relation of the Olęders to the lord, the mutual relations of the Olęders within the settlement, and finally the relations of the Olęders to institutions and third persons (among others the church). The basis of the legal relation to the lord was the common charter (contract, deed — Latin privilegium, ius, German Gerechtigkeit), issued to the Olęder community by the lord, most often after the surveying of its lands, more rarely at a later period.[^40-2] Exceptionally the proper charter was replaced by its surrogates, for example a provisional preliminary agreement,[^40-3] or else the parties accepted as binding the charter of another community. Although the charter formally was not a contract but a unilateral act, issued and signed[^40-4] by the lord, in fact its content was settled by way of agreement between the lord and the settlers.[^40-5] As a rule the charter was written on parchment, in a more or less ornate form, with the lord's seal pressed in wax directly on the document, or hanging on a ribbon or cord threaded through a cut in the lower part of the parchment. More rarely the charter had the humbler form of a sheet of paper with the lord's signature and seal. The language of the charters was mostly Polish; of the 142 known charters 108 were issued in Polish, 32 in German,[^40-6] and 2 in Polish and German.

[^40-1]: Rel. Cost. no. 191, 1756, fol. 94: "...but in the other points reference is made to the law customary among the other Olęders living in the Poznań voivodeship, and I declare that they and their descendants, together with my successors, are to observe all of this".
[^40-2]: Cf. in Appendix I the dates of the founding of the settlements against the dates of the charters. It was otherwise in settlement under German law; there the locator began the location only after receiving the charter.
[^40-3]: Thus, for example, at the founding of the Jędrzejewskie Olęders the founder Jędrzej Radoliński concluded with the Olęders in 1763 a provisional agreement, which was to remain valid until the issue of the proper charter; yet as late as 1773 the Olęders declare that the charter had not yet been issued (Kat. II, fol. 239).
[^40-4]: For example, the Młyńskie Olęders were bound by the charter for Karpicko.
[^40-5]: In concluding the agreement with the lord the party was always the Olęder community, appearing as a compact, solidary society. The role of the locator, if he existed at all (cf. p. 57), ended with the bringing in of the settlers.
[^40-6]: Meanwhile Maas (Entstehung, p. 84) maintains that "diese Privilegien... sind fast alle in deutscher Sprache geschrieben. Schon dies west uns auf die Nationalitit der Bewohner hin..." [these charters... are almost all written in the German language. This alone points to us the nationality of the inhabitants...].

[p. 41]

As regards content the charters show a great kinship. This comes of the fact that in issuing them the pattern of the charters of earlier settlements was followed. There is no lack, however, of very original documents, at times marked by a deep social and moral thought. One of such documents, the collective charter for the Olęders of the Kicin estate complex, we quote in full in the appendix. To confirm the force of the charter, the lords frequently bound themselves to enrol it in the proper grod books. This set a precedent for the Olęders, who themselves too began to submit their charters for enrolment. A number of charters contain additional confirmations by later owners of the settlement. At times such later confirmation became the occasion for an increase in the duties of the Olęders. In almost every charter the lord bound himself, in the event of the destruction or loss of the original, to issue free of any cost a counterpart with identical wording.[^41-1] b. The legal dependence of the Olęders on the lord could manifest itself in a threefold manner: as personal, judicial[^41-2] or tenurial dependence. In relation to the corvée population this dependence was complete; one distinguishes here accordingly personal, judicial and tenurial subjection.[^41-3] Personal subjection[^41-4] of the Olęder farmers did not in general exist. Almost all the charters stressed this unanimously. Less clear[^41-5] presented itself the question of the personal dependence of the Olęders in cases where a village already in existence was transferred to Olęder law. Thus, for example, the charter for Ol. Chorzępowa, which arose in this way in the Sieraków estate, allowed them to sell their farms, and therefore probably also to leave the estate, and defined their duties in the same way as the duties of other Olęders. But the charter lacks any declaration confirming their personal freedom, and the inventory of the estate, later by 6 years, classes part of the farmers as subjects.5 Entirely lacking, on the other hand, are data for the remaining settlements of this type. It is nevertheless beyond doubt that even

[^41-1]: Rudka Ol. and Szklanohuckie Ol.
[^41-2]: We quote a provision typical for the Nowe Olęders: "Item, God preserve it, should the Lord God visit the aforesaid Olęders with fire and should the very original of the charter burn, I shall at once have another written out without delay word for word and give it to my Olęders..." (AW. Nowa Tuchorza, A. 1).
[^41-3]: Rutkowski, Poddaństwo włościan, p. 12. |
[^41-4]: For illustration we quote several source texts: AW. Marienwald, B. ]: "They are to be free people both with me and with my descendants." AW. Jaromierz, B. 4, fol. 77 (Ujskie Stare Ol.): "...all these fourteen farmers are to have freedom for everlasting times, together with their forefathers, upon the real payment of the aforementioned rent." AW. Nowa Tuchorza, A. 1 (Nowe Ol.): "I wish also... that they (i.e. the Olęders) and their successors should now and henceforth be free people and for ever without any molestation." AW. Uścikowskie Ol., B. 1054: "They will render no serfdom and no labour dues, for they are free people...".
[^41-5]: AM. Sieraków, C. 53, fol. 162 ff.

[p. 42]

in the case where the settlers in question had formerly been subjects, and the issue of the charter was not accompanied by the issue of an act of manumission, the personal dependence of the Olęders became looser as a result of its restriction to the norms of the charter. Exceptionally, even at the location of new settlements, the lords took into account the possibility of settling subjects in them, as is proved by the following stipulation in the charter of 1729 for Ol. Kotusz: "...it is added to this that these Olęders are to belong to no other duties than the dues expressed above, and likewise their possessors or successors are to remain free people, except for those who had already been subjects before".[^42-1] Whether subjects were in fact settled there seems very doubtful. In any case the sources do not cast doubt on the principle itself of the personal freedom of the Olęders. This principle naturally extended also to the families of the Olęders. It is harder to establish whether the remaining, landless, socially lower stratum of the Olęder population was also personally free. Direct data in this respect we possess only in relation to the Sieraków estate. The inventory of that estate of 1759 contains a division of the whole population of the settlements into free and subject; the whole landless population of the Olęder settlements, with the exception of 1 family,[^42-2] is personally free. Only in the already mentioned Ol. Chorzępowa do we find 2 subject families.[^42-3] This confirms the supposition that 1 the landless inhabitants of the settlements were regarded as personally free with very few exceptions. Personal freedom distinguished the Olęders positively among the subject population. In passing, however, we shall note here that, as the not yet worked-up inventories[^42-4] and lists[^42-5] show, the number of non-subjects among the remaining peasant population was greater than it has hitherto been estimated.[^42-6] In the present state of research, conducting comparisons loaded with tendentiousness between the Olęders and the rest of the peasant population, as some German authors did, seems at the least premature.

c. The lord's judicature forms the second member of the relations connecting the lord with the Olęders. The question of the Olęders' recourse to the public courts was already discussed above; the relevant considerations yielded a rather negative result. The proper court, both practically and as the final instance, was for the Olęders the patrimonial court. It heard in the first

[^42-1]: Rel. Cost. no. 164, 1729, p. 487. The same mention is contained also in the charter of 1729 for the Karpickie Olęders (AW. A. 2).
[^42-2]: The herdsman in Ol. Nowy Zatom (AM. Sieraków C. 53, fol. 131).
[^42-3]: A tailor and a female lodger (ibid., fol. 162).
[^42-4]: Particularly interesting in this respect are the enrolments of village inventories in the grod records.
[^42-5]: Striking, for example, is the considerable number of "free labourers" shown in the Tol.
[^42-6]: Cf. Cieplucha, Z przeszłości, pp. 92–93.

[p. 43]

instance all the graver offences, the so-called criminalia (robberies, arson, killings, graver injuries to the body[^43-1]), and naturally all the offences directed directly against the lord. As a second instance this court decided the appeals from the judgments of the village court.[^43-2] Concerning the procedure of the lord's judicature the sources offer no closer data. The lord collected no fees for judicial acts.

2. The law of things. There come into consideration here the whole body of norms which regulated the relation of the Olęders to the land and real property in their possession, defined the manner, conditions and scope of disposing of real property, and concerned the movable property of the Olęders. In the earliest-founded settlements the Olęders, as in Royal Prussia, were settled on the conditions of a temporary lease. In Ol. Ujskie the lease ran for 44 years,[^43-3] in Ol. Nowe Dwory 60 years.[^43-4] On similar conditions there were probably settled the remaining settlements of the Wieleń estate, namely Folsztyn, Herbardowo and Marianowo 5.[^43-5] The charter reserved to the Olęders priority in the leasing of land for further years, provided only that they offered a rent not lower than the rent of the other applicants. It was permitted to the Olęders to dispose of their farms (strictly, the buildings and the movable stock) even before the expiry of the lease, but without prejudice to the duties owed to the lord. In Ol. Ujskie the temporary lease, renewed several times, lasted right up to the partitions,[^43-6] whereas the Wieleń Olęders, after the expiry of the first lease period, were granted by counterpart charters of 1677 the land in perpetual lease. Erroneous is the opinion of Gebhardt[^43-7] and Schulz[^43-8] that the grant in heritable ownership appears for the first time only in the charters of 1677 for the Olęders of the Wieleń estate. Probably already in Ol. Murzynowskie founded in 1613,[^43-9] and certainly in Ol. Róża located in 1624, the settlers received the land in perpetual

[^43-1]: "And should such an excess or crime occur, it will belong to the lord's court..." (AW. Puszczykowskie Ol., A. 1).
[^43-2]: "In their matters, which are brought before the sołtys, if either party should not be content, they are permitted an appeal to their Lord" (Rel. Posn. no. 489, 1751, fol. 69).
[^43-3]: Inscr. Valc. no. 85, 1663–74, fol. 402.
[^43-4]: Rel. Posn. no. 140, 1602, fol. 659.
[^43-5]: In 1643 the lease of the Marianowo Olęders was extended by 30 years (Q, I, no. 25). Since they were located in 1614, this proves that the lands had been granted to the Olęders on a 30-year lease the first time round as well.
[^43-6]: The lease was prolonged in succession: in 1637 for 60 years (Inscr. Valc. no. 85, 1663–74, fol. 402), in 1701 for 60 years (AW. C. 2, I, fol. 175), in 1763 for 50 years (AW. C. 1).
[^43-7]: Uniertanenbuch, p. XI.
[^43-8]: Die zwcite deutsche Ostsiedlung, I, p. 58.
[^43-9]: Cf. p. 19.

[p. 44]

holding. This form of tenure becomes the rule in the remaining settlements. Exceptionally in Ol. Barcinek the land was granted to the Olęders "by emphyteutic law... namely until the fourth generation of male blood",1 while in the Połajewskie Olęders the settlers received the contract originally for three years only.2 In connexion with the transition from temporary lease to perpetual grants — the wie-[^44-1]czyste holding — there appears the so-called okupne (German Grundgeld). This was the payment for the land assigned to the Olęders. Thus while the first Olęders, who had not redeemed themselves, could dispose only of the buildings and the movable property, and the land[^44-2] remained the exclusive property of the lord, the later Olęders, after payment of the okupne, became owners of the whole farm, of the land as well as of the buildings and movables. Likewise the Wieleń Olęders, before perpetual right was granted them in place of[^44-3][^44-4] the lease, had to redeem the land. Unredeemed settlements[^44-5] were among the Olęders an immense rarity. Besides the already mentioned Ol. Ujskie, Olęders sat partly on unredeemed farms in Daleszyno 4, in Chorzępowa, and probably 1 also[^44-6] in the Stare Łoskońskie Olęders 5.

According to the tenor of the charters the Olęders received their farms in heritable ownership (in charters issued[^44-7] in German: erb- und eigentümlich). It was, however, not full ownership but only usufructuary; the lord remained still the owner of the land, but so long as the Olęder complied with the provisions of the charter he had no right to remove him from the land. By virtue of the tenure there belonged to the lord, therefore, only the right to the services specified in the charter. Stein R.8$,

[^44-1]: AW. B. I, charter of 1781.
[^44-2]: Dom. P. 10820, fol. 39, charter of 1766: "This contract is to serve them for three years only, and if it should not please any of them to sit longer, he will be free to move out...". The contract was nevertheless kept in force further, for its provisions bound the settlers right up to the enfranchisement.
[^44-3]: In the community book of Folsztyn (AW. dep. C. 5, fol. 3) we find the note: "Die gerechtigkeit vnd die holandischen gutter sint gekaufft im Jahr 1672" [the gerechtigkeit and the Holendry farms were bought in the year sixteen hundred and seventy-two]. The relevant charter was issued in 1677, hence probably after the okupne had been paid in full. AGH court of Gostyń, the Daleszyno estate, vol. no. 55, 1778, fol. 8: "Jan Bargier, an Olęder... who sits on an empty half-cottage, unredeemed...". fol. 9: "Krysztoff Wagner, who sits on a whole cottage unredeemed... the Olęder by the name of Jan Sztull sits on a whole cottage, but unredeemed...".
[^44-4]: The charter for the Stare Łoskońskie Olęders allowed the Olęders to sell "their possession, that is the buildings and the sowings". The land, therefore, was probably not the property of the Olęders.
[^44-5]: We quote typical mentions from the charters: "we sold the land in advance, so that, once cleared, for everlasting times both the aforesaid Olęders and the children and their other successors should enjoy by right of inheritance these lands in everlasting ages" (AW. Góździn Ol., A. 1, fol. 69). "These same inhabitants are to leave their children and descendants as heirs of all this" (Sękowskie Ol., Rel. Posn. no. 043, 1792, fol. 54).
[^44-6]: "Ich habe ihnen (tzn. olędrom) ein Stick Busch zu Erb und Erbnehmen cigen=tiimlich verkauffet" [I have sold them (i.e. the Olęders) a piece of bush as heritable and inheritable property] (AW. Jaromierz, dep. A. 1, charter of 1709).
[^44-7]: Die ländliche Verfassung, p. 123.

[p. 45]

following the definition of the Prussian territorial law (Landrecht) of 1721.1,[^45-1] describes this kind of relation to the land as "perpetual rent law"[^45-2] (Erbzinsrecht), by virtue of which the holder could dispose of the land at will, provided only that he paid to the lord the services due. The rent-holder was bound to bestow on the land such care "as if it were his own property". Guradze Fr. presents the matter similarly;[^45-3] in Schmidt, on the other hand, we have the concepts of perpetual rent law mixed together with the concept of perpetual lease, which had a different significance.[^45-4] The matter presented itself differently in those few cases in which an Olęder sat on unredeemed land. Here the relation of the Olęder to the land was rather like a perpetual lease.

It is characteristic that in the charters, apart from a few exceptions 5, no sanctions were provided for the event of the Olęders[^45-5] failing to pay the services provided in the charter. This is explained by the fact that the Olęders answered for the manorial duties for the most part solidarily, so that the obligation of bearing the services for a dilatory Olęder, or one not in a position to pay, fell upon the remaining members of the community.

The charters enumerated in detail the rights belonging to the Olęders by virtue of their possession of the land: "of which land both they themselves and their possessors may make use at their pleasure; namely it shall be free for them to sell it, to give, to bestow, to barter away and to turn to their own best advantage". The right of disposing of the land was, however, not un-[^45-6]limited. Beside substantive restrictions on the alienation of the land we have a series of formal conditions which the seller, and at times also the purchaser, had to fulfil before the deed of sale was recognized by the lord as valid.

I. As a rule the lord required that the change of holder should not take place to the detriment of the manorial duties.[^45-7] In the filling of the farm

[^45-1]: T. II, book 4, title 9 (cited from Stein).
[^45-2]: Der Bauer in Posen, p. 249.
[^45-3]: Geschichte, pp. 381 and 391.
[^45-4]: Stein, Die ländliche Verfassung, p. 125.
[^45-5]: In the charter for the settlement of Suchylas (Kl. M.) the lord stipulates: "But should this community fail to pay the duties laid upon them and due from them, then, having assessed their buildings, I leave the free repayment to myself as well as to my successors, with the deduction of the dues owed by them".
[^45-6]: Podgradowice, charter of 1729 (Rel. Cost. no. 104, 1729, fol. 177).
[^45-7]: AW. Uścikowskie Ol., B. 1054, charter of 1746: "Each of them shall be free to sell his Holenderstwo, to give, to bestow, to barter away, only to a virtuous, unrecorded and free man...". AW. Wielkokrobielewskie Ol., B. 1 b., charter of 1672: "It shall be free for everyone to go and settle wherever he may wish, but with my knowledge, and having taken up a place with a good neighbour, with which I or the lordship would be content, so that the rents do not perish".

[p. 46]

with a "good man" it was not only the seller who was bound to see to it, but 1 the whole community solidarily.

2. In some cases the alienation of a farm required the lord's consent.[^46-1] Here we have already to do with a serious restriction of the Olęders in the free disposal of their land. A case, however, in which the lord on the basis of this provision made it impossible for an Olęder to alienate his land we have not found in the sources.

3. Exceptionally the lord excluded definite categories of persons from the circle of possible purchasers. Thus, for example, all the Olęders in the Sieraków estate were not permitted to alienate their farms to the church. Similarly it was in Ol. Sikorka 3. In the Kiączyńskie and Włościejewskie Olęders the purchasers could not be Dissidents.[^46-2] In the Uścikowskie Olęders the purchaser had to be a free man.[^46-3] The Olęders in Daleszyno were not permitted to sell land "across the border".[^46-4] This last condition was presumably designed to exclude the sale of land to an inhabitant[^46-5] of a neighbouring village, over whom judicial lordship was already exercised by someone else. Such a sale would lead to a division of judicial lordship over the purchaser between different lords. Whether any such division of the lord's lordship over the Olęders occurred at all seems doubtful. In any case the sources do not mention it.[^46-6] 4. We also meet restrictions of a proprietary character. In the Cisiogórskie Olęders it was not permitted to sell land "by morgs, in pieces, only half a huba together".7 This provision was intended to prevent[^46-7] the excessive fragmentation of the lands. In the Świącyńskie Olęders, on the other hand, the lord reserved to himself "that one man should not hold two plots, but that each should content himself with one".[^46-8] In the Trojanowskie Olęders a settler alienating his farm could take for the land only as much as he himself had paid.

The formal conditions which from time to time the alienator, or exceptionally the new purchaser, had to fulfil, presented themselves as follows:

I. As a rule the Olęder was bound to inform the lord of the intended sale of the land. Quite exceptionally in Ol. Pecna it was permitted

[^46-1]: The charter for the Chrząstowskie Olęders (Rel. Cost. no. 220, 1787, fol. 924). In the Stare Dymaczewskie 1 and Borkowskie Olęders, as also in the Krosińskie Olęders, the community was not permitted to admit an Olęder "without equipment".
[^46-2]: So it was, among others,1 in the Chrząstowskie, Stare Dymaczewskie Olęders, Góździn, Karpicko, Stare Jaromierskie Olęders (call numbers of the charters given in Appendix I).
[^46-3]: AW. B. 9I1: "none of the present and future holders of their lands shall be free to bequeath them to any church".
[^46-4]: AW. Kiełczynek, A. 1, Rel. Cost. no. 217, 1784, fol. 78, Włościejewskie Ol.
[^46-5]: Cf. note 7, p. 45.
[^46-6]: Rel. Posn. no. 549, 1767, fol. 442.
[^46-7]: Rel. Cost. no. 224, 1788, fol. 379.
[^46-8]: Rel. Cost. no. 191, 1756, fol. 94.

[p. 47]

7 to every Olęder "to pay off the field without a report to the manor and to sell it".1[^47-1] The same condition often bound the alienator also in relation to the community. The deed of sale had to be made in the communal office, and if the community possessed a community book, the appropriate entry had to be made. The purchaser was bound to pay the price of the land into the communal office, which covered from it all the fees arising from the sale, paid the debts contracted by the alienator, and only then paid out the remainder to him.[^47-2]

2. The alienator or the purchaser were bound to pay certain fees to the lord and the community. The fee most often collected by the lord from the alienator was the 10th grosz, more rarely the 12th grosz,[^47-3] exceptionally 5% of the sum of the sale.[^47-4] At times the lord collected a fixed fee, e.g. 72 zł in Ostrów and in Borgowo (1785),[^47-5] 2 thalers in the Chełmskie[^47-6] and Stare Łoskońskie Olęders,[^47-7] 1 thaler in the Budziszewskie Olęders,[^47-8] 1 red złoty

in Rudka Olęders.[^47-9] In the Budziszewskie and Stare Łoskońskie Olęders the obligation to pay the fee burdened the alienator and the purchaser solidarily. In the Chełmskie Olęders and in Złotów the seller and the buyer covered the fee in equal shares. In the charter for Czapury we have the provision that the settler "on sale or gift shall be bound to content the Convent".[^47-10] The purchaser was usually bound to buy for the community one, and exceptionally 2 barrels of the so-called introductory beer (German Nachbarbier) from the lord's brew.[^47-11]

3. Among the formal conditions must also be reckoned the right of pre-emption, naturally provided that it operated only upon the offer of a price equal to that of the highest bidder. An absolute right of pre-emption, which would already be a considerable restriction of free sale, probably did not exist among the Olęders. The right of pre-emption

[^47-1]: Rel. Cost. no. 165, 1730, fol. 823.
[^47-2]: AW. Chodzież, C. I, fol. 2 (Krystynka Ol.); Rel. Cost. no. 220, 1787, fol. 530, Chrząstowskie Ol.; AW. Milczewskie Ol. A. 1; Rel. Posn. no. 626, 1788, fol. 405, Zakrzewo Ol. Cf. also the wilkierz for Ol. Kotusz quoted in the appendix.
[^47-3]: So it was in Ol. Kałędek (Q I, no. 54) and in Ol. Bronice (Kl. XII, 6 II, fol. 55).
[^47-4]: In the Lubaskie Olęders (Kl. XII, 6 V, fol. 116). |
[^47-5]: Rel. Cost. no. 220, 1787, fol. 372.
[^47-6]: Rel. Posn. no. 4535, 1742, fol. ZJU.
[^47-7]: AW. Stare Łoskońskie Ol., B. 365.
[^47-8]: AW. Budziszewskie Ol., B. 110 (agreement of 1770).
[^47-9]: Rel. Posn. no. 641, 1792, fol. 127.
[^47-10]: AW. Czapury, B. 1 a.
[^47-11]: AW. Uścikowskie Ol., B. 1054: "whoever buys as successor shall owe the community a barrel of introductory beer". Similarly: Rel. Posn. no. 640, 1791, fol. 248, Rataje Ol.; Rel. Posn. no. 620, 1787, fol. 27, Snowidowo Ol.; AGH, court of Wolsztyn, the Gościeszyn estate, vol. V (334 cards), fol. 33, Stodólsko Ol.

[p. 48]

exceptionally the lord reserved to himself,[^48-1] whereas quite often it belonged to the neighbours of the alienator or to the remaining members of the community.[^48-2]

Summing up the considerations so far, it must be stated that although as a rule the lord, and at times also the community, set certain conditions at the sale of Olęder farms, these did not — apart from the rarely occurring obligation to obtain the lord's permission for the sale — seriously restrict the principle of the free disposal of the land.

The sale as a rule comprised both the land itself and everything that was on that land (timber, grass, at times sowings and buildings). At times the lord reserved to himself the right to the oaks standing on the Olęder lands.[^48-3] Similarly the barcie (bee-trees) which the lord's beekeepers, or the inhabitants of the surrounding villages, had on the ground assigned to the Olęders, usually remained their property so long as bees were there.[^48-4] Ores (minerals) that might possibly occur on the ground assigned to the Olęders remained the property of the lord.[^48-5] The Olęders themselves built the buildings on the lands assigned to them; these were their indivisible and unrestricted property. It was the same with the live and dead stock. The community, too, appeared as owner. At times it possessed its own common pasture.[^48-6] Its common property also comprised the public buildings, such as schools and houses of prayer. The community likewise had at its disposal the field forming the endowment of the teacher of the settlement.

On the lands of the Olęders there could exist certain servitudes in favour of the lord or of third persons. To these must be reckoned the sporadic right of the manor to pasture its own cattle on the Olęder lands.[^48-7] The neighbours of an Olęder sometimes had the right of digging ditches through his land for the discharge of water,[^48-8] at times there belonged to them the right of driving their herds across Olęder land.[^48-9] On the whole, however, the burdens enumerated occurred

[^48-1]: For example in the Białokoskie Olęders (AW. Białokosz, B. 1), in the Kiączyńskie Olęders (AW. Kieł-
[^48-2]: Apart from certain charters, e.g. for the Stare Jaromierskie Olęders, all the wilkierze contain such a reservation (call numbers given in the further notes).
[^48-3]: For example in the Śroczewskie Olęders (Rel. Posn. no. 035, 1790, fol. 272), in the Włościejewskie Olęders (Rel. Cost. no. 217, 1784, fol. 78).
[^48-4]: In Ol. Kolsztyn, Herbardowo, Marianowo and Nowe Dwory (charters of 1677).
[^48-5]: "Since this treasure belongs to the castle, and whatever is above the ground shall belong to the Holendrzy" (Rel. Posn. no. 641, 1792, fol. 260, Wymyślanka Ol.).
[^48-6]: See further, pp. 70–71.
[^48-7]: AW. Szklarka Trzcielska, A. 1; AW. Trojanowskie Ol., B. 1037; Rel. Posn. no. 640, 1791, fol. 248, Rataje Ol.
[^48-8]: In the oldest Olęders of the Wieleń estate and in Ol. Ujskie.
[^48-9]: Thus, for example, the inhabitants of the village of Rosko had the right of driving cattle across the lands of Ol. Nowe Dwory (AW. Nowe Dwory, B. 1).

[p. 49]

rarely and never seriously diminished the rights of the holders of the land.

3. The law of succession. Of the norms of succession in force in the Olęder settlements the sources contain only very scanty information. If an Olęder died without leaving heirs behind him, his property always passed into the ownership of the manor. As can[^49-1] be inferred from the divisions of property registered in the community books, the children inherited from their parents in equal shares, one of them as a rule taking over the farm and paying off the remaining siblings. Wills registered in the community books seem[^49-2] to indicate that the complete disinheritance of one of the children was not practised, perhaps not even permissible. As a rule the interests of orphans were protected by special provisions,[^49-3] or, in the event of the remarriage of one of the parents, the interests of the children of the first marriage.[^49-4] Wills had sometimes to be drawn

[^49-1]: The charter for Ol. Karpicko (AW. A. 1) provides: "but should an Olęder die without an heir, all his goods shall fall to the lord". Similar provisions are found in the charters for the Olęders of the Sieraków estate, for Ol. Kobus, Krosna etc. Somewhat differently does the charter for Czapury treat this matter (AW. B. 1 a): "but should any of them die without issue and without a natural successor, then the goods of that one go ad fiscum Conventi [to the treasury of the Convent], and the Convent shall be able to sell them at their proper valuation to whom it will; the nearest, however, to buy shall be the nearest neighbour, and should he not wish it, any one whatsoever of our Czapury Holendrzy...". Erroneous is the supposition of Schmidt (p. 348) that in the earlier settlements, after the death of an Olęder without issue, his property passed into the ownership of the community. Cases of inheritance by the community of part of the property of an Olęder who died without issue, though beyond the borders of the former Poznań voivodeship, are recorded by Baranowski (Wsie holenderskie, p. 73).
[^49-2]: The principle of inheritance of the land by the eldest son, to which Schmidt refers, did not exist. On the contrary, as can be inferred from the entries in the community books, the inheritance of the land even by daughters, with payment to the sons, was a matter of daily occurrence.
[^49-3]: Cf., for example, the wilkierz for the Nowodąbrowskie Olęders (AW. B. 1).
[^49-4]: Very detailed provisions in this respect are found in the charters for Ol. Huta, Krystynka and the Milczewskie Olęders: "Since considerable wrong is done to the children after each deceased father through the disposition of their stepmothers, I hereby establish for them, by good order, complete security: as soon as the father dies, the sołtys with the ławniks and the natural guardians shall at once set down the whole remaining substance of the deceased, as it stands in inventories and furnishings, and likewise value the goods conscientiously, and set down that sum together with the other movables and enter the register into this protocol where their other transactions are entered, as he is bound to do; and should the surviving widow be about to marry, then the sołtys and the ławniks together with the guardians shall assign to the children, in proportion, the shares due to them out of the paternal1 substance under the marriage contract, so that the children may enjoy the substance won by their father's labour of blood. But should there be no children from the second marriage and the mother should die before the second husband, then one half of that substance which she bequeathed to her second husband by the marriage contract is to fall to the children of the first marriage as natural successors, while the other half is to remain with the second husband. But if she has children from the second marriage, then from that portion of her own, also after her death, the children of the first marriage are to share equally, as in the maternal substance, since only by the proportion of the expressed marriage contract out of the paternal substance will those children of the first marriage be satisfied. And should she survive her second husband and have no children at all, then that part which was bequeathed to him by the marriage contract belongs to his successors and not to the children of the first marriage",

[p. 50]

up in a definite form. Thus, for example, in the Jędrzejewskie Olęders the settlers were bound to draw them up in writing and to submit them for signature first to the sołtys and the ławniks, and then to the lord. For this act the lord collected 11[^50-1] tymfs from each document.

4. Communal self-government. a) Organisation. The broadly developed communal self-government constituted, as we have mentioned, an essential feature of Olęder settlement. In principle every settlement formed a separate unit of self-government. In exceptional cases two or even three settlements could have self-governing organs in common.[^50-2] The constitution of communal self-government in the Olęder settlements presented itself differently from that in the remaining villages. This concerned above all the organs of self-government. These organs consisted of an elected sołtys[^50-3] and ławniks. Their term of office lasted one year. Quite exceptionally in the Radoszkowskie Olęders the sołtys, and in the Uścikowskie Olęders the whole village bench, was elected for life, while the common sołtys of the Czerwonackie, Janikowskie[^50-4] and Kliny Olęders was elected for a three-year period. The choice both of the sołtys and of the ławniks was made by the assembly of all the farmers of the community. Only in the Karpickie Olęders did the choice of the ławniks belong to the sołtys. The ławniks were most often 2 in number,[^50-5] more rarely 3,[^50-6] exceptionally 4. In the same composition the substitutes were appointed. As a rule the choice of the organs of self-government fell at Whitsuntide, at times, however, the term of office ended at St Martin's (11 November) 5, or St John the Baptist (24 June) 8. We meet no provisions restricting passive or active electoral rights. Undoubtedly, however, there existed in this respect certain customary rules. It is certain only that women had neither active nor passive electoral rights. The choice fell as a rule on the older farmers of the settlement. In the few cases

[^50-1]: Kar. II, fol. 239.
[^50-2]: The settlements Czerwonak, Kliny and Janikowo had common communal organs (cf. Appendix II). Ol. Zwierzyniec had no sołtys of its own, but only elected a ławnik to the community of Nowy Zatom. Ol. Sikorka belonged to the communal administration of the Kamińskie Olęders.
[^50-3]: Rel. Cost. no. 215, 1782, fol. 351; AW. Uścikowskie Ol., B. 1054.
[^50-4]: In Zakrzewo (Rel. Posn. no. 620, 1788, fol. 406).
[^50-5]: So it was, for example, in Popowo (Rel. Cost. no. 174, 1739, fol. 1121), in Kawczyn (Rel. Cost. no. 177, 1742, fol. 211).
[^50-6]: For example in the Budziszewskie Olęders (AW. B. 108), in Ol. Dziewczastruga (AW. B. 1028), in the Gębickie Olęders (AW. A. 1).

[p. 51]

the choice required confirmation by the manor, or even the lord chose one of the candidates presented to him.[^51-1]

The constitution of the communal organs did not present itself uniformly throughout the whole period of the colonization. On the part of the lords there sporadically revealed itself a tendency to restrict the collegiate prerogatives of the communal authorities in favour of the sołtys and to make the sołtys more dependent on the manor. At the location of several later settlements even a hereditary sołtys was instituted, whereby these settlements became, constitutionally, to a certain degree like settlements located contemporaneously under German law. In Appendix I these settlements have been included in the group of transitional settlements. The same significance attached to the command that the electors of the community be presented to the manor for confirmation. Considerably rarer were attempts to transform the communal constitution of settlements already in existence. Above, on p. 40, there has already been presented the evolution which the settlement of Jędrzejewo underwent. At the close of the eighteenth century an attempt was made to impose a hereditary sołtys by lordly nomination on the Stare Jaromierskie Olęders, and on the Olęders of Stare and Nowe Ujście.[^51-3] Conversely, however, the settlement of Rzecień, located on the principles of German law, became with the passage of time, constitutionally, like the Olęder settlements. In general it must be stated that the Olęder communal constitution once granted to a settlement in the location charter was respected by the manor also later.

b. The functions of self-government may be divided into delegated[^51-2] and own functions. In the first case the organs of self-government performed duties falling within the scope of the manorial administration, entrusted to them by the lord. Thus the sołtys and the ławniks were bound to see to it that the whole community punctually and completely performed the manorial duties. It was also the duty of the sołtys to collect the rents and fees due to the lord and hand them over to the manor. Moreover, as regards the fees from the sale of farms, from contracts, he had to see to it that no embezzlements were committed and that[^51-4] no false sums were declared. After the expiry of the period of freedom the communal authorities were bound to report to the manor the names and surnames of all the settlers.[^51-6] To them also belonged the duty of watching over the boundaries between[^51-5] the lands of the settlement and the neighbouring lands.

[^51-1]: Among others in the Olęders of the Sieraków estate, further in the Białokoskie Olęders, Daleszyno, Dymaczewskie, Karpickie, Kuślińskie Olęders, Łokacz, Podgradowice, Proszkowskie, Puszczykowskie Olęders, Ręcko, Różnowskie Olęders, Tarnowo.
[^51-2]: In Zakrzewo (Rel. Posn. no. 626, 1788, fol. 406), in the Stobnickie Olęders (AW. B. 991).
[^51-3]: AW. Jaromierz, B. I.
[^51-4]: For example in the Radoszkowskie Olęders. 5 In the Olęders of the Sieraków estate. 6 In Ol. Charłub, Komorowskie, Włościejewskie (call numbers in Appendix I).
[^51-5]: In Ol. Włościejewskie. 4
[^51-6]: In Ol. Charłub, Komorowskie, Włościejewskie (call numbers in Appendix I).

[p. 52]

The competences of the communal organs in their own sphere comprised two kinds of matters: communal administration and communal judicature. The administrative provisions binding the members of the community were gathered together in the so-called wilkierze (Wilküre). Although issued at different times and by[^52-1][^52-2] different lords, in their content they are almost identical, which[^52-3][^52-4] indicates that they were written according to a common pattern, or rather that the later wilkierze were modelled on the wilkierze written earlier. Comparative research into the origin of the norms contained in the wilkierze would exceed the framework of the present work; in any case it seems a certain thing that these norms are based on foreign models. We quote one of the wilkierze in full in the appendix. From the wilkierze there shines forth a strong sense of communal solidarity. A considerable number of the provisions contained in them are designed to remove the sources of discord between the individual members of the community, or bind them to help one another in need. The latter, moreover, lay in the Olęders' own interest, for the community answered for the manorial duties usually solidarily; the property situation and solvency of a neighbour could therefore be a matter of indifference to none of its members. Control over the farming of the individual members of the community belonged to the important functions of the communal administration. The communal organs were even bound to remove by force an Olęder who farmed badly and to fill the farm with another. That this duty was in fact performed is proved by the note on p. 38. In some settlements the individual farmers were bound to report to the communal authorities the more important legal acts accomplished by themselves (contracts of sale and purchase, wills, bequests etc.). These acts were entered in a special book kept by the self-governing organs for the time being. Several such books have survived;3 they contain much interesting material on the life of the settlements. At times the legal acts entered in the community books had to be presented for additional lordly approval. On that occasion the lord collected certain fees.4

[^52-1]: There have survived wilkierze: for Ol. Chromiec (AW. A. I, 1748), the Gębickie Olęders (AW. A. 4, 1730), Góździn (AW. A. 2, 1730), Huta (AW. C. 2, 1730 (?)), the Nowodąbrowskie, or Jastrzębnickie, Olęders (AW. B. I, 1775), Ol. Kotusz (AW. A. 1, date lacking), Ol. Nowe Tuchorskie (AW. A. I, 1745), Ol. Rataje (AW. A. I, 1741), Złotków (AW. B. 3).
[^52-2]: Presumably the community presented to the lord a pattern already ready for approval. Formally, however, as follows from the tenor of the wilkierz, the issuer of it was the lord and not the community. Of a different opinion is Schmidt, but he does not justify his view.
[^52-3]: For Herbardowo (AW. C. 2), Folsztyn (AW. dep. C. 9), Łokacz (AW. dep. C. 4), the Stróżewskie Olęders (AW. C. 2).
[^52-4]: For example in the Lubaskie Olęders (Kl. XIT, 6. V, fol. 116).

[p. 53]

The communal judicature comprised above all cases of offences against the provisions of the wilkierz and the administrative orders of the communal organs, further all other cases (quarrels, brawls, thefts, boundary disputes) apart from matters of greater importance reserved to the lord. To the competence of the village court belonged also cases of offences against the provisions of the location charter.1 It is possible that the village court also performed the functions of an arbitration court. According to the charter for Borui the village judges "will look most diligently to order, security[^53-1] and tranquillity, indeed to success, order and justice, and will apply all diligence and earnestness[^53-2] of their own to it". The punishment applied by the court was the fine, and in exceptional cases arrest 3. From the judgments of the village court the condemned always had an appeal[^53-3] to the manor. So that the means of appeal should not be abused, the wilkierze provided that "whoever appeals out of spite and needlessly, groundlessly troubling the lord, shall be punished double".[^53-4] While in the sphere of administration the sołtys usually had wider competences than the remaining communal organs, in the panel of judges every member had equal rights.[^53-5] An accused member of the court was judged by the remaining sworn members in the same way as any other inhabitant of the settlement. The wilkierze defined the judicial procedure in detail. In Góździn the sittings of the court were to take place every 2 weeks, in the Jastrzębnickie Olęders every 4 weeks, on a Sunday. The members of the community were bound to appear at every summons of the court, to give testimony and to respect unconditionally all the other judgments and orders issued by the court. Special police provisions were designed to secure the safety and dignity of the court during the hearings. Women as a rule had no procedural capacity, nor could they be present at the place where the court sat. Exceptionally they could appear in court when their own affairs were being decided and their personal appearance was necessary. Also by way of exception they could substitute for their husbands in urgent matters, in the event of their absence or grave illness. But if they presumed to hurl insults before the court, a severe punishment awaited them. The execution of the judgments pronounced in the village court was carried out probably by the sołtys with the backing of the lord. The community poss-

[^53-1]: "Should an Olęder also oppose any point placed in this law, he is to be judged by the sołtys and the ławniks and punished duly" (Rel. Cost. no. 223, 1787, fol. 182, Ol. Charłub).
[^53-2]: Rel. Cost. no. 218, 1785, fol. 221.
[^53-3]: Cf. the wilkierze.
[^53-4]: The wilkierz for Ol. Chromiec.
[^53-5]: According to the charter for Ol. Sikorka (AW. B. 9I1): "no matter shall the sołtys judge by himself alone, but only together with the ławniks, on pain of nullity of the decree".

[p. 54]

sessed its own seal at times, with which the communal organs sealed the official letters and documents4.[^54-1]

For their administrative and judicial acts the communal organs received a certain remuneration, usually from the community, exceptionally from the lord. This remuneration comprised certain fixed annual fees (from the farm 2[^54-2], or from the unit of land[^54-3]), further fees for the individual complaints (Klugegeld).[^54-4] The costs of the journeys of the sołtys or the ławniks on the community's business were borne by all the farmers proportionally. In the Stare Oberskie Olęders the lord exempted each sołtys for the time being from all labour dues 5.[^54-5] c. The communal treasury, insurance against fire. The revenues from the administrative fees and judicial fines formed the communal chest. The receipts were kept in a special box kept by the sołtys, of which the ławniks held the key,[^54-6] or, which happened rarely, the lord.[^54-7] Before the expiry of the term of office each sołtys with the ławniks made up the annual accounts, either before the whole community or at the manor. The revenues from the judicial fines usually fell in their entirety to the lord;[^54-8] at times, however, the lord reserved to himself only a part of the revenues,[^54-9] or else applied them to some definite purpose, e.g. the maintenance of the ditches within the settlement.[^54-10] It is possible that in the communal chest there were also kept, where they existed, funds for the maintenance of the communal herdsman, the night watchman, the teacher or the lector. The chest revenues could also be applied to replenishing the so-called fire chest. Such a chest was founded on the lord's order, among others, in Ol. Radoszkowskie.[^54-11]

[^54-1]: Cf. Goldmann, Uber die Siegel, and Gumowski, Herby i pieczęcie.
[^54-2]: In the Budziszewskie Olęders (AW. B. 110) each farmer paid 1 złoty a year.
[^54-3]: In the Nowodąbrowskie Olęders 1 grosz per morg (cf. the wilkierz).
[^54-4]: In Ol. Chromiec each party paid 12 szelągs per complaint; of this the sołtys took 4 szelągs and the rest the panel of judges. In Ol. Nowodąbrowskie (Jastrzębnickie) the fee was 0 grosz on each occasion. Of course the losing party was ordered to reimburse the costs to the opponent.
[^54-5]: AGH. court of Wolsztyn, the Obra estate, vol. IV, fol. 138.
[^54-6]: So it was, for example, in Góździn (AW. A. 1), in Kawczyn (Rel. Cost. no. 177, 1742, fol. 212), in Rataje (Rel. Posn. no. 640, 1791, fol. 249).
[^54-7]: In the settlements of the Sieraków estate.
[^54-8]: This is expressly noted in the charter for the Cisiogórskie Olęders (Rel. Cost. no. 224, 1788, fol. 379). The money fines imposed in 1784 in Ol. Terespotockie even represented a quite serious item in the annual revenues of the lord from the Olęders (AGH, court of Grodzisk, the Opalenica estate, vol. III, fol. 20).
[^54-9]: In the Olęders of the Sieraków estate the community kept the 5th grosz (20%), and the lord took the rest.
[^54-10]: In Ol. Snowidowo (Rel. Posn. no. 620, 1787, fol. 27).
[^54-11]: Rel. Cost. no. 215, 1782, fol. 351.

[p. 55]

CHAPTER IV

The economic conditions of the Olęder settlements
A. Topography of the settlements. Forms of settlement. Building

I. Topography of the settlements. Olęder colonization was in its entirety agricultural colonization. The configuration of the ground on which the settlements were founded, and the character and productivity of the soil, exercised a direct influence on their economic life, and at the same time exercised a formative influence upon certain manifestations of the material culture of the Olęders, such as the manner of settling (the forms of settlement), building and the standard of agriculture. In view of the foregoing factors, all the Olęder settlements can be divided into two categories: lowland settlements, founded in river valleys and on the banks of standing waters, and settlements situated on dry ground, brought into being by clearing. Intermediate forms, of course, also occurred. The morphology of the lands occupied by the settlements presents, on the whole, a uniform appearance. Almost all the settlements seated themselves upon the outwash plains and dunes of the frontal moraine, frayed by the waters of the ice age. Only around Nowy Tomyśl did the reach[^55-1][^55-2] of settlement embrace also a part of the flat ground moraine. The soil of settlements of either type was of no great economic value. In the lowland settlements it was, on the side of the water basin, as a rule marshy, more rarely alluvial (the so-called mady), while on the slopes of the valleys it passed into a system of sandy or stony dunes and elevations of the glacial moraine. The soil of the settlements of the second category, sandy and barren over almost its whole extent, only exceptionally rose to medium quality. In the Prussian classification records the soil of the settlements there described is constantly characterized as poor and unfruitful[^55-3]. Even today the districts where

[^55-1]: Hładyłowicz, Zmiany krajobrazu, p. 23 ff. and the sketch-maps; Maas, Die Posener Hauländereien, p. 113.
[^55-2]: Meitzen, Der Boden, I, p. 239 ff.; Miklaszewski, Gleby ziem polskich; Kielczewska, Osadnictwo wiejskie, pp. 106–7; Hładyłowicz, Zamiany krajobrazu, p. 27 ff.; Pawłowski Stanisław, O kształtach powierzchni i podziale Wielkopolski na krainy, Bad. geograficzne, Zesz. 6–7, Poznań 1931, p. 148.
[^55-3]: E.g. in the Suzeleckie Olęders: “Der Acker wäre sandigt und schlecht und könnten die Bauern den 3-ten Theil davon recht gebrauchen” [The soil is very sandy and poor, and the farmers could make good use of the third part of it] (Kl. XII 6 II, fol. 97). Matters were little better in the other settlements. The same is affirmed by Schmidt (Geschichte, p. 398).

[p. 56]

Olęder settlement developed most vigorously (Babimost, Buk, Chodzież, Czarnków, Międzychód, Międzyrzecz, Poznań, Śrem) have the least fertile soils of the whole voivodeship.[^56-1] Only on the outwash plain of Nowy Tomyśl do we meet with soil of average fertility. This fact finds its explanation in the historical development of settlement within the area here discussed. The fertile lands had long since been settled by the local population, which, growing in numbers, occupied later, in part, also the less fertile lands. The colonists of the Middle Ages likewise chose lands as fertile as possible. The stock of land left at the Olęders' disposal therefore constituted the least valuable remnant remaining after the selection of land carried out by the settlement of many centuries.

2. Forms of settlement. In correspondence with the topographical conditions there developed two basic forms of Olęder settlement: the type of lowland settlement presenting itself as a street village, and the type of settlement[^56-2]. In both kinds of settlement the arrangement and disposition of the farm buildings is bound up closely with the character and shape of the piece, or pieces, of land belonging to the individual farms. The lands of the farms comprised in a lowland settlement consisted of a series of elongated strips running at right angles to the edge of the waters or of the morass. Such a strip of land began at the lowest point, by the body of water, and, running up the slope, ended upon dry ground lying higher. Here, at the top, parallel to the edge of the waters or of the bog, ran the road linking together the whole series of strips of land lying next to one another and belonging to the individual farms. Along this road, at the highest-lying end of the land, the Olęders raised their farm buildings. As a rule these stood on the same side of the road on which the land lay, but they could also stand on the opposite side, in which case the road separated them from the lands of the farm. Typical settlements of this kind are the oldest Olęders of the Wieleń estate. In the later period street villages occur also upon dry ground, and the lands are there laid out less regularly.

The settlement of the second type presented itself otherwise. Here the lord allowed the Olęders to clear for themselves lands of a defined size within some larger area of forest or pasture, while every Olęder as a

[^56-1]: Cf. Meitzen, op. cit., and Hładyłowicz, p. 30.
[^56-2]: Kuhn, Die deutschen Siedlungsformen; Maas, Entstehung; idem, Über deutsche Dorfformen in Posen; Martiny, Formen der Siedlungen. Cf. also Ebert, Ländliche Siedelformen im deutschen Osten. The author goes beyond the limits laid down by his title, occupying himself also with western Wielkopolska. There, on p. 43, we find an extensive German-language literature of the subject. Of the old maps we have made use here of MGd. and of a number of maps of the individual districts or estates, preserved in the State Archive in Poznań.

[p. 57]

rule chose for himself the spot convenient for clearing. For economic reasons he endeavoured to prepare for cultivation a piece of land lying in one block, and that in a form approximating a square or else a polygon. For the same reasons he set his buildings at the most convenient point, most often in the middle of the cleared land, without regard to the position of the neighbouring farms. He linked his farm by a separate way to the main track running through the settlement or at the least distance from it. Cartographic examples of both forms of settlement are given at the end of the book.

Beyond the two described categories of settlement there occurred Olęder settlements of other types. These arose in a twofold manner. In the first case, when an existing village was transferred to Olęder law, or the Olęders bought up a village left empty together with its buildings, or else, taking over a deserted village already without buildings, restored its original shape, there arose an Olęder settlement in the form existing previously. In the second case, when the Olęders had to conform to the special requirements of the lord, there arose a planned settlement, of shapes traced out in an artificial manner.[^57-1] 3. Building. In the sources we find scanty information on the manner of erecting the farm buildings in the Olęder settlements, on their shape and dimensions, and on the material used in their construction. In principle the complex of buildings belonging to one farm consisted of a dwelling cottage, a barn and a byre. Cart-sheds are met with more rarely. The byre was often built on to the cottage; exceptionally a second byre, or a separate granary, entered into the complex of buildings. The cottage was built either of logs or of sawn boards, or as a clay-walled cottage, and so likewise the byre and the barn. There also occurred buildings simply put together out of brushwood.[^57-2] Part of a building might be of timber, while the remainder was plastered with clay. For the most part the buildings had a covering of straw (sheaves), more rarely of reed, and exceptionally the more considerable buildings were roofed with shingles.[^57-3]

[^57-1]: According to the charter for the Krosien (AW. Krosna, A. 2) the Olęders “are to build in a line, in the order appointed for them by me (i.e. the lord)”. In the Kiączyńskie Olęders (AW. A. 1) “the buildings are to be set up along the ditch by which the water has from of old been accustomed to flow to the great pond called Kiełpin... in good array and order, so that they form a village”. In Góździn (AW. A. I, fol. 71) “I do not permit them to build otherwise than in a cluster, in the likeness of other villages”. In the Borkowskie Olęders (AW. A. 1) the Olęders “ought to build themselves up as is proper, and their homesteads are not to be built far one from another, but in two rows, so that on one row stand the dwelling houses, on the other the barns”. The same in the Dymaczewskie Olęders (Rel. Posn. nr 489, 1751, fol. 69).
[^57-2]: In Kawczyn (Rel. Cost. nr 196, 1761, fol. 477).
[^57-3]: Cf. the descriptions of the buildings of the Ol. Borkowskie, Ol. Krosińskie, Krosien, Ol. Nowe and Ol. Sowińskie (Rel. Posn. nr 632, 1790, fol. 47 ff.), of Kawczyn (Rel. Cost. nr 196, 1761, fol. 476) and of the Ol. Radoszkowskie (Rel. Cost. nr 197, 1762, fol. 82).

[p. 58]

5 OO The buildings were, on the whole, in good condition. To the farm there belonged also a yard fenced on all sides.[^58-1] In many instances the lords made, in the founding charter, stipulations as to the manner in which the future buildings were to be erected. In the Ol. Charłub every dwelling house “ought to be built properly, of two chambers and with a façade”. In the Ol. Dymaczewskie “those homesteads which they[^58-2] are to build ought to be spacious, built fundamentally of squared logs, and set out according to a regular plan”.[^58-3] Elsewhere it was stipulated that the Olęders should “erect proper buildings upon foundation sills”.[^58-4] It may be concluded with great probability that timber buildings were more frequent in the settlements which arose upon clearings, where building material was easier to come by.

B. Occupational and social composition

The occupational composition of the Olęders. Among the population of the Olęder settlements we must distinguish: 1. the Olęder farmers, who sat on lands received from the lord or bought up, and who stood with the lord in a direct economic and legal relation regulated in the founding charter; 2. the remaining population, economically dependent upon the Olęders[^58-5], such as, for example, certain craftsmen, the journeymen etc.; 3. it is also possible that in one and the same settlement there dwelt persons who did not belong to the communal society of the Olęders. Such was the case in the instances of mixed settlements already discussed. It could also happen that within an Olęder settlement there lived someone belonging to the manorial household service.[^58-6]

[^58-1]: Kawczyno, the file as above.
[^58-2]: Rel. Cost. nr 223, 1790, fol. 182.
[^58-3]: Rel. Posn. nr 489, 1751, fol. 69.
[^58-4]: The Ol. Wymyślanka, Rel. Posn. nr 641, 1792, fol. 260; the Ol. Węgielna, ibid., fol. 824. Similarly in the Ol. Komorowskie, Rel. Posn. nr 645, 1793, fol. 102.
[^58-5]: As a rule the Olęders were unrestricted in the keeping of the labour forces they needed (journeymen, lodgers). Only now and then did the lord stipulate that the Olęders were not to keep about them “bad folk (vagabonds, thieves), through whom harm might come upon the community” (charter for the Lubaskie Olęders of 1773, Kl. XII 6 V, fol. 116). In exceptional instances the lord himself drew direct profit from the fact of lodgers being kept in the settlement. In the Jędrzejewskie Olęders each of them had to work off for the lord, during the harvest, one day in the week (charter of 1763, Kl. XII 13, fol. 239); in the Radoskie Olęders every lodger was bound to pay to the manor an annual rent in the amount of 1 złoty 15 groszy (charter of 1751, AM. Sieraków, GC. 53, fol. 79). A similar provision we meet with in the charter of 1788 for the Trojanowskie Olęders: “No one shall keep lodgers with their livestock without an agreement with the manor concerning rent and labour dues” (AW. B. 1037).
[^58-6]: Cases of this kind are known to us in only two instances: in Przyborowo (AGH sąd Szamotuły, Tax Acten fol. 46) and in the Kwiejce Nowe (Kl. XII 6 II, fol. 43). In both these settlements there lived, outside the community, manorial forest guards.

[p. 59]

Of course such inhabitants did not belong to the Olęder community, and consequently do not form the object of our considerations.

The population of the Olęder settlements was differentiated by occupation only to a small degree. Nearly all the members of the community were employed in agriculture, whether as their principal occupation or at least incidentally. Exceptionally this did not apply to the communal herdsmen, whose basis of subsistence was the rearing of domestic animals; it must nevertheless be emphasized here that stock-raising in the settlements did not pass beyond the bounds of a subsidiary occupation alongside the proper agricultural economy; among the Olęders we nowhere meet with farms devoted specially to stock-raising. Likewise only exceptionally do we encounter craftsmen who at the same time were not engaged in agriculture.

The most important source for the occupational composition of the population of the Olęder settlements of Kościan county is the Tol. It was drawn up according to the scheme laid down by the Crown Treasury Commission, which provided for the division of the rural population into the following groups: 1. farmer peasants (gospodarze rolnicy), 2. cottagers, 3. lodgers, 4. manufacturers, 5. craftsmen,[^59-1] 6. tapsters and innkeepers, 7. journeymen and servants, 8. the unattached “busied with work”, 9. beggars. In practice there was usually indicated as well the economic and legal relation of the inhabitants to the lord, there being added as need arose: “okupnicy” (holders by purchase), “czynszownicy” (rent-payers), “kontraktowi” (contract-holders). In the Olęder villages there was usually given: “Olęder farmers”, “okupni”, “okupnicy”, “czynszowi (czynszownicy)”. This whole scheme leaves much to be desired, for it mixes occupational with social categories, and is thus incomplete. Further, the figures contained in the table inspire far less confidence than, for example, the figures of an inventory or of a lustration, for they are nowhere explained in detail. The categories of persons ad 1, 2, 3, 7 and 8 in the Olęder settlements were without doubt occupied with agriculture. At point 4 we have a term that is rather obscure; the occupational allegiance of the persons classed here must be decided in each individual case. Point 9, as one may easily perceive on glancing into the table, the lustrators interpreted very broadly. Besides undoubted beggars there were assigned to this group all the old persons incapable of work who remained at the charge of the community (widows, orphans), and presumably also other persons who could not be brought under any of the preceding categories. These persons we too have counted among the agricultural population, as occupationally passive. Besides, this column supplements the column “the unattached at work”; the lustrators, as may be gathered from the table, did not in the majority of cases themselves very well know which of the two columns to give precedence.[1] On approximately the same principles the hearth tables were at the same time drawn up. One such table,

[^59-1]: Thus, for example, in the Starojastrzębskie Olęders, out of a total of 282 souls they entered 50 in the column “beggars”, and none at all in the column “the unattached at work”.

[p. 60]

60 : of which mention has already been made, has survived for Kościan county (Tod. 89). The hearths are there divided into the following categories: I. “manor houses and manorial buildings, that is to say the palace, the outbuildings, the folwarks, the stables, the sheepfolds, reckoned as one hearth”; 2. “the rectory and other parsonage buildings”; 3. “monasteries, monks, nuns”; 4. “breweries”; 5. “taverns”; 6. “mills”; 7. “Jewish hearths”; 8. “hearths in village cottages, futors and forest cottages possessing land”: a. “a cottage with two farmers”, b. “a cottage with one farmer”; 9. “hearths in cottages and garden cottages”: a. “a garden cottage”, b. “a cottage without land and without garden”; 10. “hearths in the houses where craftsmen dwell”: a. “manufacturing hearths”, b. “craftsmen's hearths”. Table II presents the occupational composition of the population of the Olęder settlements of Kościan county, computed on the basis of the population table, supplemented as need arose by the hearth table.

The corresponding source for Poznań county is lacking. The occupational and social composition of the inhabitants of the settlements seized in 1772 is furnished by the Prussian classification records. Another, still earlier source, namely the inventory of the Sieraków estate of 1759, contains a detailed specification of the population of the eight Olęder settlements belonging to that estate. Both sources, as far as their lists of population are concerned, are considerably more complete than the Tol.; they do not, however, agree with it in point of time. It may nevertheless be assumed with great probability that in 1759–89 no deep, fundamental changes occurred in the occupational and social composition of the Olęder population of the settlements enumerated above; on this assumption we have brought together the data of the two sources in Table III. A comparison of the percentage results of the two tables will reveal certain differences, which however become minimal if we take into account that in Tab. II the shepherds, given separately in Tab. III, have been assigned to the agricultural population, as have also part of the innkeepers, the millers and the teachers or readers. In both tables the dominant significance of agriculture in the Olęder villages is striking. The figure of those engaged in agriculture was in reality still larger, for account must be taken of the fact that a considerable part of the members of the families classed as occupationally passive in other occupations was likewise employed in agriculture, if not constantly, then at least in the seasons of more pressing work. It may therefore be safely assumed that the number of persons employed in agriculture (active and passive) reached 98% of the total Olęder population.

2. Social composition. Different figures will be obtained if we attempt to reconstruct the social division of the Olęder population. In pre-partition Poland the broadest framework for social stratification was furnished by estate membership. Within each estate, however, social po-

ing stratum we shall assign the economically independent units; a socially lower category will be formed by those units which, having insufficient inco-

[^62-1]: The source mixes the cottagers with the lodgers. These two strata cannot be strictly distinguished on the basis of the data of the classification records; the terminology of the source has been adopted in the table as the basis of the distinction.
[^62-2]: Innkeepers and millers.

[p. 63]

mes from their own workshops are forced to supplement them by work upon another's farm, while the lowest social category will be formed by those who live exclusively by hired labour upon the farms of others. Among the peasants these are the strata designated in the Tol. as “journeymen”, “the unattached at work” and “beggars”. A factor indirectly causing the social differentiation of the peasant stratum was the legal position of its individual members. A peasant personally free had far greater chances of attaining economic independence, if only for the reason that, being independent of the lord upon whose land he wished to settle, he could in the contract put forward his own demands and in this way reach agreement upon terms more favourable to himself. This applies particularly to the population of the Olęder settlements. This population belonged as a whole to the estate of the peasants,[1][^63-1] and was also, in its entirety at least — so far as the Olęder farmers proper were concerned — free of personal subjection. For lack of more precise bases for reconstructing the whole social hierarchy of this population, it was necessary to limit oneself to a simplified division into the “economically independent”, the “intermediate” and the “economically dependent”. To depict the social structure of the Olęder population within these broadest limits there will serve us the population and hearth tables of 1789 and 1790 already employed in the discussion of the occupational division. To the independent must be assigned the farmers, innkeepers and millers enumerated there. Their independence was decided by the fact of their possessing sufficiently large farms. It is harder to determine the social position of the craftsmen. Some of them were endowed with land, which placed them on a level with the independent stratum. The isolation of the craftsmen is, however, a very difficult matter. In general, although they worked in their own workshops, their material situation arranged itself variously, according to the local demand for skilled work or for finished products. Under these conditions it will be most appropriate to assign them to the “intermediate” social stratum. The remaining occupational strata, that is all the landless, among them those performing cultural[^63-2] and religious functions,

[^63-1]: The szlachta settled in the Olęder settlements is recorded by the sources in only three cases. In Krzyżewniki, which moreover were not of the pure Olęder-settlement type, there sat in 1795 “Joh. v. Schavelsky, Joseph v. Ratkiewicz” and “v. Monkowska” (Kl. M. Krzyżewniki); in the Brodnickie Olęders in 1775 “Pan Jan Gruntowski” is mentioned (Rel. Cost. nr 208, 1775, fol. 800); finally, in 1787 the sołtys of the Chrząstowskie Olęders enrolled in the Kościan grod court a manifesto against “the well-born Stanisław Kossowski, co-inhabitant of those same Olęders”, charging him with having bought a farm in the settlement without the knowledge of the community and without paying the lord the tenth grosz (Rel. Cost. nr 220, 1787, fols. 524 and 530). Whether, however, the szlachta mentioned belonged to the commune of the Olęders seems doubtful. Presumably it made a separate agreement with the manor.
[^63-2]: See further, pp. 113–117.

[p. 64]

must be assigned to the dependent. The social division of the population carried out on the basis of the foregoing considerations will present itself as follows:
