For lack of comparative figures for the other settlements of Wielkopolska we shall limit ourselves to confronting the results obtained here with the figures concerning the social stratification of the population of the royal estates in Prussia.[^64-1] The socio-occupational structure of that population was in many respects analogous to the structure of the population of the Olęder settlements of Kościan and Poznań counties.

[^64-1]: Cf. Rusiński Władysław, Uwarstwienie ludności wiejskiej w królewszczyznach Prus Królewskich w drugiej połowie XVII w., Roczniki dziejów społecznych i gospodarczych, vol. VI, 1937, p. 111.

[p. 65]

C. The individual branches of the Olęder economy

The full sweep of the economic activity of the Olęders will show itself most completely against the background of their share in the social income of a given estate.[^65-1] The division of the Olęders' income according to the different branches of production will lead to a picture of the individual branches of their economy; the division of income between the Olęders and the lord will be connected, on the one hand, with the distinguishing of the income from their own land and of the profits from the lord's land (the servitudes), and, on the other, with a discussion of the manorial dues. The share of the state and of the church in the Olęders' incomes will present itself to us as the public and ecclesiastical dues. Finally, the division of income between the individual settlers will require, among other things, an examination of their endowment with land and of the social structure of the individual occupations. In discussing the economic activity of the Olęders it will be indispensable to distinguish its twofold character: technical and socio-economic.

I. Agriculture. a. The agricultural technique of the Olęders, like the forms of settlement, developed along two paths. In the lowland settlements it consisted, in the first period, in the improvement of the land through drainage, in the second in the keeping, in a state fit for cultivation, of land once drained. In the settlements of the second type it consisted in the clearing of forests and thickets, and subsequently in raising the fertility of the land thus stripped of wood and in defending it against the renewed invasion of weeds and scrub. The considerable number of Olęder settlements justifies the supposition that the Olęders were able to rise to their task. They were enabled to do so by the multi-year exemptions from all dues granted them by the lord (the wolnizny),[^65-2] while the prospect of a free, independent existence upon land bought by themselves doubtless spurred the Olęders on to greater efforts. Not always, however, did the settlers receive as their lot the improvement of the lands allotted to them by the lord. In many instances they took over arable lands already manured before them by the Polish peasant, sometimes even already with buildings and crops. In column 6 of Annex I there is described the economic character of the land in the individual settlements at the moment of the founding of the Olęders. Only certain cases, clearly discussed in the founding charters or in other sources, have been recorded. The terms “village”, “folwark”, “ready-made arable land” signify that the Olęders here took over land already prepared for cultivation, whether after a village or folwark existing there before, or else were settled upon part of the lands of a settlement still continuing in existence. The improvement of the land naturally had no connexion with the rise of the settlement in such cases, likewise when an already existing settlement was transferred to Olęder

[^65-1]: Cf. Rutkowski, Badania nad podziałem dochodów, the introductory chapter.
[^65-2]: Cf. p. 84.

[p. 66]

law. It was made easier also in the cases in which the Olęders took over a “village gone over to scrub”. After allowing for the lands partly overgrown, the data contained in Annex I will express themselves in the following figures:

TABLE V

The character of the lands upon which the Olęder settlements arose

| Counties | Total number of settlements recorded in the sources | settlements founded upon ready-made (arable) land | settlements founded upon partly overgrown land | settlements founded upon overgrown land (cleared by the Olęders) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **in absolute figures** | | | | |
| Kościan county | 44 | 20 | 3 | 21 |
| Poznań county | 78 | 17 | 3 | 58 |
| **Total** | **122** | **37** | **6** | **79** |
| **in percentages** | | | | |
| Kościan county | — | 45.5 | 6.8 | 47.7 |
| Poznań county | — | 21.8 | 3.8 | 74.4 |
| **Total** | — | **30.3** | **4.9** | **64.8** |

In the light of these figures there fade the assertions of German scholarship concerning the pioneering role of the colonists of German nationality in the tillage of the soils of western Wielkopolska. At the same time these figures undermine the assertions of Maas concerning the great afforestation of the Wielkopolskan landscape in the period before modern colonization[^66-1], and justify the critical position of Hładyłowicz.[^66-2]

The drainage of the land was carried out in such a way that the water was either conducted by ditches to a lower-lying basin (a river, a lake), or else collected in deep ditches and pits. In the charters we occasionally find assurances that these ditches or pits would not be counted in to the Olęders in the measurement of their lands.[^66-3] Exceptionally the Olęders were permitted to dig ditches even through the land of others.[^66-4] The clearing of wooded land was accomplished probably for the most part by the felling and grubbing-up of trees and bushes. Clearing by burning was in-

[^66-1]: Maas, Entwaldung, pp. 23–5.
[^66-2]: Zmiany krajobrazu, pp. 19–20.
[^66-3]: E.g. in the charter for the Ol. Milczewskie (AW. A. 1).
[^66-4]: Such a provision we find in the charters of 1677 for the Olęders of the Wieleń estate.

[p. 67]

deed also practised, but presumably more rarely, by reason of the certain value which felled timber must always have represented for an Olęder. The lands, doubtless at the settlers' request, were almost always measured out to them in a single block. This property was characteristic, moreover, of the other types of modern settlement in the area here discussed. Exceptionally an Olęder farm possessed lands in two blocks, or even in three, usually when the settlers had bought up deserted peasant farms. For the most part, as the old maps show, the lands were divided into two fields: winter and spring. Probably part of each field lay fallow for a time, so that the system of land cultivation most frequently practised by the Olęders was the three-course system.[^67-1] It is also possible that upon the better lands the improved three-course system was employed at least in part. In many settlements of particularly poor soil the fallow exceeded ⅓ of the total area of the lands; the land had to lie fallow for several years before it could again be taken under cultivation.

b. Endowment with land. The share of the individual settlers in the social income was decided above all by their endowment with land. This was usually reckoned in Chełmno włókas (hubas). The włóka was divided into[^67-2] [—] morgs; the morg contained 300 square rods (30×10). The rod, called also the laska or the różga, was of unequal length; it ranged from 7½ ells (the rod of the new or Warsaw measure)[5] to 9 ells[^67-3] (the margravial rod).[6] In an isolated instance we meet with a mention of lands measured with the Magdeburg rod,[^67-4] but there are no data as to how many ells it contained. Most often the lands were measured[^67-5] with the rod of 8 and of 9 ells, most rarely with that of 7½ ells. The matter[^67-6] was further complicated by the fact that 1 ell itself was of unequal length. The sources mention ells of the Chełmno, Polish (old), German and Poznań measures, but do not define these measures more closely. By a

[^67-1]: Holsche, Geographie u. Statistik, II, p. 493.
[^67-2]: AW. Łoskońskie Stare Ol. B. 365.
[^67-3]: AW. Milczewskie Ol., A. 1: “The said włókas they are free to divide into two or three fields according to their own wish”. The same AW. Strzeleckie Ol., C, 3, fol. 9.
[^67-4]: The Ol. Bielawy, Kl. XII, 6 V, fol. 134: “Der Acker sey sehr sandig und bestünde nur in ausgerodeter Waldung, welcher, wenn solcher einige Jahre besät worden wäre, einige Zeit wieder unbeackert müsse liegen bleiben” [The soil is very sandy and consists only of grubbed-up woodland, which, once it has been sown for some years, must again lie unploughed for a time]. The same was the case in the Ol. Jasionna, Obelżanki and Rzecień. Cf. also Schmidt, Geschichte, p. 398.
[^67-5]: Rel. Cost. nr 220, 1787, fol. 371, Ostrowo, AW. Stare Łoskońskie Ol., B. 365.
[^67-6]: Rel. Cost. nr 222, 1789, fol. 348, the Ol. Gogolewskie: “I permit the same Hollendry to measure out their lands with the margravial rod, that is, the rod of nine ells”. Stamm (Miary powierzchni, p. 9 ff.) in his deliberations takes into account only the rod of 7½ ells.[7] AW. Lewice, dep. A. I (1732).

[p. 68]

decree of the sejm of 1764 it was fixed at 24 inches (59.55 cm). According to Stamm[^68-1] the Chełmno ell, probably identical with the old or German ell mentioned in the sources, was somewhat shorter, measuring 57.6 cm.[^68-2] If we take this last ell as our basis, the area of a włóka measured with the rod of 7½ ells will come to 16.80 ha; of 8 ells — 19.10 ha; of 8½ ells — 21.60 ha; of 9 ells — 24.20 ha.

Besides the Chełmno włóka other measures were sporadically employed. In the neighbourhood of Międzychód the land was measured in ślads.[^68-3] From the mention in the charter for the Ol. Marienwald defining the area of the settlement as “seventeen ślads, 13 morgs, 107 różgas, all in Chełmno ells and measure” we may infer that they were identical with the włóka (huba).[^68-4] It was otherwise in the starostwo of Rogoźno. Here too the lands were reckoned in ślads, but these amounted to 2 włókas, that is 60 morgs.[^68-5] Likewise in the neighbouring Wieleń estate the włóka was reckoned at 50 or even 60[^68-6] morgs of normal size.

The size of the farms in the Olęder settlements underwent considerable changes in the course of the colonization period. In the earlier settlements the norm was the włóka; alongside this, farms of an area of 1½ włókas were also created. More rarely do we meet with farms of other sizes: 2 włókas, ¼, ½ of a włóka.[^68-7] False is the assertion of W. Schulz that at the founding the size of all the farms was equal; one of the charters lays down, for example, that of the land “each of them (i.e. the Olęders) in that forest shall be bound to have one or two włókas”,[^68-8] while in another settlement 2 Olęders are settled upon one ślad, 2 upon 1½ of a ślad, 3 upon 1½ of a ślad.[^68-9] The lustration of the Wieleń estate of 1653 records that at Marianowo an Olęder sits upon 2 włókas, 6 upon 1 włóka and 5 upon ½ of a włóka. It is out of the question that so unequal a division of the lands in the settlement could have come about in the course of 50 years (from 1630 the population of the settlement increased by only 2 farmers).[9] An unequal endowment with land,[^68-10]

[^68-1]: Vol. Leg. VII 330.
[^68-2]: Stamm, pp. 13–17. The Chełmno ell — 57.6 cm; the ell of 1764 — 59.55 cm.
[^68-3]: AW. Marienwald, B. I.
[^68-4]: The so-called little ślads. Cf. Sochaniewicz, Ślad, p. 99 ff.
[^68-5]: AW. Gościejewskie Ol., B. 252; Rel. Posn. nr 628, 1789, fol. 204.
[^68-6]: Kat. I, fol. 59, the Ol. Herbardowo.
[^68-7]: E.g. the Ol. Piaski was founded upon a single włóka; 4 farmers were settled upon it (Rel. Posn. nr 641, 1792, fol. 240).
[^68-8]: AW. Dziewczastruga, B. 1028.
[^68-9]: AW. Orłowice, A. I.
[^68-10]: Cf. the lustration of the Wieleń estate of 1630, Rel. Posn. nr 157, 1632, fol. 170.

[p. 69]

especially at the founding of the later settlements, must even have been a fairly common thing, considering that the Olęders bought up the lands allotted to them, while the means of the individual settlers were doubtless unequal. The number and size of the farms changed continually[^69-1] as a result of their division by inheritance, and possibly also through partial sale.[^69-2] A change in the area of the farms was brought about likewise by the unlawful ploughing-in of the lord's lands. This was, as far as the Olęders were concerned, an abuse commonly practised, which the manor tried in vain to combat. The surveys of the Prussian period showed that the area of almost every farm represented a size with a fraction.[^69-3] In the Prussian classification records we meet with copies of a series of deeds of sale of lands long since ploughed in unlawfully and used by the Olęders.[^69-4] There occurred cases in which the Olęders enlarged the area of their farms at the cost of the public roads. Thus, for example, in 1752 Jan Gruszczyński, judge of Wałcz, enrolled in the Kościan grod court a manifesto against the Olęders of Kawczyn, complaining that they ploughed up the boundaries, the balks and the boundary mounds “not contenting themselves with their former acts of violence”, further, that the road leading from Jasień to Kościan they had so

[^69-1]: Kl. XII, 7, fol. 122.
[^69-2]: An example may serve the Ol. Folsztyn. According to the lustration of the Wieleń estate in 1630 there lived there 32 farmers upon 30 włókas. The lustration of 1653 already lists 38 farmers, of whom 2 sat upon 1½ włókas, 14 upon 1 włóka, 5 upon ¾ of a włóka, 12 upon ½ of a włóka and 1 upon ¼ of a włóka; altogether there fall 27 włókas. The diminution by 3 włókas resulted probably from the lands having been measured out with a larger rod. In 1773 (Kl. XIII, 7, fol. 122) Folsztyn numbered 50 farmers, of whom 1 sat upon one włóka, 2 upon ½ of a włóka, 42 upon ½ of a wł., 8 upon ⅜ of a wł., 3 upon ½ of a wł.; the settlement numbered altogether 27½ włókas. Finally, according to the lustration drawn up about 1800 (AM. Wieleń, C. 250, a copy of the lustration of the Wieleń estate deposited in the process records of 1800), there were in Folsztyn likewise 56 farmers, of whom 2 possessed whole włókas, 1 — ¼ of a wł., 44 — ½ of a wł., — ⅗ of a wł., 13 — ½ of a wł., which makes altogether 27⅝ włókas. As the comparison shows, in the meantime the average area of the farm had diminished by half, from 50 to 25 morgs. In reality the area of the farms in Folsztyn somewhat exceeded the round figures given, for in the period from the founding of the settlement to 1773 the individual farmers had ploughed in for themselves considerable pieces of land, sometimes reaching 15 morgs. Altogether the original area of the settlement, amounting to 27 włókas with a fraction, grew in this way by more than 4½ włókas.
[^69-3]: Thus, for example, in the Ol. Jaromierz Stary (AW. Jaromierz, B. 4) there existed in 1829 farms of 13, 15, 16, 17½, 18, 18½, 20, 20½, 21½, 22, 23½, 24 and 24½ morgs. In the Ol. Radoszkowskie the survey of the lands, still from the Polish period (in 1791), showed farms of 15, 19, 20, 24, 25, 32½ and 41 morgs, likewise, as in the preceding case, in Chełmno measure (Rel. Cost. nr 224, 1791, fol. 252).
[^69-4]: Cases of this kind are quoted also by the village records. In the Ol. Nowe Dwory, for example, Adam Sapieha in 1094 sells to 2 settlers land hitherto used by them unlawfully beyond the original extent. In 1702 Jan Kazimierz Sapieha sells to the Olęders the lands ploughed in before that, of an area of 3¾ włókas, forbidding them categorically for the future any further ploughing-in (AW. Nowe Dwory B. 1, fol. 110–11).

[p. 70]

ploughed up “that it was no longer possible for a broad wagon to drive through there any more; they had ploughed over the hillocks set out in the manner of boundary marks; in a row they had rooted out the great stones lying on the left side in the fields above the road running from Jasień to Kościan and had carted them off to Kawczyn; and so that the evidence itself — the documents plainly mark the boundaries — might be obscured”.[^70-1]

Against such ploughing-in of land the manor sought to protect itself in various ways: it threatened the guilty with severe punishments,[^70-2] it reserved to itself the power of carrying out at any moment a measurement of the lands,[^70-3] it bound the communal authority to carry out at intervals an inspection of the boundaries and of the boundary marks,[^70-4] or else to carry out measurements of the whole settlement.[^70-5] The same aim was served by the provisions concerning the obligatory dimensions of the boundary ditch or of the balk,[^70-7] or concerning the width of the public roads running[^70-6] through the lands of the settlement.

Attached to the farms there were as a rule gardens. Their size ranged from ¼ to 2 morgs. The ground upon which the buildings stood did not, as a rule, enter into the measurement of the lands,[^70-8] and neither, of course, did the boundaries and balks.[^70-9] Occasionally the lord added to every włóka a piece of meadow.[^70-10]

Sometimes the Olęders, being the sole users of their own lands, drew profit in common from the additional use of the common

[^70-1]: Rel. Cost. nr 187, 1752, fol. 337.
[^70-2]: Tarnowa, Rel. Cost. nr 155, 1719–20, fol. 141: “which lands are bounded by marks, beyond which no one shall be permitted to stray, on pain of a heavy fine”. Similarly in the Olęders of the Sieraków estate.
[^70-3]: AW. Puszczykowskie Ol., A. 1: “It shall be free (liberum) for the Lord Estate Owner to measure out the hubas anew whenever he may wish”.
[^70-4]: E.g. in Rakoniewice and Podgradowice, Rel. Cost. nr 164, 1729, fol. 177.
[^70-5]: In the Olęders of the Sieraków estate such a measurement was to take place every 6 years.
[^70-6]: According to the wilkierz for the Nowodąbrowskie Olęders a proper boundary ought to form a ditch ½ of a rod wide and 2 ells deep, or else a fence 2 ells high, so that a half-year-old calf could not leap over it. In the Ol. Kotusz the width of the balk between the lands of the individual farms was fixed at 1 foot (Rel. Cost. nr 164, 1729, fol. 485).
[^70-7]: According to the charter for the Ol. Zamorze the main village road ought to be 2 rods, and the side roads 1 rod, in width. The sołtys was to see to it that no one ploughed up the roads (Rel. Posn. nr 641, 1792, fol. 164).
[^70-8]: Sometimes, however, it was the other way about: in the Ol. Jaracz the settlers received the charter “each of them a włóka of land (into which forest, arable land, gardens, buildings and meadows enter), already measured out to him” (AW. B. 296).
[^70-9]: Góździn, AW. A. 1: “There is permitted them also... the free balks needed at every whole huba”. Karpicko, AW. A. 1: “The balks, roads and commons do not enter into the measurement of the hubas”.
[^70-10]: Thus, for example, in Lewice, AW. dep. A. 1, there were added to every huba, free of charge, 2 morgs of meadow for the Olęders.

[p. 71]

lands. Such lands, however, occurred rather rarely. They were almost always meadows or pastures.[^71-1]

c. Plant production. More exact data concerning plant production we possess only for the settlements seized by Prussia in the First Partition (of 1773) and for the settlements of the Sieraków estate. The data relating to the settlements in the area of the First Prussian Partition, contained in the Prussian classification records, are schematic and for that reason inspire less confidence; the data relating to the Sieraków estate, contained in the inventory of 1759 already several times mentioned, are on the whole set out conscientiously. As follows from the above sources, the plant production of the Olęders, owing to the poor soil, presented a most modest appearance.[^71-2] Wheat was not cultivated at all.[^71-3] Of the other cereals there were cultivated in the Sieraków estate[^71-4] rye, oats and barley, and among the other produce peas. In the settlements classified in 1773 production embraced sometimes also buckwheat and flax. The sowing per unit of land presented itself variously, according to how long the individual pieces of land had to lie fallow. In the Ol. Króteckie, for example, the land had to remain untilled for 6–8 years, and in the Kwiejce Nowe for 6–9 years.[^71-5] The average sowing in the Ol. Bielawy, Jasionna, Obelżanki and Rzecień, where there is given also a specification of the lands of the individual settlers, amounted, per włóka of 30 morgs, in korcy of the Berlin measure (⅛ of the korzec of Warsaw), to: 10 korcy of rye, 4 korcy of barley, 3[^71-6] korcy of oats, ⅛ of a korzec of peas, 1¼ of a korzec of buckwheat and 3 garnce of flax. On the whole, however, it would seem, more oats was cultivated than barley. The yield was 3 grains, sometimes still less, exceptionally 3½ grains.

In the gardens hops were occasionally grown, sometimes potatoes and carrots, but as a rule only for household use. Exceptionally, in the Ol. Kwiejce Nowe and Bronice,[8] where the cultivation of cereals was almost impossible owing to the very barren[^71-7] soil, the Olęders lived from the cultivation of potatoes and carrots, which they carted for sale to Drezdenko, distant 2 miles. In quite exceptional cases tobacco was grown in the gar-

[^71-1]: Common meadows or pastures were possessed by the Olęders, among others, in the Jaromierskie Nowe (AW. B. 4, fol. 63), the Jaromierskie Stare (AW. dep. A. 1), the Lubieńskie Olęders (AW. B. 1), Popowo (Rel. Cost. nr 174, 1739, fols. 1119 and 1122), Rataje (Rel. Posn. nr 640, 1791, fol. 247), Ręcko (Rel. Cost. nr 167, 1732, fol. 205).
[^71-2]: Schmidt himself (Geschichte, p. 398) depicts it in dark colours.
[^71-3]: It was cultivated in minimal quantity in other districts, as the data for the Ol. Kliny of 1793 seem to show (AW. C. 2). The sowing there amounted per włóka to: 10 korcy of rye,
[^71-4]: [—] korcy of barley, 5 korcy of oats, 1 korzec of wheat and 1 korzec of buckwheat.
[^71-5]: Kl. XII 6 V, fol. 32; 6 II, fol. 43.
[^71-6]: Kl. XII 6 V, fols. 132, 137, 142, 147.
[^71-7]: Kl. XII 6 II.

[p. 72]

dens. In the sources there is no information whether[^72-1], and to what measure, the Olęders reared fruit trees.

The plant production embraced also hay. It was, however, harvested not in all the settlements[^72-2] in very unequal quantities (in some of the Olęders of the Sieraków estate up to 15 loads upon a one-włóka farm), so that it is difficult to give here any average figures.

d. The rearing of domestic animals, in contrast to plant production, developed in the Olęder settlements on the whole successfully. Draft animals, it is true, were kept, in connexion with the small agricultural production, in numbers not very great (on a one-włóka farm on the average 3 or 4 oxen, or else 2 oxen and one or 2 horses), but cows and hornless stock in a quantity approaching the norm prevailing in the corvée villages, and sheep sometimes even in a larger number. As a characteristic feature it should be noted that the Olęders more often possessed horses than the tenantry, who for draft purposes used chiefly oxen. The draft stock was always the property of the Olęders. Since meadows entered only occasionally into the composition of the Olęder lands, and even when the Olęders possessed them, on account of their meagre extent they played no serious part in the stock-raising economy, the breeding of cattle and sheep in the Olęder settlements in the summer season rested almost exclusively upon the pasture servitudes, that is upon the right of pasturing herds, under certain conditions and within a defined range, upon the lord's lands. Since the lord, in his own interest, could not permit the pasturing of his lands by cattle in unlimited numbers, restrictions upon stock-raising came into being. The restrictions concerned as a rule sheep, more rarely goats, whereas hornless stock, cows and draft animals the Olęders were free to keep in any number.[^72-3] We meet with two basic kinds of restriction concerning the keeping of sheep. Relative restrictions, in the form of payments for the pasturing of sheep upon the lord's lands, or in general for the keeping of them, hindered it, but did not by any means make it impossible; absolute restrictions, on the other hand, consisting in the fixing of a certain maximum norm which the number of sheep upon a farm of a given size ought not to exceed,

[^72-1]: Kl. XII 6 II, fol. 111, the Ol. Stróżewskie.
[^72-2]: Schmidt (Geschichte, p. 398) imagines that the Olęders reared apple trees, pear trees, plum trees and cherry trees.
[^72-3]: Exceptionally in the Ol. Budziszewskie: “since concerning the hubas the Olęders lodged a complaint with the manor, suffering wrong in the pasture from the half-huba holders, who keep as many or more cattle and give from the pasture half as much less oats according to law... every Olęder keeping five head of horned cattle of whatever kind apart from horses owes the manor... on St Martin's day two wierteles of oats of the great skocki measure; and every Olęder keeping more than five head of horned cattle is bound each year to come to an agreement with the lord”.

[p. 73]

excluded the increase of stock-raising beyond that norm. A case in which the keeping of any domestic animals at all, apart from goats, was forbidden outright, the sources do not record. Returning to the first kind of restriction, it must be said that these always had the character of a payment for the pasture servitudes; for whenever the Olęders were obliged to pay dues for the keeping of sheep in general, there belonged to them the right of pasturage. The pasture servitudes and the payments connected with them will be discussed elsewhere. Here we may remark that dues payable upon the total number of sheep were rare; as a rule a certain contingent was entirely exempt from dues, and only upon the surplus were additional rents paid. On the average the limit was the figure of 50[^73-1] sheep. It could of course happen that the relative restrictions, in the form of additional rents for stock-raising, had in fact an absolute character, when they were set so high that, their existence notwithstanding, stock-raising presented no interest whatever. As a rule the Olęders were allowed to keep[^73-2] [—] sheep upon one huba. Exceptionally permission was given for the keeping of 60 or even 100 sheep,[^73-3] while sometimes the norm was lower: 30, 25, 20 sheep, for example.[^73-4] In the Olęders of the Sieraków estate it was at first free to keep sheep without any dues; but in order to prevent abuses on the part of the smallholders, [the free contingent was fixed] in proportion to the area of the farms. Probably the number of sheep possessed by the larger farmers was decisive. Sheep-keeping there attained a great flowering; according to the inventory of 1759 there fell, upon the average, 22 sheep and 9 lambs[^73-5] to the Olęder włóka. In 1776 dues were introduced in the amount of 15 gr from every sheep above 20 head; it is not known, however, how this affected the extent of the keeping.

The pasturing of the Olęder sheep was as a rule in the hands of a common communal herdsman. Sometimes there was kept in addition a separate herdsman for the cows, for the pigs, even for the horses.[8] The income of the Olęders from stock-raising was thus diminished by the remuneration of the herdsmen. The sources, however, give no information as to the kind and amount of this remuneration. Nor is it easy to compute the herdsmen's remaining incomes. The basis of these, as far as the shepherds were concerned, was doubtless the keeping of their own sheep, which but seldom exceeded

[^73-1]: AW. Budziszewskie Ol., B. 110; AGH sąd Wronki, t. XIX, 430, fol. 13, the Ol. Kobus; AW. Różnowskie Ol., B. 770; AW. Złotkowo Ol., A. 1.
[^73-2]: AW. Trojanowskie Ol., B. 1037.
[^73-3]: Ostrówek, Kl. XII 6 I.
[^73-4]: AW. Góździn, A. 1; Kawczyn, Rel. Cost. nr 177, 1742, fol. 211; AW. Dziewczastruga, A. I.
[^73-5]: Cf. the classification records. The total number of herdsmen in the classified settlements and in the Sieraków estate is given in Tab. III.

[p. 74]

the dimensions of the keeping of a one-huba farmer. The lord as a rule permitted[^74-1], sometimes even ordered, the keeping of herdsmen.[^74-2] Exceptionally, in the Ol. Rataje the lord forbade the Olęders to keep a shepherd of their own, and ordered them to come to an agreement concerning the pasturing of the sheep with the manorial shepherd.[^74-3]

2. Bee-keeping. Bees in hives the Olęders might keep in any number, without any dues.[^74-4] Sometimes, however, they were forbidden to keep bees in tree-hollow hives (barcie).[^74-5] The products of this keeping (honey, wax) they might dispose of at will. Exceptionally, in the Ol. Złotkowo they were bound to sell the wax exclusively at the manor.[6][^74-6] The income from bee-keeping did not, however, constitute a serious item in the Olęders' economy. A case such as that of the sołtys in the Ol. Kliny, who possessed 11 bee-hives, the sources quote as a kind of curiosity.[^74-7] Professional bee-keepers there were none among the Olęders.

3. Hunting and fishing. The right of hunting game upon their own land did not belong to the Olęders. They were permitted only to hunt birds. They might indeed also organise hunts of wild boar, bears and wolves, but the animals killed they were bound, on pain of severe penalties, to deliver to the manor. The lord on his part promised them in return a certain reward, not more closely defined.[^74-8] Only in the Ol. Ujskie was the right of hunting unrestricted.[^74-9] The Olęders' income from hunting, within limits thus curtailed, presumably had no great significance. It is obvious that the relations described above were not favourable to the formation, among the Olęders, of a group of professional hunters.

[^74-1]: AW. Milczewskie Ol., A. 1: “...for that flock of sheep they are permitted to keep a shepherd with seventy sheep”. AW. Uścikowskie Ol., B. 1054, fol. 9: “The sołtys will be free to keep a shepherd and fifty sheep, the other Hollendry twenty-five sheep”. AW. Stobnickie Ol., B. 991: “They will be free to keep horned and hornless cattle in whatever number shall seem good to the whole community, and to keep for that purpose shepherds with the cattle and the sheep”.
[^74-2]: The Ol. Rataje, Rel. Posn. nr 640, 1791, fol. 248: “Concerning a herdsman for the cattle and the swine the community shall have to take care”.
[^74-3]: The reference as above.
[^74-4]: AW. Stróżewskie Ol. A. 1: “Bees, without any tribute from them, in whatever number they themselves desire, I permit them to keep”.
[^74-5]: Rel. Posn. nr 626, 1788, fol. 406, the Ol. Zakrzewo.
[^74-6]: AW. Złotkowo, Ol. A. 1.
[^74-7]: AW. Kliny, C. 2.
[^74-8]: AW. Puszczykowskie Ol., A. 1: “Of big game of any kind, wolves and bears excepted, it shall not be permitted to shoot, kill or hunt; should any such chance to be taken, they shall be bound to deliver it to the manor for a consideration and nowhere else, under penalty. Birds of every kind, however, to catch, kill and shoot I always permit them”.
[^74-9]: Inscr. Valc. nr 85, 1663–74, fol. 402. In the settlements upon the territory of central Poland the right of unrestricted hunting belonged to the Olęders more often. Cf. Baranowski, Wsie holenderskie, p. 76.

[p. 75]

The Olęders' total income from fishing is made up of two items most closely linked with one another: the incomes from their own fish-farming and the incomes from the fishing servitudes. The separation of these two items is here extremely difficult to carry out. For it is not known whether, and in what cases, the possession of Olęder land carried with it the right of fishing in the bordering waters (rivers, lakes, marshes, ponds). The same doubts are raised by the ponds, which often stood upon Olęder land, and which either did or did not enter into the area of the individual farms. While it seems a certain thing that the adjoining waters, which separated the lord's lands from the Olęders', and likewise ponds lying indeed upon Olęder land but not entering into the composition of those lands, remained the property of the lord, and the right of using them must be regarded as a fishing servitude, it is so much the harder to decide whether, in cases where, for example, the Olęders received lands upon a river or lake bordering upon another estate, they entered into the existing manorial rights to those waters, or whether these belonged to them likewise only in the character of servitudes. The sources rather support the second possibility, for they distinguish fairly clearly the waters lying upon Olęder land and entering into the area of those lands (ponds[^75-1] dug by the Olęders, the seasonal floods upon the riparian lands),[1] and the remaining waters. On this assumption the lion's share[^75-2] of the Olęders' fishing income will fall to the fishing servitudes. The rule was that the lord permitted fishing in all the bordering waters, the manorial ponds upon Olęder land excepted. The Olęders were not, however, permitted to set up wears upon flowing waters.[^75-3] Sometimes the lord forbade the taking of fish in the spawning season.[^75-4] In exceptional cases, when the lord reserved to himself the exclusive right of fishing, he gave the community priority in letting it out on lease.[5][^75-5] The sources make no mention of professional fishermen among the Olęders.

[^75-1]: AW. Dłuskie Stare Ol., A. 1: “If water should fall to anyone's share in his measured-out land, he shall have the free use of it”. AW. Białężyńskie Ol., B. 59–60: “for the preservation of fish they shall be free to make and dam up fish-ponds for themselves”. AW. Folsztyn, B. 2: “It shall also be permitted them to take fish in the ditches, on the floods of the river, with their zabrodnie, without any hindrance from my subjects”. AW. Meryń Stary, C. 7, fol. 101: “Fischerei ist in ihren Gränzen, Lacken und Seen ganz frei, weil dieselben beflossen als unbeilossen mussen verzinst werden” [Fishing is entirely free within their bounds, lakes and waters, since these, whether flowing or non-flowing, must be rated].
[^75-2]: Rel. Posn. nr 638, 1791, fol. 602, the Ol. Róża: “on the Warta they are permitted to take fish with rods, sacks and baskets, as far as their boundaries reach, on this condition, that they hinder me in nothing in my ponds”.
[^75-3]: There were usually enumerated here the implements with which the fishing might be carried on: rods, hand-nets, baskets and sacks, larger nets and zabrodnie.
[^75-4]: AW. Drzewce, B. 2.
[^75-5]: Such was the case in the settlements of the Sieraków estate.

[p. 76]

4. Industry. a. Tavern-keeping. Taverns stood in almost all the Olęder settlements. They were, however, always, as it were, branch establishments of the manorial breweries (mielcuchy), for the propination monopoly was in force in the Olęder settlements and the innkeepers received ready-made manorial liquors, which they were bound to retail in the settlement at a price fixed in advance.

Exceptionally in Ostrówek the settlers, to whom Kerol Maciej Grudziński in the founding document of 1750 made over the brewery with its requisite equipment and a granary for the storing of malt, themselves engaged in the brewing of beer. Production was here organized in the following manner. The individual settlers were bound to deliver to the brewery, from every huba,[^76-1] [—] wierteles of barley and the quantity of hops needed for the brewing of [—] barrels of beer.[^76-2] They were obliged to haul the wood in turn in the amount of 2–3 sążni; for this they received the aforementioned 10 barrels of beer. The rest was presumably retailed by the innkeeper, whom the lord freed from rent, while his remaining dues the sołtys, the ławniks and the community were bound to take upon themselves. The incomes from the tapping of liquors (the production of vodka was similarly organized) the sołtys and the ławniks were bound to divide evenly among all the members of the community. We have here, then, to do with a typical co-operative organization.[^76-3] The organization of the sale of the manorial liquors presented itself in the Olęder settlements as follows. At the founding of the settlement the lord set aside ½ of a włóka (huba), free of rent, for the innkeeper. One of the members of the community paid the okupne, settled on the land,[^76-5] and even before the tavern was built began the tapping of the manorial liquors[^76-4]. His remuneration, besides the aforementioned ½ of a włóka free of all rents,[3] often embraced also the so-called świętojanka, that is the 21st barrel or, respectively, the 21st garniec of the beer or vodka tapped.[4] In rare cases other advantages were granted to the innkeeper. In the Ol. Gębickie, for example, he received no świętojanka, but he was free to keep 100 sheep without any dues.[5] The basis of the innkeeper's subsistence was agriculture, and the tap

[^76-1]: AW. Puszczykowskie Ol., A. 1: “to him (i.e. the innkeeper) half a huba of land ought to belong for all time, from which, the grundgield once paid, I make him for ever free of rent...”. The same Rel. Cost. nr 190, 1755, fol. 33, Krzemieniewo, and Rel. Cost. nr 164, 1729, fol. 486, the Ol. Kotusz.
[^76-2]: AW. Puszczykowskie Ol., A. 1: “to him (i.e. the innkeeper) half a huba of land ought to belong for all time, from which, the grundgield once paid, I make him for ever free of rent...”. The same Rel. Cost. nr 190, 1755, fol. 33, Krzemieniewo, and Rel. Cost. nr 164, 1729, fol. 486, the Ol. Kotusz.
[^76-3]: It rarely happened that the innkeeper received land of an area larger or smaller than 1/5 of a włóka; e.g. 1 włóka in the Ol. Kamionka (AW. Chodzież, C. 1, fol. 49), 12 morgs in Ostrów and Borgowo (Rel. Cost. nr 220, 1787, fol. 372), or 7½ morgs in the Ol. Jaracz (AW. B. 296) and the Ol. Dziewczastruga (AW. B. 1020).
[^76-4]: Exceptionally, in the Cisiogórskie Olęders, he received the 22nd barrel of beer and the 22nd quart of vodka (Rel. Cost. nr 224, 1788, fol. 379).
[^76-5]: AW. A. I.

[p. 77]

of liquors was for him a kind of manorial duty. It happened exceptionally that in a settlement there was neither a tavern nor the farm belonging to it, and the settlers kept the tap in turn, receiving for it only the świętojanka, or a money remuneration in the same proportion.[^77-1] Sales of liquors of this kind might even be for them a burdensome duty. The innkeeper was bound to haul the liquors himself from the manorial[^77-2] brewery,[3] and, under penalty, to have them always in sufficient quantity in[^77-3][^77-4] store.[^77-5] The tap embraced beer as well as vodka. “The sołtys[^77-6] and the ławniks are to see to it that (the innkeeper) does not make the liquors out of water, that people do not drink bad liquors for good money”.[5] The innkeeper was also bound “to see to music for the merry folk in the time of leisure”, presumably in order to increase the consumption of liquors. For the liquors sold the innkeeper had to render an account, presumably whenever new supplies were drawn. Isolated cases occurred in which the offices of sołtys[^77-7] and innkeeper were combined in one person. Thus, for example, in the Ol. Radoszkowskie the sołtys of the day (holding for life) was at the same time the innkeeper.[^77-8] In accordance with the lord's directives the tavern was to present itself as “a seemly house, fit for the tap as well as for the comfort of travellers”,[8] and beside it there was to stand a spacious stable.[^77-9] Incidentally we shall touch here upon the question of the extent of the consumption of liquors, concerning which we possess only fragmentary statistical data. In the taverns of the Stare and Nowe Borujskie Olęders and in the Ol. Szarki, according to the official assessment of the manorial incomes drawn up in 1797, the annual tap amounted to 229 barrels[^77-10] of garnce of beer and 12 barrels 121½ garnce of vodka, in Kościan measure.[19] In 1789 (Tol.) the population of these communities amounted to 1152 persons. The consumption of liquors per person, reckoning

[^77-1]: Rel. Posn. nr 620, 1787, fol. 26, Snowidowo: “The manorial beer and vodka they shall tap in turn, for a remuneration of the 21st grosz”. Item AGH sąd Wolsztyn, the Gościeszyn estate, vol. V (kart 334), fol. 34, the Ol. Stodólsko.
[^77-2]: AW. Jaracz Ol., B. 296: “And since the tapping of my beer in turn seems onerous to them, I do not withhold from them the remitting of a quart of land above the Olszewka for the innkeeper”.
[^77-3]: AW. Budziszewskie Ol., 108; Rel. Cost. 167, 1732, fol. 205, Ręcko.
[^77-4]: Ręcko, as above.
[^77-5]: AW. Góździn, A. I.
[^77-6]: As above. Item Rel. Posn. nr 640, 1791, fol. 248, the Ol. Rataje.
[^77-7]: Rel. Cost. nr 215, 1782, fol. 351. Item Kl. XII 6 V, fol. 144, Obelżanki.
[^77-8]: Rel. Cost. nr 220, 1787, fol. 372, Ostrowo and Borgowo.
[^77-9]: Rel. Posn. nr 430, 1741, fol. 234, the Kuślińskie Olęders.
[^77-10]: AGH sąd Wolsztyn, the Hamer estate, t. III H. I (Tax Acten), fol. 110. Alternatively — though this seems scarcely probable — the sołtyses may still have received the świętojanka, which did not enter into the given quantity of liquors.

[p. 78]

36 garnce to the barrel,[^78-1] amounted therefore to about 8 garnce of beer and ½ of a garniec of vodka a year. The capacity of the garniec of Kościan measure is not exactly established; it was presumably close to the capacity of the Berlin garniec, which held 4.58 litres.[^78-2] The actual consumption must have been at least[^78-3] twice as great, for the Olęders, thanks to their complete freedom to trade in agricultural produce and in all other goods, often went to the towns and neighbouring settlements,[^78-4] and this, as happens today likewise, was undoubtedly an occasion for the consumption of liquors. On the other hand, of course, in the Olęder taverns a certain percentage of the liquors might be consumed by the inhabitants of other settlements or by merchants passing through. The quantitative ratio of the consumption of vodka to the consumption of beer stood at 1 : 16. On the average, beer and vodka were consumed in approximately the same ratio in Bronice and Kwiejce Nowe according to the data of 1773.[^78-5].[^78-6] b. Milling. The total number of millers has been given by estimate in Tab. II and III. Almost all the mills existing in the Olęder settlements were windmills. The building of a mill required, in all probability, a special permission from the lord.[5] The Olęder millers paid for the mill only a fixed money rent.[6] The millers' incomes were diminished by the fact that the obligation to grind at the lord's mill did not as a rule bind the Olęders, who might therefore cart their grain to any mill whatever, whereas the neighbouring corvée peasants were bound to grind their grain only in the manorial mills. c. Crafts. As Tab. II and III show, crafts were but weakly developed in the Olęder settlements. This is presumably to be explained by the fact that the Olęders had a closer contact with the neighbouring towns than the rest of the population (cf. above). The lord did not forbid the community to keep craftsmen, requiring only that they be “honest”.[^78-7] In Podgradowice and Rakoniewice the Olęders were permitted “to make linen for their own household needs, but for no one else for money — not permitted, under penalty”.[^78-9].[^78-8]

[^78-1]: In the charter for Boruja (Rel. Cost. nr 218, 1785, fol. 221): “for which the manor shall give them two sądek of beer or seventy-two garnce”.
[^78-2]: O, p. XVI ff.
[^78-3]: Cf. Stenger, Die Hauländer, p. 252.
[^78-4]: Kl. XII 6 II, fol. 43: “Die Krüger nehmen das Bier von Schneidemühl und debitirt der Neu Sorgerer Krüger 6 Tonnen Bier 60 Quart Brandtwein. Der Bronizer Krüger”
[^78-5]: “Tonnen Bier 65 Quart Brandtwein Pohl. Maass. Niemand darf sich Bier brauen”. [The tavern-keepers take the beer from Schneidemühl, and the tavern-keeper of Neu Sorge retails barrels of beer and quarts of brandy in the stated quantities; the tavern-keeper of Bronice likewise, Polish measure. No one may brew beer for himself.]
[^78-6]: In 1790 Marcin Miaskowski issued a separate charter to the sołtys of the settlement of Suchylas for the building of a windmill (Kl. M.).
[^78-7]: In the charter for Kąkolewo the lord permits the Olęders to build a windmill, from which, when it is in working order, the owner is to pay an annual rent in the amount of 4 ducats.
[^78-8]: The Ol. Rataje, Rel. Posn. nr 640, 1791, fol. 249: “We permit them also to have among their community craftsmen of every kind, such as tailors and shoemakers and others, provided only they be honest”.
[^78-9]: Rel. Cost. nr 164, 1729, fol. 177 ad.

[p. 79]

d. Forest industry, embracing the burning of charcoal, the making of tar[^79-1] and of potash, likewise had no great significance in the Olęder settlements. It is indeed possible that the sources are silent concerning such an industry because it rested only upon the utilizing of the stock of timber standing upon the Olęder hubas and bought together with the land. The working-up of the timber had then, however, only a transitional character and ended with the clearing of the land. A permanent production resting upon the exploitation of the lord's forests was impossible without an agreement with the lord, and of such agreements the sources have preserved very little record.[^79-2] The classification records mention a tar-burner in Herbardowo only once;[^79-3] besides this they also mention the production of tar in the Ol. Króteckie. Concerning this last settlement the records supply a series of very valuable data concerning the production of tar. The Olęders distilled tar in common in the Krótek forests. The burning took place[^79-4] [—] times a year, and from each burning were obtained[^79-5] [—] barrels of tar. The value of each barrel was 10–11 zł. For each burning the Olęders paid the lord[^79-6] [—] zł, or else delivered a barrel of tar. The annual production of the furnace, after deduction of the manorial rent, thus represented a value of 350 zł.

[^79-1]: Trade. As a rule the Olęders enjoyed unrestricted freedom of trade. “All trade, however, in salt as in herrings and other goods, as also in grain, shall be free to them to carry on”.[3] Trade as a profession was carried on by the innkeeper, selling above all salt and herrings, then iron and other articles of everyday use. Besides the innkeeper other settlers also might draw incomes from trade, to which their charters entitled them, noting that “it shall be free to them to earn their bread by trade of every kind”.[5] This is likewise con-
[^79-2]: The information given by Schmidt (Geschichte, pp. 397–8) that the Olęders of Tomyśl produced tar and wood ashes for export to Saxony can relate only to production in the period of the clearing of the land. The burning of tar and of potash is regulated by charters only for Kiszewko (AW. B. 339), Łokacz (Kat. XIII II, fol. 420) and Rzecień (AGH sąd Wronki, the records of Rzecień, fol. 42).
[^79-3]: Kl. XII 6 V, fol. 33. In Kiszewko (AW. B. 339, 1697) the settlers were bound to deliver to the lord, from every burning, ½ of a barrel of tar or 5 zł.
[^79-4]: Rel. Posn. nr 430, 1741, fol. 230, the Kuślińskie Olęders.
[^79-5]: AGH sąd Wronki, the Popowo estate, t. XIX, 199, fol. 33: “(they may) thereupon carry on various trades, as in salt, herrings, iron, and may deal in other things...”.
[^79-6]: Dom. P. 6755, fol. 16, the Ol. Uścikowskie.

[p. 80]

firmed by the provisions of the charters by virtue of which the Olęders were exempted from market dues in towns belonging to the same lord, provided they did not buy “for re-sale”.[^80-1] A frequent object of trade was probably grain, whose free sale and purchase was permitted to the Olęders in the charters, doubtless in response to their earlier efforts.[^80-2] In very rare cases the lord restricted the freedom of selling grain, demanding, for example, that the Olęders should dispose of their surplus barley exclusively as malt at the manorial brewery (mielcuch).[^80-3] 6. Other occupations. The community might sometimes keep its own watchman. In Popowo the lord assigned to such a watchman, who was at the same time to have an eye upon the manorial buildings, “a piece of land”.[^80-4] A certain percentage of the population of the settlements was formed by teachers or readers, performing the functions of schoolmasters and of religious ministers. Their economic situation will be discussed on later pages. Here we note only that they drew their principal incomes from the land assigned to them by the lord, free of all rents. D. The Olęders' rights of user upon the lord's possessions (servitudes)

Besides the incomes from their own economy the Olęders drew profits from the lord's possessions. These flowed from the servitudes, chiefly the pasture, the forest and the fishing servitudes. I. The pasture servitudes were the foundation of the stock-raising economy of the Olęders. Cases in which the Olęders had no right at all of pasturing their herds upon the lord's lands occurred extremely rarely.[^80-5] As a rule the Olęders had the right of pasturing cattle in any number, and that often without payment;[^80-6] the right of pasturing sheep was in rare cases wholly forbidden;[^80-7] most often it was

[^80-1]: Rel. Cost. nr 224, 1788, fol. 379, the Cisiogórskie Olęders.
[^80-2]: Cf. e.g. the charters of 1677 for the Olęders of the Wieleń estate, or the charter for the Ol. Nowe Dwory of 1601 (Rel. Posn. nr 140, 1602, fol. 660).
[^80-3]: AW. Jaromierz, B. 4, Ujście Nowe.
[^80-4]: Rel. Cost. nr 174, 1739, fol. 1120, the Ol. Popowo.
[^80-5]: AW. Marienwald, B. 1; AW. Orłowiec, A. I.
[^80-6]: Rel. Posn. nr 640, 1791, fol. 270, the Ol. Glinki: “Item pasture for their cattle is free in my forest”. As a rule the fixed payment for pasture embraced also the pasturing of cattle, whereas the proportional dues were reckoned only from sheep. Exceptionally, in the Stare Dłuskie Olęders, the settlers “for every head of cattle, when they pasture it in my forest (i.e. the lord's), must give a hen apiece”.
[^80-7]: AW. Borkowskie Ol., A. 1: “for the pasture... they shall have to pay, apart from the sheep, which, if they keep them, each must pasture on his own huba”. Item AW. Krosińskie Ol., A. 1.

[p. 81]

either limited in quantity, or else made conditional upon certain payments for the pasture (cf. p. 83). The following combinations were possible here: a. The Olęders paid for the pasture a certain rent in money or in kind, or rendered certain labour dues in a fixed amount, irrespective of the number of sheep pastured upon the lord's lands.[^81-1] b. The payments were assessed upon every sheep (or upon a certain number, e.g. upon every 4, upon every 25 sheep),[^81-2] and that in such a way that either all the sheep were subject to payment,[^81-3] or else the Olęders might pasture a certain number of sheep without any payment, certain dues applying only to the remaining surplus.[^81-4] c. Payments assessed proportionally to the area of the pasture set aside for the Olęders belonged to the rarities.[^81-5] Swine and pigs might for the most part not be pastured in the lord's forests without a special agreement with the lord.[^81-6] In some settlements of the Wieleń estate the Olęders might pasture their swine upon the lord's acorns, but they might not gather acorns for a store;[^81-7] in another case the lord permitted at the most 2 pigs from every huba to be pastured upon the manorial acorns.[^81-8] It was strictly forbidden to the Olęders to take in for pasturing cattle, sheep or hornless stock belonging to others.[^81-9] Only in few cases were they permitted to pasture their herds upon all the manorial lands. The lord then demanded that they should do no damage with their cattle to the manorial grain and meadows.[^81-10] For the most part the pasture servitudes referred to the lord's forests and pastures. Plantations and cultivated grounds were excluded.[^81-11] Sometimes pasturage could take place only upon a set-apart part of the pasture or of the forest.[^81-12] The charters contained —

[^81-1]: E.g. in the Chromiec Olęders (AW. A. 2), in the Ol. Gogolewskie (Rel. Cost. nr 222, 1786, fol. 348), in the Ol. Nieszawskie (AW. B. 1–5); in the Ol. Paproć (Rel. Posn. nr 040, 1791, fol. 268).
[^81-2]: AW. Puszczykowskie Ol., A. 1; Rel. Posn. nr 635, 1790, fol. 316, the Słonawskie Olęders; Rel. Cost. nr 165, 1730, p. 824, the Ol. Pecna.
[^81-3]: See the references given in the preceding note.
[^81-4]: AW. Huta Ol. (Knarrhütte), C. 3; AW. Chodzież, C. I, fol. I (the Ol. Krystynka); AW. Milczewskie Ol., A. 1; AW. Swóżewskie Ol., A. I.
[^81-5]: AW. Tuchorskie Stare Ol., A. I: [Bewillige Ich ihnen auf einer gantzen hube 50 Stick Schaffe zu halten, die selbe auf meinem gebiete zu hüten, so dass felt und wiese, vor die hüttung der Schaffe sollen sie einen jeden Morg vier Brommer... verzinssen] [I grant them to keep, on one whole huba, fifty head of sheep and to herd them within my domain, such that for the herding of the sheep they shall pay for every morg, in field and meadow, four...].
[^81-6]: AW. Borkowskie Ol., A. 1; AW. Chromiec Ol., A. 2; Rel. Posn. nr 489, 1751, fol. 69.
[^81-7]: In the Ol. Folsztyn, Herbardowo, Marianowo and Nowe Dwory (cf. the charters of 1677).
[^81-8]: In the Ol. Kąkolewo (Rel. Cost. nr 214, 1781, fol. 82).
[^81-9]: Rel. Posn. nr 641, 1792, fol. 164. Cf. also the charters for the Wieleń Olęders of 1677.
[^81-10]: Rel. Posn. nr 626, 1788, fol. 406, the Ol. Zakrzewo.
[^81-11]: AW. Złotkowo, A. I.
[^81-12]: Dom. P. 10 820, fol. 39, the Połajewskie Olęders.

[p. 82]

over and above this the general stipulation that the pasturing of the Olęder herds should not take place to the detriment of the manorial and corvée cattle.[^82-1] Pasturage might also be limited in time. In the Ol. Jasionowe, for example, it was permitted only from Easter to Whitsun,[^82-2] and in the Ol. Strychowskie only in autumn.[^82-4] In Złotkowo it was not permitted[^82-3] to pasture cattle upon the lord's stubble “zaraz za żolem”, that is before the field had been raked over.[^82-5] 2. Forest servitudes. By these we shall understand the Olęders' entitlements to the use of the lord's forest within defined limits — apart from the pasture servitudes. The range of the Olęders' forest servitudes presented itself one way in the rent-free years, another in the later years. In the first period the Olęders always had the right of taking timber of every kind from the lord's forest, provided there was not enough of it in sufficient quantity and quality upon the lands allotted to them.[5] After the term of the wolnizna had expired[^82-6][^82-7], there belonged to the Olęders as a rule the right of gathering in the lord's forest timber for fuel, while timber for building and rods for fences they might not cut without the consent of the manor.[8] Permission to make use, to any extent, of timber of every kind was a rarity. Often the Olęders received timber free of charge for the repair of buildings,[^82-8] or against the event of a fire, in this last case by a separate agreement with the lord. In general it was a principle that the felling of timber had to be preceded[^82-9] by the obtaining of the appropriate “receipt” from the lord.[19] The sale of timber from the lord's forests was strictly forbidden.[^82-10] In some charters we meet also with permission to gather in the lord's forest pine-needles (igliwie) for manure.[^82-11] The question of the gathering of mushrooms and berries, however, was not regulated in the charters.[^82-12] Cases in which the Olęders were at all de-

[p. 83]

nied forest servitudes at all were rare.[^83-1] In the Ol. Budziszewskie (founded in 1746) the place of the original servitudes was taken in 1770 by an annual agreement with the lord, from which either party had the right to withdraw at the end of the year.[^83-2] Only exceptionally did the manor take no equivalent for the enjoyment of the servitudes.[^83-3] As a rule there were payable on this account certain fixed dues, usually in kind (most often in oats).[^83-4]

3. Other servitudes. Owing to the difficulty of separating the Olęders' own fish economy from the fishing servitudes, both these branches have been discussed together on p. 75. Here we merely add that for the use of these servitudes the lord exacted no fees.

Among the servitudes must likewise be counted the right, belonging to the Olęders in some cases, of digging clay and gathering stones free of charge upon the lord's lands,[^83-5] and the right of cutting reeds upon the lord's marshes.[^83-6] Of other kinds of servitudes the sources have preserved no information.

E. The lord's non-recurrent economic benefits

To this category belong the lord's non-recurrent material benefits of the nature of gifts, or else immaterial benefits which nevertheless had an influence upon the development and profitability of the Olęder farm. Both the one and the other kind of benefit was promised to the Olęders in the charter, which made it possible to demand them of the lord should certain conditions, defined in the charter, arise. Thus, for example, for building the Olęders received bricks from the manorial brickyard at a reduced price.[^83-7] In another case he promised every settler who completed the building of a dwelling house the gift of a barrel of beer.[^83-8] In the event of destruction through the elemental calamities (fires, wars, floods) the lord, besides the timber for buildings already mentioned above as given free of charge, promised the Olęders “to extend a merciful hand”, among other things, presumably, by taking their Olęder

[^83-2]: AW. B. 110.
[^83-3]: E.g. in the Ol. Krystynka (AW. Chodzież, C. 1, fol. 1).
[^83-4]: Kl. XII 6 V, fol. 144, the Ol. Obelżanki; AW. Góździn, A. 1; Rel. Posn. nr 638, 1791, fol. 908, the Ol. Strychowskie; Dom. P. 6755, fol. 16, the Ol. Uścikowskie.
[^83-5]: Rel. Cost. nr 224, 1788, fol. 339, the Cisiogórskie Olęders; AW. Gębickie Ol., dep. A. I; AW. Orłowiec, A. I.
[^83-6]: Rel. Cost. nr 177, 1742, fol. 210, the Ol. Kawczyn.

[^83-8]: AW. Stróżewskie Ol., A. I.

[p. 84]

stock in to be fed at the manor.[^84-1] An immaterial benefit was the care of the manor for the Olęders and the defence of them against harm from the side of the inhabitants of the neighbouring settlements.[^84-2] Such care expressed itself for the Olęders in material advantages, which showed itself above all when the lord guaranteed to the Olęders compensation from the neighbours for damage done (to the crops, to the livestock etc.).[^84-3]

F. Manorial dues

I. Rents. All the Olęders were rent-payers. Labour dues had only a slight significance. Among the rents the principal part was played by the money rent. Independently of the division of rents into money and into kind, there draws itself quite clearly a division of the Olęder rents into dues from the individual branches of the economy (from the land, from the individual servitudes and advantages). As at the colonization under German law, so the Olęders did not pay rent immediately upon settlement, but enjoyed a certain number of so-called free years. The wolnizny had for their aim to make it possible for the Olęders to maintain themselves on the spot so long as the land had not yet been cleared and had not begun to yield incomes. As a rule there was required of the Olęders that in the free years they should erect the needed farm buildings.[^84-4] In settlements founded “from the new root” the wolnizna amounted most often to 6–7 years,[^84-5] whereas at the founding of settlements upon the site of deserted villages there were either no years of freedom at all, or they ranged from 1 to 3 years, according to the state of the lands and of any buildings still existing. In settlements founded upon lands partly overgrown there existed a differentiation of the years of freedom according to the state of the individual pieces of land. Independently of the exemptions in the initial period of the settlement's existence, the lord as a rule promised the Olęders an additional year of freedom in the event that, having abandoned their farms in consequence of the upheavals of war or of other elemental calamities, they should later wish to return to their seats.[^84-6]

[^84-4]: E.g. in the Ol. Grabowiec, Kl. XII 6 V, fol. 56.
[^84-5]: Of the 73 cases known to us the wolnizna amounted in one case to 8 years (Rzecień), in 35 cases — 7 years, in 21 — 6 years, in 8 — 5 years, in 4 — 4 years and in 4 — 3 years.

[p. 85]

8D a. Money rents were reckoned in different monetary units, whose rate was subject to continual fluctuations, and in consequence of which their mutual relation was continually changing. In the further considerations all the rents have been recalculated into Polish złotys of thirty groszy.[^85-1] Often, indeed, they were fixed alternatively in two distinct monetary units, and in connexion with this the value of the one coin was given in relation to the other.

]

Among the money rents the most important, and sometimes the only, item was formed by the rent from the land (the gruntczynsz). In the earliest settlements it was very low: in the Ujskie Olęders (in 1597) it came to 20 zł from a huba of 30 morgs,[^85-2] in the Ol. Nowe Dwory (in 1601) 25 zł from a huba which, as follows from the later surveys, was of 60 morgs.[^85-3] In the Ol. Róża (in 1624) the rent was nominally somewhat higher, amounting to 36 zł from the huba;[^85-4] in reality, however, owing to the fall in the value of the złoty, it was no higher than the rents given previously. The rent remained at this level throughout the seventeenth century.[^85-5] At the end of that century dues began to be reckoned in tymfs. Thus in the Dłuskie Stare Olęders (in 1688)[^85-8] the rent from the ślad was fixed at 64 tymfs. The real value of the tymf, however, did not amount to even half the value of the złoty current hitherto, so that in reality we have to do here with a lowering of the rent.[^85-6] In the first half of the eighteenth century the dues are reckoned either in “good money”,[^85-7] that is in the złotys current before the introduction of the tymf, or in thalers, reckoning at first 36 gr, and as the value of the złoty fell 3.6 and even 8 zł to the thaler, in which case the value of the rent stood, as before, at 30–36 zł (of silver), or else 8–12 thalers[^85-9] — or else in debased

[^85-8]: AW. Dłuskie Stare Ol., A. 1. Not evidently orientated in the monetary relations of the time, Schmidt (Geschichte, p. 383) regarded this rent as exceptionally high.
[^85-6]: The Ol. Jasionowe, 1701 (Rel. Posn. nr 645, 1793, fol. 319); the Ol. Glinki, 1701 (Rel. Posn. nr 640, 1791, fol. 270).
[^85-7]: As above, and: AW. Jaromierskie Stare Ol., dep. A. I (1709); AW. Meryń Stary, B. 2 (1713).
[^85-9]: AW. Tuchorskie Stare Ol., dep. A. 1 (1703) — 10 thalers; AW. Sowiagóra, B. 2 (1703) — 8 thalers; AW. Zielonka, A. I (1704) — 10 thalers; Rel. Posn. nr 035, 1790, fol. 316, the Słonawskie Olęders (1707) — 8 thalers; AW. Jaromierz (Nowy), B. 4, fol. 63 (1719) — 12 thalers.

[p. 86]

tymf złotys; in this last case the due amounted on the average to 50 tymfs, ranging in the individual cases from 30[^86-1] to 70[^86-2] tymfs. A somewhat higher rent we note in the settlements founded in the 1780s; it amounts there sometimes to 75–88 zł,[^86-3] that is 60–70 tymfs, reckoning the tymf at 38 gr. An exceptionally low rent in the Ol. Radoszkowskie amounts, from the huba (włóka), to 16 zł (in 1781); in the Włościejewskie Olęders (in 1783), on the other hand, we have dues quite high, amounting to 100 tymfs from the huba. About this sum the rent fluctuates in the settlements founded in the last quarter of the eighteenth century.[^86-4]

Payment in money was made for the most part also for the pasture servitudes. Separate payments for pasture appear at the end of the seventeenth century. They persist until the partitions in the amount of 2 thalers or 10 tymfs at the fixed rate per huba, or else 6 to 10 gr for every head of cattle. In the Ol. Stodólsko the fixed payment amounted to only 1 thaler, but the use of the pastures was limited in time.[5] In the Ol. Wymyślanka[^86-5] the Olęders were bound to pay both kinds of dues simultaneously.[^86-6] A manifestly punitive character had the sum fixed in the Ol. Głęboczek. There it was permitted to keep 50 sheep free of payment, while for every sheep beyond that a fee of as much as 1 tymf was payable.[^86-7] In a few cases the payment for pasture was already included in the general rent.[^86-8] Sometimes, again, a single payment was made for the pasture and forest servitudes together.[^86-9]

In several settlements the Olęders paid to the lord the so-called stróżowe (watch-money). This was assessed either upon every huba of land (thus, for example, in Krzemieniewo 1 zł was paid from every huba[^86-10]), or the whole settlement was bound to pay a certain fixed lump sum. In this last case the amount of the watch-

II, fol. 32 (in 1794) —

[p. 87]

money was very various; in Popowo it even amounted to 23[^87-1] zł a year, elsewhere less was paid (8 zł, 6[^87-2] zł).

b. Rents in kind. Rents of this kind we meet with in the second half of the seventeenth century; in the earliest settlements rents in kind do not occur. The most common rent was oats, delivered on the average in the quantity of[^87-3] [—] wierteles or ćwiertnie of local measure. (In the Borujskie Olęders, for example, there was in use the wiertel of Poznań measure, containing[^87-4] [—] garnce.) In exceptional cases the rent reached[^87-5] [—] wierteles of oats from the huba.[^87-6] Far more rarely was the rent reckoned in another kind of grain, for example in rye or in barley.[^87-7] The object of the rent might also be poultry (from the huba on the average 1–4 capons or hens, or 1–4 geese, exceptionally also ducks),[7] or eggs (in the quantity of from 1[^87-8] mendel to 1[^87-9] kopa), in rare cases butter[^87-10] (1 garniec) and cut straw (sieczka)[^87-11] (2 korcy). It should be noted that when the rent was reckoned in oats or in other naturalia, no special fees were usually taken for the forest servitudes. The rent defined as a payment for the servitudes amounted likewise on the average[^87-12] to [—] wierteles of oats, more rarely embracing other naturalia.[^87-13] Sometimes, again, naturalia took the place of a separate payment for the pasture servitudes.[11] In an isolated case wood ash likewise appears[^87-14] as a due. And sometimes the lord left the Olęders the choice of paying the rents in kind or their equivalent in money.[1]

B. 1028) 4 capons and 2 geese; in the Proszkowskie Olęders (AW. A. 1) 6 wierteles of barley or 8 wierteles of oats; in the Ol. Słonawskie for every 4 sheep 1 goose and 2 hens (Rel. Posn. nr 635, 1790, fol. 3106).

[p. 88]

2. Payments. Besides the money rents the Olęders paid various dues. They differed from the rents in this, that they were paid only in certain circumstances (e.g. upon the sale of a farm), whereas the rents were paid always, in a fixed and defined amount. On the border between rents and dues stood the already discussed payment for the pasture servitudes, paid for each individual sheep. The proper dues paid by the Olęders may be divided into 2 groups: dues of an economic character and administrative dues. To the first category of dues belonged the okupne (for land, for buildings), then the eventual market dues. The amount of the okupne fluctuated within very wide limits and it is difficult to give here any average figure. In 2 settlements founded at the close of the seventeenth century the okupne came, for the huba, to 40 thalers, reckoning the thaler at 15 szóstaks (== 120 zł),[^88-1] and 100 thalers at the rate of 5 zł to the thaler (== 500 zł).[^88-2] At the beginning of the eighteenth century there was paid for the huba: 150 zł (in 1701),[^88-3] 30 zł (in 1707),[^88-4] 10 zł (in 1709),[^88-5] 100 tymfs (in 1712).[^88-6] In connexion with the fall in the value of money the sum of the okupne rose nominally, reaching about 1730 exceptionally even 600 zł.[^88-7] On the other hand, in the same period we have to record okupne in the amount of 6 zł,[^88-8] and somewhat later (in 1752) 1 red złoty from the huba.[^88-9] For a longer time the amount of the okupne settles down at approximately 100 zł from the huba. In the last quarter of the eighteenth century the okupne is higher. For the huba there is paid: 900 zł (in 1771 — arable lands with buildings),[^88-10] 24 zł (in 1773),[^88-11] 30 red zł. (in 1775),[^88-12] 900 zł (arable lands with buildings — in 1776 and 1781),[^88-13] 10 red zł. (in 1783),[^88-14] 1,800 zł and 1,500 zł (arable lands with buildings — in 1785),[^88-15] 150 zł (in 1786),[^88-16] 20 red zł. (in 1787).[^88-17]

[p. 89]

Sometimes the lord exempted the Olęders from the payment of market dues[^89-1] in the towns lying within his estate. One must suppose that when the Olęders were not exempt from the dues, the rates applied generally were binding on them. The sources, however, say nothing about this.

Among the administrative dues the greatest significance belonged to the fee on the sale of farms. Its amount and kinds have already been given above, on p. 66. Apart from this, in a few settlements we encounter the dues already mentioned for the confirmation of more important legal acts (records, wills, divisions of property, marriage contracts). In the Milczewskie and Lubaskie Ol. the lord collected 11 tymfs (14 zł) for each act[^89-2]. In the Stare Borujskie Ol. we encounter a formally administrative fee, though by its character very close to a rent: the Olęders paid it every 50 years at the rate of 36 zł per huba in return for the manor's recognition of their right of ownership to the land they held[^89-3]. Maas's remark that the Olęders paid dues when marrying their daughters off outside the community rests on a misunderstanding[^89-4] 3. Labour dues. In the charters for the two earliest Olęder settlements on the territory of the former voivodeship of Poznań manorial obligations in the form of labour dues do not appear. In the Ol. Róża, located in 1624 in the krobielewskie estate, we already find the obligation to repair the bridge over the river Warta[^89-5], while the inventory of the Wieleń estate of 1653 enumerates among the Olęders' obligations, among others, one day of labour and the cartage of 3 loads of wood. Labour dues on a wider scale we find only in the charters for settlements located in the eighteenth century. Attention must, however, be drawn to the fact that their character is different from that of the serf labour of the enserfed peasants; the labour dues always burdened the Olęders to a small extent, were for the most part strictly fixed in the charter and arose by way of mutual agreement of the parties, and were not conditioned by a personal dependence on the lord, as were the labour dues of the enserfed population. In accordance with the provisions of the charters we can distinguish labour dues fixed in a definite quantity and labour dues binding in an unspecified quantity. As a rule we meet with labour dues quantitatively defi-

[^89-1]: Exempt from tolls were the Olęders of the Wieleń estate. Cf. the charters of 1677.
[^89-2]: AW. Milczewskie Ol., A. 1 i Kl. XII 6 V, fol. 116, Lubaskie Ol.
[^89-3]: zur Anerkennung des Ober Eigenthums jedes mal nach Verlauf von finfzig Jahren, zwei Dukaten zu achtzehn polskie Gulden” [“for the recognition of the superior ownership, each time after the lapse of fifty years, two ducats to eighteen Polish gulden”] (AGH, Wolsztyn court, the Obra estate, vol. IV, fol. 1137, recess of 1843).
[^89-4]: Entstehung, p. 84. The author, translating the text of Stan. Kutrzeba's work Historia ustroju Polski w zarysie, in which vol. I, p. 236 (the edition of 1931) contains a phrase concerning the Olęders, failed to notice that Kutrzeba's further considerations already concern the peasant population in general. It followed from this that the Olęders not only pay the fee mentioned above but must moreover, at the lord's demand, present themselves for service at the manor.
[^89-5]: Rel. Posn. nr 038, r. 1791, fol. 602.

[p. 90]

nite, either by the specification of a number of working days, or by the designation of an area of land on which certain works were to be performed. The number of working days, as indeed the quantity of the other labour dues, fluctuated within very wide limits. Beside cases in which there was no labour due at all, we find a case in which it was rendered at the rate of 50 days[^90-1] yearly per huba[^90-2]. On average the number of days of labour due amounted to 6–12 days per huba; lower labour dues or their absence we encounter in cases where other labour dues were also rendered, not specified in days (e.g. cartages). Usually the kind of work and the season of the year in which the work was to be performed were specified (ploughing, carting manure, sowings, the reaping of grain, haymaking etc.). More rarely we meet the obligation to perform certain agricultural works on a defined area of land, that is, the so-called jutrzyny [dawn labours] 3. In exceptional cases[^90-3] an intermediate combination was applied, the so-called apportioned labour due (robocizna wydziałowa), consisting in this, that a certain number of working days was fixed, but at the same time a minimum of work to be performed in the course of the day was specified. Thus, for instance, in one of the settlements the Olęders were obliged to “plough for 3 days, 12 ridges at a time”[^90-4], in another case it was their duty, from St Adalbert's day to St Martin's day, to plough a morg of land every week[^90-5]. The jutrzyny usually covered ploughing only (30[^90-6] to 60[^90-7] ridges), sometimes mowing only[^90-8], more rarely ploughing and mowing[^90-9], and exceptionally all the works except sowing[^90-10]. Jutrzyna labour dues could also consist in the mowing and drying of hay on a defined piece of meadow[^90-11]. In the Cisiogórskie Ol. the Olęders were further obliged to keep the meadows allotted to them “under watch … lest they become overgrown”[^90-12]. To this category of obligations also belonged
