[p. 109]

issued after those years mention again the hearth tax in place of the poll tax[^109-1]. The Olęders were not passed over either by the obligation of paying the tax on hides and slaughtering[^109-3] introduced in 1789[^109-2] and the subsidium charitativum in the church estates[^109-4].

An obligation of a military character we meet only in the Króteckie Ol. According to the note in the classification records, in case of war the settlers were bound to provide one soldier with horse and equipment[^109-5]. The character of this obligation is, moreover, unclear, if we take into account that probably what the possessor was aiming at here — he was Łukasz Bniński, the owner of extensive estates — was a recruit for the purpose of raising a private banner. It is difficult to determine to what extent this banner had in view the defence of the public interests, and to what extent the interests of the lord. I. The general economic situation and the standard of living of the Olęders

Judge Stenger, who wrote his remarks on the Olęders on the basis of personal observation of the settlements in the neighbourhood of Trzciel, Nowy Tomyśl and Zbąszyń, states that in these Olęder settlements a relative prosperity prevailed: “On the whole the Olęder likes to represent himself as quite poor and suffering misery; taking it all in all, he is not rich, but by no means poor”[^109-6]. The same is stated by Holsche: “These people here and there are doing quite well”[^109-7]. The author of the brochure “Uber Ursprung u. Verbreitung” (p. 33) follows the opinion of the writers mentioned above, adding, however, a proviso: “It is true that the unfavourable fact that many of these Olęder settlements were founded on ground too light, sandy, forest soil, and received no meadows, often hindered them in attaining prosperity”. The scanty source materials that have survived with regard to this question speak for the truth of the opinions cited above. On the whole the economic position of the Olęders, despite the poor soil, was satisfactory, but there existed differences in the economic situation of the individual settlements. It seems that the Olęders of the neighbourhood of Trzciel and Nowy Tomyśl were best situated; the soil there reached a medium quality, and the obligations were on the whole low. This is confirmed also

[^109-1]: AW. Rożnowskie Ol., B. 770, 1777; AGH, Międzychód court, the Skrzydlewo estate, vol. I, fol. 28, Skrzydlewo Ol., 1785.
[^109-2]: Rybarski, Skarbowość, p. 124.
[^109-3]: AW. Wilcz OI, C. II.
[^109-4]: Kl. XII O V, fol. 3s3.
[^109-5]: Kl. XII 6 V, fol. 33.
[^109-6]: Von den Haulindern, p. 252.
[^109-7]: Netzdistrikt, p. 223.

[p. 110]

by the fact that the settlements located earlier supplied a certain contingent of settlers for the later settlements; and, as is known, making good in the newly founded settlements required a certain reserve of ready money. Not badly either, as may be inferred from the old inventory, did the Olęders fare in the Sieraków estate. Worst of all was the state of affairs in the settlements of the northern and western part of the Poznań county. The majority of the settlers, because of the wretched soil, was condemned to mere vegetation. Certain incomes were afforded only by the keeping of cattle and sheep on the basis of the pasture servitudes, and, in a few cases, the growing of garden plants (vegetables, root crops). The standard of living of the Olęders did not at all differ from the scale of life of the rest of the rural population. In the one case as in the other the basis of nourishment was grain and its products. According to the classification records in the Stróżewskie Ol., where the quality of the soil was still tolerable, the annual consumption per person amounted to[^110-1] ? korcy of rye, one korzec of barley and 1½ korca of peas; in the Bronice Ol. 4 korcy of rye and 1 korzec of barley. Of the consumption of meat by the settlers it is difficult to say anything definite; in any case it must be stated that the keeping of swine was not here at all greater than in the corvée villages. On the whole, however, the Olęder farm developed in better conditions than the farm of the corvée peasants. The economic position of the Olęders was privileged, if we take into account at least their personal independence, the ownership of the land, buildings and livestock, the possibility of freely disposing of the fruit of their work (freedom of trade), the low rents, the minimal labour dues. The Olęder therefore, although he sat on unfruitful soil, had in comparison with the corvée kmieć greater chances of attaining prosperity.

[^110-1]: Kl. XII 6 V, fol. 134, Bielawy: „Weil der Acker von solcher schlechten Beschaffenheit sey, so kónnten dieselben selten das 3-te Korn gewinnen, und wiirden sie auch hieselbst ihr nótiges Auskommen nicht finden, wenn sie sich nicht durch Holtz Verkauf bishero ernihret hitten; da ihnen aber diese Freyheit anjetzo benommen wśre, so wiirden sie sich wohl an diesem Orte in der Folge nicht erhalten kónnen”. ["Since the soil is of such poor quality, they can seldom win the third grain, and they would not find here their necessary livelihood if they had not hitherto supported themselves by the sale of timber; but if this freedom were now taken from them, they would hardly be able to maintain themselves in this place in future.”] The same, fol. 139, Jasionna.

[p. 111]

CHAPTER V

The spiritual culture of the Olęders

A. Psychological type

The Olęders, as has already been said, apart from the first comers from the Netherlands, came from various parts of eastern Germany and western Poland. They were thus, at the moment of the location of the settlements, neither uniform in the racial respect, nor united by ties of character, customs and culture. In the later generations, however, the common conditions of existence largely levelled their cultural and customary distinctiveness, and the self-governing constitution of the communities, above all the institution of the joint liability of all the members of the community in external relations, closely bound together the fortunes of the individual settlers and contributed to the formation among them of a kind of group consciousness. This may be regarded as equivalent to the formation of a distinct psychological type, that of the “Olęder”. Contemporaries did not speak with enthusiasm of the character of the Olęders. A serious observer such as Stenger writes of them as follows: “It is a certain thing that with the ancestors of our present-day Olęders the best part of the German nation did not leave its fatherland; for, although below we tried to find the reasons why the Olęders have become worse, their present corruption could not be explained if one were to assume that they brought with them good customs and character. Diligence and dexterity, as the children of poverty, were probably their only dowry: if only they had at least preserved these qualities intact! The Olęder is not narrow-minded, but by no means intelligent: he is cunning when he fears an attack, and he holds so strictly to old prejudices and habits that, blinded, he does not notice his own obvious advantages; to the most convincing arguments he gives no hearing at all, for the innate fear of everything that is new makes him deaf to it. Further, he is extraordinarily suspicious: the husband does not trust his wife, the father his children, but all unite when it is a matter of distrust towards the lord or the superior. The Olęder is also extraordinarily stubborn, audacious and un-

[p. 112]

obliging: he does not willingly do anything that he need not do; finally, he has no religion at all” 1. Elsewhere the author charges the Olęders with litigiousness. This matter has already been discussed above[^112-2]. As a favourable trait of the Olęders the author emphasises their strong feeling of communal solidarity (social sense), and states also that sobriety generally prevailed in the Olęder settlements. These last two remarks must be recognised as correct. It is true that taverns, or the tapping of the lord's liquors, were to be found in almost all the settlements, and for the labour dues called “tłoki” the settlers received their remuneration in beer, possibly[^112-1] in spirits; this proves nothing at all, however, that the use of liquors was practised among the Olęders habitually. In a certain contradiction with the feeling of communal solidarity stood the sometimes strikingly weak attachment of the Olęders to their land 1 and their inclination to move from one settlement to another[^112-4]. It seems to follow from this[^112-3] that the Olęders, often newcomers from abroad, were accompanied even in later generations by a feeling of a certain strangeness. The lord also cared for good morals in the community, threatening sometimes even with the expulsion from the settlement of individuals without moral principles and character.

[^112-1]: Von den Haulśndern, p. 247. The author adds the proviso that he would not have set down the above remarks had he not been convinced of their truth.
[^112-2]: On p. 103. The Olęders, moreover, sued not only the manor. They carried on protracted disputes also 7 with the community teachers (of which more will be said below), and even with their own clergymen. Such was the case, for instance, in Herbardowo, where the community sued the local preacher (Prediger) over the use of a meadow alleged to belong to the community (AW. Herbardowo, C. III). 3 In Rel. Cost. nr 226, r. 1792, fol. 3, we find an interesting manifesto of the hon. Koszutski, lord of the village of Zabory, charging the hon. Młodziejowska, castellan's wife of Nakło, with having organised an armed attack on his forest, “having sent one Grudziński, of whatever name, the revenue clerk from the village of Łęg, with people from her estates and the steward of those estates … carrying arms, three Olęders, two foresters and the maltster Andrzej from the village of Łęg with a musket, and moreover many other Olęders, present with cudgels and clubs, all of them drunk; and they beset the plaintiff, likewise thirty-five boors with axes, paying no heed to the plaintiff's oral defence, violently, piece by piece, spoiling the timber” etc. The plaintiff demands the punishment of the steward, the clerk and “the Olęders of those estates”.
[^112-3]: Striking is the considerable number of deeds of sale recorded in the community books. This trait will appear still more clearly if we compare, for instance, the surnames given in the charter of 1601 for the settlement of Nowe Dwory with the surnames of the Wieleń Olęders given in the inventory of 1653, and these in turn with the surnames given for the same settlements in the Prussian classification records of 1773. Attention has been drawn to this phenomenon also in German literature (Schulz W., Die zweite deutsche Ostsiedlung).
[^112-4]: AGH, Międzychód court, Skrzydlewo vol. I, fol. 27, charter of 1785: „Wird denen- selben gute Żucht und Ehrbarkeit... nicht nur auls beste anemplohlen, und das Fluchen, Schwó- ren, Gottesliistern, und alle iibrige iuppige und liederliche Lebensarth aufs ernstlichste ver- bothen, widrigenfalls dieselben von der Herrschaft recht nachdriicklich gestrafet. Die ganzen Widersprustigen hingegen nach Auszahlung ihrer taxirten Giither aus der Gemeinde fort- 39 e * gejagt werden sollen.. ["Good discipline and uprightness … shall be not only most earnestly commended to them, and cursing, swearing, blasphemy and all other riotous and disorderly ways of life most earnestly forbidden, failing which they shall be punished by the lordship in the most emphatic manner. The stubborn recalcitrants, on the other hand, after payment for their valued goods, shall be chased out of the community.."]

[p. 113]

B. Schooling

According to the Prussian classification records for the part of the Poznań county taken in the first partition, in every third Olęder settlement there existed an evangelical school[^113-1]. Probably the number of schools in the settlements with an evangelical population was still greater in the remaining part of the former voivodeship, where there was a greater number of populous settlements able to maintain a teacher. On the other hand it must be taken into account that in the Catholic Olęder settlements there were no schools at all[^113-3].[^113-2] In any case, however, the estimated figures given in Table II must be regarded rather as minimal. The Olęder schools were typical denominational institutions (Kantorat-, Lektoratschulen). They were conducted by church singers or other persons who at the same time performed religious ministrations in the community. They stood in for the evangelical clergymen for the Olęders. Hence the inhabitants of a new settlement usually tried to secure for themselves the religious ministrations of a cantor or lector, striving with the lord for permission to found a school and for its endowment. It is obvious that in schools of this kind the first place was held by religious preparation. With regard to some schools the source notices even raise the doubt whether anything other than the teaching of religion 3% was taught there. Similar reflections are suggested also by the circumstance that there were no schools either in the Olęder settlements with a Catholic population, which had easy contact with their clergymen and the possibility of using the parochial schools, or in those evangelical settlements where the pastor was on the spot, or in the nearest neighbourhood (e.g. in the settlements of the Wieleń estate).

The school was usually housed in a separate building, erected with the lord's consent. This building was at the same time the local house of prayer. Only in the Sieraków estate were the Olęders, under the provision of the then owner Henryk Bruhl, not permitted to have separate school buildings, and instruction could take place only in the cottage of one

[^113-1]: Schmidt (Geschichte, p. 399) maintains that every settlement had a teacher. He did not, however, support his assertion with examples.
[^113-2]: The settlement of Chromiec was an exception.
[^113-3]: AW. Gębiczyn A. 2, Gębickie Ol.: And since these Olęders are of the evangelical faith, I permit them to keep a bachelor [schoolmaster] of their faith for the reading of the gospel. Rel. Posn, nr 645, r. 1793, fol. 320, Jasionowe Ol.: The supplement of 1780 to the charter of 1701 confirms to the community the right of keeping a “forlezer”. Similarly Rel. Cost. nf 164, r. 1729, s. 485, Kotusz Ol.; AW. Krosno Ol., A. 1, Krosińskie Ol.: A bachelor for divine service is permitted to them. Rel. Posn. nr 430, r. 1741, fol. 230, Kuślińskie Ol.: The Olęders are permitted to build a school and keep a teacher “for the holding of divine service in that same school”. AW. Lubicńskie Ol., B. 1: „...soll dicsc Gemeine ihre Schule zum Gottes-Dienst mit den Hutten Hollindern haben und halten, und auch darzu hegen und ee ["…this community shall have and keep its school for divine service together with the Hutt Holländer, and shall also maintain and endow it for that purpose…"] AW. Nieszawskie Ol., B. 15: A bachelor, who should read them the gospel, I permit…

[p. 114]

of the farmers[^114-1]. The maintenance of the teacher was made possible by the lord, who assigned to the school a certain area of land free from all rents.

The school land usually amounted to ½ wł., more rarely 1/4 wł., exceptionally still less[^114-2]. In the Ujskie Ol. one włóka was assigned to the school, from which, however, the Olęders were obliged to pay half the rent. If the lord settled the Olęders on a site where buildings of another village, or of a folwark, still existed, he offered one of the buildings gratuitously for the school[^114-3]. Apart from the income of the school land the teacher drew incomes also from the contributions of the community. In the latter the most common, but at the same time the most precarious item was formed by the dues from each child making use of the school instruction; the rest consisted of certain offerings fixed in agreement with the teacher, in money and in kind. By way of example we state that the income of the teacher in the Siereckie Ol. in 1793[^114-4] consisted of: 1) the profits from the use of 1½ włóka of school land, 2) a tribute at the rate of 16½ thalers, 3) 4 contributions (Opfer), not further specified, 4) from every farmer one loaf, 5) one pound of butter and 4 cheeses from each huba, 6) from every farmer a cart-load of wood, 7) dues according to a separate tariff for singing at burials, and 8) dues from the pupils at the rate of 6 zł from the older children and 3 zł from the younger children.

The community could not always afford to maintain a professional teacher. The lord also sometimes stipulated in the charter that a craftsman[^114-5] or a farmer[^114-6] should be appointed as teacher. In one of the cases known to us the functions of teacher were performed by a weaver[^114-7], in another an innkeeper 8, and, as Stenger relates, they were often entrusted to cobblers or[^114-8] tailors[^114-9]. Candidates for teachers came either from the neighbouring little towns, from which lack of work or other considerations had induced them to depart, or else, as regards candidates without a trade, they were recruited from the rural labourers who in one way or another had acquired the ability to read and write. Of the future teachers little

[^114-1]: Cf. the charters for Bucharzewo, Bukowiec, Chorzępowa, the Dębowskie, Radoskie Ol., Tuchola, Zatom Nowy and Zwierzyniec (call numbers in annex I).
[^114-2]: AW. Blinek Ol., A. I — 4 morgs, AW. Szklarka Trzcielska, A. 1 and AW. Drzewce, B. 2 — 3 morgs.
[^114-3]: Rel. Posn. nr 641, r. 1792, fol. 451, Miłostowo; Kl. XII 6 I, Ostrówek; Rel. Posn. nr 641, fol. 163, Zamorze.
[^114-4]: Szk. nr 47 a, fol. 4.
[^114-5]: Kl. XII 13, fol. 239, Jędrzejewskie Ol.; AW. Milczewskie OIl., A. 1; AW. Stróżewice, A. 1; AW. Strzeleckie Ol., C. 3, fol. 9.
[^114-6]: Rel. Cost. nr 224, r. 1788, fol. 379, Cisiogórskie Ol.; AW. Kąkolewo, A. I and Rel. Cost. nr 217, r. 1784, fol. 230.
[^114-7]: Kl. XII 13, fol. 238, Jędrzejewskie Ol.
[^114-8]: AW. Budziszewskie Wielkie Ol., B. 108.
[^114-9]: Von den Haulindern, p. 254.

[p. 115]

was required; it sufficed that they should be able somehow to read and write and adapt themselves to the requirements of the community. To the teacher's duties belonged the teaching of religion and the performance of certain religious ministrations, and the exercise of the children in reading, writing[^115-1] and counting. At the same time the teacher performed the function of community secretary; he kept the community book, transacted the correspondence of the community, and probably also the private affairs of the Olęders.

The relations of the teacher with the community shaped themselves markedly unfavourably, which in the result greatly weakened the value and level of the school instruction. The cause here was the circumstance that the teacher, as one engaged[^115-2] and largely supported by the community, was entirely dependent on it, and thereby powerless in the face of the caprices and arbitrariness of the settlers. The Prussian officials, who carried out directly after the second partition a revision of the evangelical schools in the occupied territories, reckoned among the most important ailments of the school system, among others: the complete lack of respect and understanding for the persons of the teachers, sometimes indeed through their own fault, and the widespread custom that every settler presumed to be entitled to give commands to the teacher, and, when his child was reprimanded at school, came himself to the teacher and abused him in the most brutal manner. Still more forcefully the attitude of the Olęders to the teachers is characterised by Stenger. The surviving lawsuit records concerning matters between the settlements and the teachers show also that the latter were often charged with utterly unserious accusations and expelled for trivial reasons[^115-3]. Probably a cause of this development of relations was also the insufficient supervision of the school system (partly exercised by the pastors).

It remains in turn to discuss the extent, level and significance of schooling in the Olęder settlements. Recent German literature has expressed the view that the schooling in the settlements inhabited by German settlers (among others in some Olęder settlements), despite all its primitiveness, was at least by two centuries ahead of the conditions prevailing contemporaneously in the Polish villages[^115-4]. This opinion, formulated moreover unclearly, has no source justification. It is true that the number of schools in the Olęder settlements was

[^115-1]: Von den Hauliindern s. 255: „ein solcher Schulhalter musste dann auch iein den Winken der Haulinder gehorchen, wenn er nicht in seinen Reveniien verkirzt, und endlich gar abgesetzt seyn wollte. Ich kenne einen sehr rechtschafnen Schulhalter, der neuerlich re- signirte, und mir mit Thrinen gestand, dass er lieber die S.... hiiten wolle, als linger Hau- linder — Schulhalter seyn”. ["such a schoolmaster then had to obey the becks of the Hauländer if he did not wish to suffer in his revenues and finally even to be dismissed. I know a very decent schoolmaster who lately resigned and confessed to me with tears that he would rather keep the S…. than be a Hauländer schoolmaster any longer”]
[^115-2]: Szk. nr 47 a, fol. 5.
[^115-3]: Von den Haulindern s. 255: „cin solcher Schulhalter musste dann auch fein den Winken der Haulinder gehorchen, wenn er nicht in seinen Reveniien verkirzt, und endlich gar abgesetzt seyn wollte. Ich kenne einen sehr rechtschafnen Schulhalter, der neuerlich re- signirte, und mir mit Thrźnen gestand, dass er lieber die S.... hiiten wolle, als linger Hau- linder — Schulhalter seyn”. 4 The Stróżewo Olęders (AW. C. 2), asked for the reasons for the expulsion of the teacher, adduced, among others, that in the Sunday sermon he had made a spiteful allusion to those present (namely he prayed for the conversion of sinners, which the community took as a thrust at themselves). ["such a schoolmaster then had to obey the becks of the Hauländer if he did not wish to suffer in his revenues and finally even to be dismissed. I know a very decent schoolmaster who lately resigned and confessed to me with tears that he would rather keep the S…. than be a Hauländer schoolmaster any longer”]
[^115-4]: Schulz W., Die zweite deutsche Ostsiedlung, I, p. 23.

[p. 116]

indisputably greater than the number of schools in the contract villages; but the matter changes fundamentally if we take into account the percentage of children making use of school instruction. From the official revision of schools carried out in 1793 in the territory of the second partition we learn that, for instance, 20 pupils attended the school in the Siereckie Ol. in the time of its greatest attendance. The settlement numbered 43 hearths in 1789, which, applying the coefficient of 6.05 persons per hearth, gives in the result 260 persons. Thus one person in every thirteen attended[^116-1] the school, assuming that children from other settlements were not[^116-2] sent to it and that attendance was only during the winter months. Almost until Christmas the school stood empty 1. Relations of this kind were not at all exceptional. The Prussian authorities carrying out in 1793 the revision of the evangelical schools in the Trzciel district give, as a universal defect of the school system, the slowness of the parents in sending their children to school. The parents, in the opinion of the authorities, perform this duty unwillingly and irregularly, sometimes only when the child is to approach the sacraments[^116-3]. There follows the characteristic remark: „Diese Klage muss man besonders iber viele den Einwohvon der Haulander fuhren, die ihre Kinder żusserst nern sogenannten. schlecht zur Schule anhalten, sie nur immer zum Hitten ihres Viches brauchen, und sie durch solche Vernachlissigung beynahe selbst bis zum Vich herabwirdigen[^116-4]. ["This complaint must be made above all of many of the inhabitants of the Hauländer, who keep their children most badly to school, use them only ever for the tending of their cattle, and through such neglect degrade them almost to the level of cattle themselves"] The scope of the school instruction did not go beyond[^116-5] the teaching of religion and the beginnings of writing, reading and counting. The level of the teaching and its effectiveness were the resultant both of the education and pedagogical abilities of the teacher, and of the attitude of the parents and children to the school.

In the older generation of the Olęders the ability to write belonged to the rarities. On documents concerning the settlements we find mostly manual signs instead of signatures 8. Possibly a contributory cause of this state of things

[^116-1]: Szk. nr 47 a, fol. 4. Conditions no better prevailed in the Międzyrzecz district (Szk.
[^116-2]: III 93, fol. 7).
[^116-3]: Szk. nr 47 a, fol. 5. Stenger characterises the Olęders' attitude to the school similarly (Von den Haulindern, pp. 254–5). 3 Szk., ibid. 1 Under the call number AW. Reńsko, C. 1 there has survived a protocol of the inspection of the school in Ręcko of 1798, that is after 5 years of the school's existence under the care of the Prussian authorities. Attending the school were
[^116-4]: children (10 boys, 8 girls), of whom 4 boys could write with chalk on the board.
[^116-5]: In the records concerning the Stróżewskie Ol., of 11 signatories 10 made manual signs (AW. Stróżewskie Ol., C. 2); in the Ujskie Ol., of the total number of 31 settlers only 2 were able to sign (AW. Ujskie Ol., C. 2, I, fol. 160). The agreement of 1798 between the heir Gajewski and the community of the Tłockie Ol. was signed with little crosses by all the Olęders to the number of 13 (AW. Tłockie Ol., dep. A. I, fol. 4). Also in Złotkowo, whose monograph was worked out by Brandenburger (Das Flaulinder — Dorf Goldau), all the inhabitants to the number of 12 were illiterate (AW. B. 14–15). A great number of illiterate Olęders is shown likewise by the earliest Protestant registers of Lwówek (Archiwum Państwowe w Poznaniu, Metryki 441 C). Even in the regulation records drawn up mostly in 1830–40 the settlers, although part of them had had school instruction already in Prussian times, were for the most part unable to sign.

[p. 117]

partly secondary illiteracy contributed. Be that as it may, even if one takes into account that the Olęder schools were founded mostly for reasons of a religious nature, and that the results of the school instruction were very modest, it must be stated that their very existence constitutes in the history of the settlements an indisputably positive item. Advantageously, too, for the interests of the settlement must have reflected the teacher's performance of the function of community scribe. In this way, through the transaction of the community's affairs in writing, the necessary order and tidiness could be maintained in them.

C. Religious worship

As a rule the charters guaranteed to the Olęders freedom of confession[^117-1] and of religious practice. This had of course significance only in relation[^117-2] to the evangelical confessions, for the Olęders of the Catholic confession could always perform their religious practices without hindrance. The restrictions in the exercise of religious practices which occasionally occurred concerned exceptionally only the religious life of the individual members[^117-3]; more often they restricted or excluded the collective practice of religious worship. Here belonged the prohibition of preaching sermons by the teacher or

[^117-1]: AW. Wielkokrobielewskie Ol., B. 1 b: Their religion is to be free to them; they are not to be compelled to any. Likewise Rel. Posn. nr 549, r. 1767, fol. 442, Daleszyno; AW. Jaromierz B. 4, fol. 63; Rel. Cost. nr 214, r. 1781, fol. 82, Kąkolewskie Ol.; AW. Chodzież, C. 1, fol. 1, Krystynka Ol.; AW. Lewickie Ol. dep. A. 1; AW. Lubieńskie Ol., B. 1; AW. Marienwala, B. I; AW. Meryń Nowy, C. 7, fol. 101; Rel. Posn. nr 038, r. 1791, fol. 602, Róża Ol.; AW. Nowe Tuchorskie Ol., A. 1; AW. Stare Tuchorskie Ol., dep. A. 1; Q nr 38 and the call numbers in annex I b, Ujskie Ol.
[^117-2]: Rel. Posn. nr 430, r. 1741, fol. 230, Kuślińskie Ol.: They are permitted to erect a school and to hold divine service according to the Hollander custom, and none shall gainsay them therein, neither clergy nor others. Rel. Posn. nr 140, r. 1602, fol. 660, Nowe Dwory: It shall also be permitted to them to hold divine service according to their faith and to keep a priest at their own cost. Rel. Posn. nr 549, r. 1767, fol. 442: I also permit them to build a school and give timber, so that they may hold their divine service and keep a prolector for themselves…, who are likewise to be troubled by no one, neither by any clergyman.
[^117-3]: A restriction of this kind we met only once, with regard to the Olęders of Bukowiec (charter of 1692, AW. Bukowiec, A. 1): neither to keep a preacher, nor to bring him into the village, but should the greatest need arise, they are free to ride to the preacher. Baptism, however, they ought to receive in the Sieraków church, and weddings likewise. The above prohibition, inserted in the charter probably in consequence of the remonstrances of the Sieraków parish priest, survived only until 1751. In the charter of that year it no longer figures. It is possible that in practice it was observed for an even shorter space of time.

[p. 118]

lector 1,[^118-1] and in isolated cases also the prohibition of reading the gospel 2%.[^118-2] On the other hand the freedom of the Olęders to exercise their religious practices[^118-3][^118-4] outside the community (in the town meeting-houses etc.) was not called in question 3.[^118-5] On the whole, if we leave aside the settlements lying in the estates of the dissenting szlachta, disposed with particular favour towards their co-religionists, it must be stated that the lords showed no great interest in the religious affairs of the Olęders. The placement in the charters of the few, above-mentioned restrictions in connection with the obligations towards the church suggests the thought that they were introduced into the charters in consequence of the interventions of the proper parish priests. Sometimes the lords took up no position of their own at all in the religious matters of the Olęders, recommending that in these matters they should proceed as “the Commonwealth permits” 4. The sources contain not a single mention of any control of the religious life of the Olęders undertaken on the lord's initiative with a view to its conformity with the provisions of the charter. Nor is there to be found in the sources a single case of a later restriction of the freedom of confession guaranteed to the Olęders in the location charter. As for the above-mentioned restrictions contained in the location charters, these could not be imposed on the Olęders, as free men able to choose their future lord at will, without their consent.

Where there were particularly numerous clusters of Olęder settlements with an evangelical population, there the Olęders, with the manor's consent, built themselves churches and maintained at their own cost clergymen of their confession 5. On the whole, however, the evangelical churches in the Olęder settlements were few, partly because the laws of the old Commonwealth (before

[^118-1]: AW. Krosna, A. 1: A bachelor for divine service is permitted to them,… which bachelor shall proceed no further than belongs to a dissenting bachelor, without preaching and without singing.
[^118-2]: AW. Góździn, A. I, fol. 71: Item, there is permitted to them for everlasting times to keep a schoolmaster, that is, that he should teach the children reading and writing. But he shall not presume to read any Scripture or the gospel to the community.
[^118-3]: The exception is the case cited in n. 3 of the preceding page.
[^118-4]: Dom. P. 6755, fol. 16, Uścikowskie Ol.: In their religion they will proceed as the Commonwealth permits. Likewise AW. Białężyńskie Ol., B. 59–60.
[^118-5]: Rel. Posn. nr 140, r. 1602, fol. 660, Nowe Dwory. Such permission we meet likewise in the charters of 1677 for the remaining settlements of the Wieleń estate. Werner Steffani (Geschichte der ewangelischen Parochieen) enumerates the following churches existing in the settlements in the times of the old Commonwealth: in Folsztyn, the Gębickie Ol., Herbardowo (registers from 1692, cf. Jahrbuch der Historischen Gesellschaft f. d. Netzedistrikt, 1898, pp. 24–5), Krosno, the Lewickie Ol., Łokacz, Nowe Dwory, Szklana Huta, the Wielkokrobielewskie Ol.

[p. 119]

1768) restricted the freedom of erecting dissenting churches, partly again for this reason that the Olęders were not in a position to build a church at their own expense or to maintain a pastor, or else — and this was the most frequent case — their ordinary religious needs were fully satisfied by the local teacher, while in exceptional cases they had the possibility of getting into touch with the nearest pastor. The sources contain no closer data concerning the pastors; it is to be supposed, however, that they were persons as a rule enlightened and prepared for the exercise of clerical functions. Of course exceptions occurred here too[^119-1]. The relations of the pastors with the community were not always satisfactory. Mention has already been made above of the lawsuits of the Herbardowo community with the local clergyman. A wholly unfavourable testimonial was given to his parishioners by the pastor of the evangelical community of Piotrowo, to which the Króteckie and Miłkowskie Ol. also belonged[^119-2].

The ordinary religious needs of the Olęders were fully satisfied by the local teacher, often called the “ferlezer” (German Vorleser, Lektor, Kantor). He held the Sunday service in the community (read the gospel, delivered the sermon, sang religious songs), conducted the burial rites (escorted the dead to the cemetery, sang songs and delivered funeral orations), and taught the children the principles of religion. The defects in the choice of teachers have already been mentioned above. In consequence they often lacked the sufficient moral qualities for guiding the spiritual life of the community. Hence also Stenger speaks of them with irony[^119-3]. Schmidt, on the other hand, is of the opinion that the merits of the teachers as guardians of the communal morals were great, and sometimes they even became

[^119-1]: In Rel. Cost. nr 217, r. 1784, fol. 234 we find an enrolment of a manifesto according to which “the citizens of Rostarzewo … an honest Olęder whom they had long ago brought from the Olęders belonging to the little town of Grodzisko, later a bachelor on the Kąkolewskie Olęders … they brought to Rostarzewo and proclaimed him their pastor, and this one, brought in only two years ago, has begun the administration of all the sacraments and has procured the forbidding of the Rostarzewo communities to attend other churches”.
[^119-2]: The Prussian commission of 1794 (Szk. nr 65, fol. 17), on the basis of the pastor's depositions, states as follows: „Carl Wilhelm Miller aus Reppen in der Neumarkt gebirtig, stehet bereits Jahre als Prediger hier im Amie. Hat aber wihrend dieser kurzen Zeit schon enizetzlich viele Krinkungen von der Gemcine erdulden miissen. Sie lassen alles, da die Kirche arm ist, eingchen, repariren nichts an derselben und an den zur Kirche gehórigen Gebiiuden, Zaunen und Stóllen, und halten ihm in vielen $ticken dic Vocation nicht. Es ist bekannt, dass diese Pie- trover Gemeine sich von je her so betragen, und in Zeit von 20 Jahren, 3 Prediger hinter- einander zu Todc geźrgert hat, mir gehet es nicht besser, und ich stehe schon bereits ein ganzes Jahr unter den Hinden des Arztes. Es hat sich diese Gemeine auch von Je her aller guten Ordnung des Consistorii widersetzt, weshalb die hiesige Kirch 7 Jahre ohne Prediger gewe- sen. Es ist die aller schlechteste Stelle, wie cs Gott und der ganzen umlicgenden Christenhcit bekannt ist, und nicht móglich, dass cin rechtschaflener Mann, wenn keine Verbesserung geschehet, linger dabey bestehen kann”. ["Carl Wilhelm Miller, born at Reppen in the Neumark, has already stood for years as preacher here in office. But during this short time he has already had to endure countless affronts from the community. Since the church is poor, they let everything go to ruin, repair nothing on it nor on the buildings, fences and stalls belonging to the church, and in many particulars do not keep to the Vocation towards him. It is known that this Piotrowo community has always behaved in this way, and in the space of twenty years has vexed three preachers one after another to death; it goes no better with me, and I have already stood for a whole year under the hands of the physician. This community has likewise from of old resisted all good order of the Consistory, wherefore the church here was seven years without a preacher. It is the very worst post, as is known to God and to the whole Christendom round about, and it is impossible that an honest man can hold out there any longer if no improvement takes place”]
[^119-3]: Von den Haulindern, p. 254.

[p. 120]

a blessing for their neighbourhood[^120-1]. In accordance with the principles of their confessions (the Catholic and the evangelical) the Olęders devoted much care to the cult of the dead. The Catholics buried their dead in the parochial cemeteries, like the rest of the Catholic population. The evangelicals usually had a separate cemetery at each settlement, established on ground received gratuitously from the lord for this purpose. The cemetery ground the Olęders might fence as they pleased[^120-2]. The burial rites were performed, as has already been mentioned, by the teacher.

[^120-1]: Geschichte, p. 401.
[^120-2]: AW. Góździn, A. I: There is permitted to them also a piece of free ground for a churchyard, on which they will be free to bury the bodies of the dead, each according to his custom, and this ground they may enclose with a fence. AW. Kąkolewo, A. 1: „in gleichen vergónne ihre Todten nach hollinderischen gebrauch ehrlich zur Erde bestadten, wo zu ich ihnen cinen freyen Kirchhoff so im ihren Griintzen gebe, welcher auch von Niemanden so]l gekriincket, oder von cinem einigen Gesstlichen turbieret werden”. ["in like manner I grant that their dead may be honourably committed to earth according to the Holländer custom, for which I give them a free churchyard within their boundaries, which shall also be encroached upon by no one, nor molested by any ecclesiastic”] AW. Dziewczastruga, B. 1028: a place for burial is likewise promised to be designated for them.

[p. 121]

Concluding remarks

In the foregoing considerations the political position of the Olęders has not been dealt with, because the question of a separate attitude of the state and of private individuals towards peasants of Polish 1 and German nationality in the old Commonwealth did not exist. National affiliation was, moreover, at that time, in the period of the continual mingling of nationalities and of two-way processes of assimilation, a notion that could not be established even in rough approximation. The pursuit, therefore, of a national policy, even if tendencies in that direction had existed, was a perfectly impossible thing. All attempts, therefore, at writing about the discrimination of German settlers in relation to Poles must be regarded as an undertaking doomed in advance. On the contrary, the gap[^121-1] between the favourable economic situation of the free Olęders and the incomparably worse position of the subject kmiecie continually deepened. The sources contain likewise no mention whatsoever of any steps on the part of the landowners towards the polonisation of the German Olęders subject to them. Probably processes of polonisation of the settlers did exist, but they took place spontaneously, without any purposeful action on the Polish side.

Since they were not szlachta, the Olęders took no part in the political life of their time. Probably to the constitution of the Olęder settlements, and to the resulting absorption solely in the affairs of the community, together with their estrangement from the neighbouring settlements, must be ascribed the fact that the stratum of Olęders, in contrast to the burgher stratum, produced no personality of any outstanding eminence for those times.

In order to grasp what the Olęder colonisation meant for the old Wielkopolska, we must consider it separately from the economic and separately from the political point of view. As an economic phenomenon the settlement must unquestionably be judged

[^121-1]: Of a different opinion was Schmidt (Geschichte, p. 422): „Der Hass Polen der gegen alles, was deutsch und michtig und wuchs sich leiden- protestantisch war, flammite empor zur schaftlichsten Verfolgungssucht aus” ["The Pole's hatred of everything that was German and Protestant flared up into the most unbounded mania of persecution"]

[p. 122]

favourably. On the other hand, as regards the maintenance of the security and integrity of the state, the Olęder colonisation brought no advantage. A considerable number of German settlers on the frontier lands weakened the defensive power of the old Commonwealth in the face of Prussia, created a pretext for the interference of a foreign state in the internal affairs of the country, was even adduced by the Prussian king as one of the causes of the partitions, and finally contributed to a certain degree to the cropping of Poland's borders at the moment of the rebirth of our independence. As for the cultural influence of the Olęders upon the rest of the population, it made no mark because, as has already been emphasised, the culture of the Olęders lacked the fundamental qualities needed for holding a leading position in relation to the native culture.

[p. 123]

ANNEX II — Sig.: AW. Kliny Ol., pow. Poznań-Wschód, CG. 3, Jol. 46 5I. -

Charter for the leaseholders of the Kicin estate complex

Actum feria quarta post Dominicam, ramas Palmarum die scilicet 1l-ma mensis Aprilis Anno 1781. [Done on the fourth day after Palm Sunday, namely on the eleventh day of the month of April.]

The copy of the law of the village of Czerwonak, of the Czerwonak Olęders, of the Olęders on the Kliny and of those dwelling at the Hamer mill, and of the rent-payers at the Mchowo mill, written by the hand of His Worthiness the Reverend Mr Józef de Bończa Miaskowski, Provost of Poznań Cathedral; given in writing to that whole village community and confirmed with his armorial seal in red wax, of which law and of the articles expressed in it the tenor is as follows:

Yielding to the petitions of our faithful subjects paying rent in the Kicin estate complex, and in particular of the leaseholders of the village of Czerwonak, thirteen in number, likewise of the Czerwonak Olęders, eleven in number, as also of the Olęders on the Kliny, ten in number, likewise of the leaseholders at our Hamer mill, three in number, and of those rent-payers settled on our lands at the Miechowo mill, four in number — that to the former, in particular to the village of Czerwonak and to some of the Czerwonak Olęders, laws had been granted by our predecessors, that is: the Reverend Mr Paweł Łodzi Poniński, suffragan and Provost of Poznań Cathedral; likewise His Serene Highness Prince Cardinal Lipski, formerly Provost of Poznań Cathedral and later Bishop of Kraków; the Reverend Mr Wawrzyniec z Wybrarowa Świniarski, Canon of Poznań Cathedral and for that time administrator of the provost's estates; the Reverend Mr Konstanty Moszyński, Bishop of Inflanty [Livonia] and Provost of Poznań Cathedral; the Reverend Mr Józef Tadeusz z Kirska Kierski, Bishop of Boliń and suffragan, Provost of Poznań Cathedral — while to the latter, in particular to the Olęders on the Kliny at the Hamer and at the Miechowo mill, after the expiry of the years of freedom granted by the Reverend Mr Antoni Onufry z Okęcia Okęcki, then Bishop of Poznań and Chancellor of the Crown, formerly Provost of Poznań Cathedral, a share in that same

Polski, 5 10 Prace Komisji Atlasu hist. z.

[p. 146]

law had been bestowed — to all whom it may concern we make known that, having before our eyes the law granted by our predecessors to some of the leaseholders of the Kicin estate complex, we have, in accordance with this, for all the others mentioned above, after measuring out their hubas, granted and written out the law.

First

Establishment of the village office and its duties. Having assembled on the feast of the Three Kings, the communities of the Kicin estate complex, in the village of Czerwonak and at the former sołtys's, are to elect from among the farmers of that village, the most long-standing rent-payers, a sołtys, whose term of office is to last for [printed: from] three years; and if he should prove remiss in anything or wanting in vigilance, and in particular should transgress this present law, and just complaints against him should come from the communities to the manor, then, with the permission received from the manor, they may proceed within the three years to the election of another sołtys. On the same feast the communities are also to elect two ławniks in that village, one from the Czerwonak Olęders, the other from the Kliny Olęders; one of them must at least know Polish and German [printed: 'po niemieckich', recte: 'po niemiecku']; and if within those three years one of these officials dies or sells up, the elder of those remaining is to summon the community at any time to elect another. The duty of these two ławniks will be to come to Czerwonak to the sołtys on every court day and then, together with the sołtys, to hear the complaints that a neighbour may have against another from whichever community, and to punish the guilty party according to the articles of the law written out below. At the same time it will also belong to this office to see to the public roads, the dams and the ditches, to give one another notice in good time of any meadow tax or dues, likewise to collect the taxes from the communities and to pay them over to whom they are due, to take the receipts for what has been paid and to keep them in the chest of the community treasury.

If, however, some new case should call them out beyond those court days — whether by order of the manor or by summons of the sołtys — the ławniks must appear at once and deal with the matter for which they have been summoned. If this village office (into which it should diligently inquire) sees that any of the rent-payers is slothful in the management of his holding or is letting his buildings go to ruin, then, after a triple [warning], it is to assess his huba and his buildings and see to finding a buyer.

Second

Establishment of the community chest.

It is to this end that people join together, so as to be of use to one another; unforeseen events reduce even the mightiest men to nothing and

[p. 147]

to poverty; it is therefore the most just of things that the communities should have their own secure treasury, in which in genuine misfortune they might find help. Accordingly every one of the rent-payers, of all the communities in general, will be bound to pay without fail into the community chest, on every feast of St Wojciech, into the hands of a ławnik or of the sołtys, one grzywna, that is one złoty and fifteen copper groszes; in case of non-payment of that grzywna he will incur the penalty of paying two grzywnas. This chest is to remain with the sołtys under two different locks, for which the ławniks are to have the two keys, but no one may open it without the other and without the sołtys. If, however, one of them should fall ill at the time and not be at home when the chest needs to be opened, the key is then to be entrusted to the senior farmer; and on every feast of St Wojciech, when the communities are called together, this chest is to be counted over, and it is never to be lent to anyone without the summons of at least ten farmers and without an evident [cause]; and even then for no longer than half a year, except in case of fire, for which money may be borrowed from the chest for up to three years, and after those years repaid without interest.

Penalties for offenders

If any insubordination against the authority of the manor, or of the sołtys or of the ławniks, should appear in any of the rent-payers, or he transgresses even the smallest point of this ordinance, then, once this is proven to him, he is to pay three grzywnas into the community chest, that is four and a half złotys. If a neighbour does damage to a neighbour in the field or in the barn, or causes an uproar, a quarrel or plain abuse of the house, or in an inn — God forbid — a beating (this is to be understood as applying to outsiders as well), and the matter is brought before the village court, then the wrong done to the injured party is to be made good as soon as it can be assessed; as in cases of quarrels and abuse, that is three grzywnas for the injured party, and the offender is to give one and a half to the chest. If on a Sunday or on a holy day ordered for public observance in the Kingdom of Poland any of the rent-payers presumes to go to town to sell or to buy, and as a Catholic fails through laziness to be found in his parish church at Kicin, he is to be fined two grzywnas to the community chest. If anyone — God preserve him — is careless with fire in his house, as by carrying a burning splinter about the yard or the buildings, or using a candle without a lantern, or not keeping a chimney or ladders, or failing to sweep out the soot on a Saturday or a Friday, or drying splinters at, and yet not properly on, the household oven, he is to be fined two grzywnas to the chest; and if from such an occasion a fire of any kind should break out, the injured party is to pursue the wrongdoer for damages on his estate. Should God permit an unlucky fire at any of the farmers', then anyone who is lazy about helping to save the property and does not come with water incurs a fine of three grzywnas; 10*

[p. 148]

but whoever comes first to the rescue with water, or in any other unhappy accident comes to the aid of life or limb, is to be paid six grzywnas by the community chest without delay as recompense, and the village court is to see to this most solemnly. None of the rent-payers remaining under this present law in the Kicin estate complex is to contract a debt upon his huba and buildings from anyone without first declaring it in the village court and receiving written permission from the manor; this permission he must in turn lay before the village court, and only then, if the court judges that there is genuine need of it, is it to accept it; and if the court sees that there is no just reason for contracting the debt, or that the borrower is already heavily indebted, the manorial permission is to be carried back to the manor and the reasons for non-acceptance laid before it, which the manor is to accept as just; otherwise the lender will have no claim against the debtor. Every one of the rent-payers is to take care that all his children, or at least one of them, especially the sons, can read, write and count; whoever neglects this is to pay three grzywnas to the chest.

|

Any leaseholder in the Kicin estate complex who intends to sell up must first notify the village court, and it the manor; only after express permission for this has been received, and after the seller has deposited thirty złotys and the buyer the same sum, will he be free to leave his holding — but not before the seller and the buyer alike have each paid three Polish złotys into the community chest.

The dues of the leaseholders of the Kicin estate complex.

Every leaseholder (except those who may hold separate charters) of the Kicin estate complex is to pay into the lord's treasury on every feast of St Martin fifty Polish złotys, 50 zł, counting thirty copper groszes to the złoty, and two wierts of oats; the wiertel is to hold sixteen garnce of the new measure. From the other lordly dues — hens, sheep, spinning quotas, nuts, mushrooms, watch duty — they are freed for ever. Excepted from this common point applying to the other leaseholders are the three leaseholders at the Hamer, who are to pay rent, without the other lordly dues and even without oats, sixty-six Polish złotys. Every one of the leaseholders of the Kicin estate complex, unless he sits under a separate charter, is to go out ploughing three times a year, that is for the root crops, for the fallow and for the winter sowing, and on each occasion he is to plough eight rods, i.e. 8; the rod is to hold seven and a half ells of the new measure. They are also to do nine days' hand labour, that is three in the garden, three with the sickle and three with the scythe. From all the other labour dues, such as the carting of manure from the provost's curia and the carting of wood there, they are freed; in return they are to receive each year six sądki [small casks] of beer of the new measure — three at Czerwonak with their Olęders, two at Kliny, one at the Hamer and the Janikowo Olęders.

[p. 149]

The carting of grain, over a distance of at most six miles, they are to perform once a year; provided, however, that no more than four korcy of the new measure, that is eight wierts, may ever be loaded on to one waggon. If the manor has no need of this, then instead of the cartage over six miles he is to do a day's ploughing and break up and plough eight rods. Each of the leaseholders is to perform one road cartage of at most six miles, and if this is not needed, he is to go out ploughing for one day, eight rods. The Czerwonak Olęders are freed from the grain cartage and from the rod-ploughing of the cartage duty; in return they are to go out ploughing two days a year, eight rods each. For the brewing of beer at the Kicin brewhouse they are to take turns, prepare and cart the wood for it, and carry out the malt and bring it back. All manner of help in building — that is, in bringing timber, stone, brick, lime, clay and sand, in raising buildings and in daubing — they owe in turn. Church dues, as well as taxes and instalments of whatever kind, now or in future, each is to hand over to the sołtys, and he is to deliver them where they are due.

They are to keep the ditches (so that the water has its fall), the dams by the mills and the public roads in the best possible condition and repair them when called upon to do so by the sołtys. As to the laying waste of the Kicin forests, they shall not only not cut there themselves (apart from on their own morgs), but the seizure of what they detect being felled shall be their duty.

So that everyone may know and understand all this, when the community has assembled twice a year at the village court, that is on St Martin's day and on St Wojciech's day, at the sołtys's at Czerwonak, it is to be read out aloud and freely.

Since it is the honour of our leaseholders of the Kicin estate complex that is at stake, the last point concerns theft, namely that if there should appear the least sign not only of actual theft but of complicity with a thief, such a person, as an enemy of the common good, is to be expelled without delay; his estate having been sold, the injured party is to be compensated as the case shows, ten grzywnas are to be paid into the community chest, and the remainder given to the culprit.

So that these our present provisions may carry the greater weight, we append them with our own hand together with the impression of the seal. Given at Kicin on the second day of February of the year one thousand seven hundred and eighty-one.

Józef Bończa Miaskowski, Provost of Poznań Cathedral, mpp.

I certify the corrections made above, so that they may not fall under any suspicion that they were made by my own hand. Datum ut supra [as above].

Miaskowski Kat. Poznański mpp.

[p. 150]

The foregoing extract from the Chwaliszewo burgher books has been written out and collated with those books, that is with the enrolment, and found to agree in every respect; the Contentious Department of the Court of Peace of the City of Poznań, which has oversight of the archive, hereby certifies this and signs in its own hand with the seal of the court affixed, with the added remark that the crossing-out of two lines, written by mistake from another case, was done by the court itself.

Poznań, on the 26th of the month, in the year 1810. (signed) Wincenty Mierzyński, Podsędek of Poznań (L. S.) [locus sigilli]. Certified as authentic by signature:

M. Kamiński P. D. i L. M. O. B. Certified as agreeing with the original (signed) Betkowski

[p. 151]

ANNEX III

Wilkierz for the Kotusz Olęders. Sig.: Kotusz A. 1 (spelling corrected).

[fol. 5] The laws of Kotusz, from the lords.

Given [1] and confirmed to the Olęders. I give notice and make known to each and every one, and to whomever it is needful to know this: whereas I have sold my hereditary lands named Kotusz to good honest people, and to their children and descendants, for a sum, and have set them the said lands at a fixed yearly rent, yet these inhabitants live without order and without obedience to the laws; therefore, so that in the times to come there may be among them order, justice, peace, concord and obedience, I have judged it well to give and appoint to them the law and articles, fines and penalties described below, which both parties alike are bound to keep and observe as strictly as possible on pain of their own penalty. For the sake of better order the neighbours shall come together and elect for themselves a sołtys and ławniks for the year, and whomsoever they choose I shall always confirm. They are to be such persons from the community as will look after the good of the whole village and of their common property; they are also to judge and settle whatever disputes may arise, according to this law and my direction, strictly to uphold it, to collect and take the fines from anyone who offends against this law, and to render an account of them to the neighbours. I order that the said fines be paid into the official chest, which chest is to be kept at the sołtys's. The ławniks are to have a key to this chest. They are not to spend the fine money on gatherings of any kind, but are to apply the fine money to the good order and benefit of the village, as for instance the dams, the ditches, the causeways and whatever may be useful to the community; this is to be done with the knowledge of the neighbours and of the whole community. The laws and articles set out below are to be read to the whole community twice each year, so that no one can excuse himself out of ignorance when he offends against the law. These are the articles:

1. Whenever the sołtys commands or forbids anything, they are to obey.

If anyone, however, stays at home and does not carry out the obedience owed to his sołtys, he is to be fined five groszes.

[p. 152]

2. If a neighbour addresses the sołtys or his ławniks in discourteous or abusive words, or threatens them with a weapon, and when he comes to court refuses to listen, he is to be fined two good grzywnas without any [exception]. If he then behaves worse still, or threatens blows, he is to be sentenced by their judgement to a heavier penalty.

3. But if anyone boldly and violently attacks the sołtys or his ławniks, and having given rein to his anger [fol. 6] refuses to obey, or will not submit, then whoever beats or wounds him in that event, by day or by night, goes free and incurs no penalty, nor is any court needed.

4. Whoever is sent by the sołtys on official business, and anyone presumes to abuse or beat him, shall be fined two good grzywnas.

5. When the sołtys holds court with the ławniks, no woman is to be present, unless she herself has some complaint of her own, or her husband is not at home. Otherwise a woman who offends in this is to pay a fine of five groszes.

6. I permit that each year they are to pay the sołtys thirty groszes from every huba for his trouble and toil, and his ławniks fifteen groszes each year. Whoever opposes this is to be punished in duplo [twice over].

7. If the sołtys and the ławniks make any journey on the community's business and for the good of their estate, the costs are to be taken from the hubas according to the account.

8. The sołtys and the ławniks are to watch carefully over the good of the whole village and community, to take care of the fences, dams, ditches and sluices, and wherever harm might come to the community, to block, repair and put it right. If the sołtys and the ławniks are negligent in this, they are to be punished at the discretion of the whole community.

9. When there is any purchase of grain or of cattle, and a sealing drink (litkup) has already been drunk at the purchase, the party who does not keep the bargain is to give a barrel of beer as a penalty.

10. God forbid that a building should catch fire and burn down through a thunderbolt or through the doing of wicked men; the neighbours are then to help him out of Christian charity — each huba contributing, at 100 grzywnas, the 15th grosz, according to the assessment of the damage — and they are to help him with timber, reed and carting.

11. God forbid that a fire from carelessness should break out in the village, in one of the houses: any neighbour who will not come to the rescue and to the extinguishing is to be [fined] three good grzywnas.

12. Whoever at a fire takes someone else's axe, hatchet, weborek [?], hoe or any other utensil belonging to another, is to bring those things to the sołtys and deposit them with him, so that they may be returned to their owner, on pain of a fine of one good grzywna.

[p. 153]

13. No one is to presume to go and challenge another before his porch or his door. Whoever does this incurs a fine of three good grzywnas.

14. If anyone presumes to waylay another on the road, in the field or in the village, and this can be proved by two reputable witnesses, he is to be punished according to the assessment of the seriousness of the matter. [fol. 7]

15. If one neighbour reviles another, or women abuse one another with unseemly words, to the damage of their honour, and there are two steady witnesses, they are to be fined two good grzywnas; the unseemly words [are to] be proved, or honour made good, at the discretion of the court.

16. One neighbour is to help another with manure and water in the building and fitting out of his farmyard, when this is needed. Whoever will not do this is liable to a fine of one good grzywna.

17. If a neighbour opens an enclosure or a fence and does not close it again, so that any damage results, he is to make good that damage and pay a fine of one grzywna.

18. If anyone drives across another's sown field, he is to bear a fine of one grzywna and make good the damage.

19. If the sołtys orders the pastures to be cleaned, the boundaries to be finished, the outflow of the waters to be cleaned and sluiced, and whatever ditches may be useful to the village, whoever does not obey this order and does not do his part is liable to a fine of two grzywnas and must complete the said work within a week, on pain of a double penalty.

20. One neighbour is to keep and maintain the boundaries of another, whether ditches or fences, as the place requires. Whoever does not do this, if damage results, is to make good and pay for the damage; and whatever complaint there may be, ten groszes always fall to the sołtys.

21. The proper boundary is to be as follows: a ditch is to be half a rod or a laska wide, and two ells deep. A fence or enclosure is to be two ells high, so that a half-year-old calf cannot climb over it. Moreover, if there is in the village a horse or horned cattle that spoil and jump the fences, this is to be prevented, or such cattle disposed of. There are to be thirty rządek or rods of farmyard, with the dwelling, the garden and the stables for the cattle, pigs, hens, geese and ducks, well provided.

22. If anyone's horse, ox or cow is stolen, the neighbours are to take pains that the theft is disclosed and found out.

[p. 154]

23. No one is to presume to refuse and take away labourers or hired men who have been engaged, without the consent of the man for whom they work, unless they are already released after payment. Whoever presumes to do this will be liable to a fine of one good grzywna. 4fol. 8]

24. Whoever presumes to refuse a neighbour a farmhand or a maidservant, or to buy them off, shall be fined one grzywna, and is to send the farmhand or the maid back to serve their former master.

25. If anyone impounds a neighbour's cattle to its harm, he is to water and guard it, so that it does not suffer or die. Whoever is the cause of this is to bear the damage.

26. When anyone's cattle is impounded, the sołtys has notice given to the owner of the cattle; when he leaves his cattle in the pound, he is to pay for the first night five groszes a head, for the second night ten groszes a head, and so on up to the fifth night, always doubling. If after the fifth night the cattle is not collected and redeemed, it is to be driven to the lord's farmyard.

27. No one is to presume to drive a neighbour's cattle into his own house; it is to be handed over at once to the sołtys or a ławnik, on pain of a fine of one grzywna.

28. The sołtys is entitled to a third part of the redemption money for impounded cattle.

Whoever has no money to redeem the cattle is to find those who will stand surety for him, and leave a twofold pledge, to the sołtys's satisfaction.

29. If anyone, when his cattle is impounded and driven to the sołtys, opposes this and wants to take the cattle back by force, he is to be fined two good grzywnas.

30. If anyone beats impounded cattle or throws things at it, so that the animal suffers harm by it, he is to make good the damage and is to be fined three good grzywnas.

31. No one is to milk impounded cattle, or make use of impounded horses, or ride on them. Whoever contravenes this is to be fined one grzywna.

32. When someone finds another's cattle in his corn or in his meadow, he is to have the ławniks inspect and assess the damage; if he will not have this done, he is to be content with the customary redemption money, namely a grosz a head of cattle. For the inspection he is to pay the ławniks twelve imperial groszes, which they are to take on every occasion.

33. If the sołtys orders the pigs to be hobbled (klockować), and anyone neglects this, so that damage results to a neighbour, he is to take [recte: give] 5 groszes a pig for the impounding.

[p. 155]

[fol. 9] 34. Geese and ducks, when they stray in and do damage to a neighbour, may be killed and sent back home to their owner.

35. If anyone during a court session hisses at another in discourteous words or plays a trick on him, he is to pay a fine of five groszes; if he threatens blows, he is to go into obedience and pay 10 groszes. And he is not to be released from obedience any sooner until he has come to an agreement with his neighbour and apologised.

36. The wiertel, the quarter and the beer measure are to be measured out fairly according to the measures of the neighbouring towns, so that no one suffers wrong. Whoever acts against this and cheats his neighbour is to be punished at the discretion of the sołtys and the ławniks.

37. No one is to presume — and this I do not allow — to take a smallholder (zagrodnik) or a cottager, that is a lodger, into his own house, or settle him on his land, without the knowledge of the whole neighbourhood, on pain of a barrel of beer.

38. If there is need, for the better good of the village, to make a dam with a sluice, or ditches for draining off the waters, or to provide the fields with dams, the neighbours are to contribute to it according to their hubas; I on my side promise to bring in the neighbouring villages to help. Whoever opposes this is to be punished at the discretion of the sołtys and the ławniks.

39. The sołtys and the ławniks are obliged every two weeks, on a Tuesday, to hold court for the neighbours, hear complaints, settle disputes and collect the fines as diligently as possible; only, one party is to give notice and instruction to the other a day before the court sitting; and for an outsider justice is to be done at any time, on payment of the official fee.

40. Before the court sitting the complainant is to deposit twelve imperial groszes, of which four imperial groszes belong to the sołtys.

41. And this I reserve to myself: that the sołtys is to keep good watch that criminal cases and fines belonging to the lord's court are not concealed. These are always to be brought before me, and such matters must be reported to me. As for the other cases, I entrust them to the sołtys for the better judgement of the village; for wounding and beating [with] marks, eight imperial groszes are always to be paid to the sołtys, besides the amount due to his ławniks.

42. If someone complains before the sołtys and his ławniks and wishes to take his case to the lord, he must be allowed to do so, and this is [fol. 10] ordered; but if anyone wants to appeal presumptuously and insolently, he is to be fined one grzywna.

43. If one neighbour wishes to sell another an estate or property of any kind, he must first notify the sołtys

[p. 156]

and not sell it secretly, so that no one leaves debts behind him. Whether it is

under the lord or under the community, the buyer is always to give the neighbours a barrel of beer when he buys. And his own kinsmen, who are always the nearest to the purchase, are also to give the neighbours a barrel of beer. — 44. If anyone wishes to buy another person's huba or anyone's estate, he must first give due notice at the sołtys's, with a barrel of beer; if there is a neighbour who can buy that huba, and such a person is found [in] the village, he is to have precedence in the purchase of this huba and be admitted to the purchase; but the neighbour who borders on the huba being sold has the nearer right of purchase. If there are kinsmen from that huba, they are the nearest to receive the huba being sold, and they are not to be barred from it in any way. Whoever opposes this is to pay to the lord a fine of ten good grzywnas, and to the neighbours, for this presumption, a barrel of beer by way of penalty.

I therefore earnestly command the sołtys and the ławniks to hold firmly to the said law and its penalties and to keep them, on pain of severe penalty from the manor. This statute and constitution is to be kept and carried out as diligently as possible; I permit no one to act against these laws. For surer assurance and confirmation I seal with my own natural seal knowingly and sign. Given in my hereditary estate of Żerków.

[The date, seal and signature are lacking. The founding charter was granted to the Olęders in 1729. The issue of the present wilkierz probably followed in the years nearest to it.]

[p. 157]
